# Jesus in Isaiah — A Chapter-by-Chapter Commentary

**The Hope Connection**  
**Lewis Fuller**

*Meeting others where they are in the Name of Jesus*

*"He was pierced for our transgressions; he was crushed for our iniquities; upon him was the chastisement that brought us peace, and with his wounds we are healed."* — Isaiah 53:5 (ESV)

---

## Introduction — Isaiah's Gospel at a Glance

If the Psalms are the prayer book of the Old Testament, Isaiah is its gospel. The early church knew it. Jerome called Isaiah not just a prophet but an evangelist, because no other Old Testament writer sets Christ before us so fully, so often, and so clearly. Isaiah saw the virgin's Son and named Him Immanuel. He saw the child born who would be called Wonderful Counselor, Mighty God, Everlasting Father, Prince of Peace. He saw the Servant of the LORD despised and rejected, pierced and crushed, silent before His accusers, buried in a rich man's tomb, and then — impossibly — prolonging His days and seeing the fruit of His suffering. When you read Isaiah, you are reading the gospel of Jesus Christ written seven hundred years before Bethlehem.

**The most quoted prophet.** The New Testament quotes Isaiah more than any other prophet. By common count there are more than sixty direct quotations and hundreds of allusions. John the Baptist introduces himself with Isaiah's words: "I am the voice of one crying out in the wilderness" (John 1:23; Isaiah 40:3). Jesus opens His public ministry by unrolling the scroll of Isaiah in the synagogue at Nazareth, reading Isaiah 61 — "The Spirit of the Lord is upon me, because he has anointed me to proclaim good news to the poor" — and then sitting down and saying, "Today this Scripture has been fulfilled in your hearing" (Luke 4:17–21). Paul reaches for Isaiah again and again in Romans, the great letter of justification by faith. And the Ethiopian eunuch in Acts 8 is reading Isaiah 53 in his chariot when Philip runs up beside him, and "beginning with this Scripture he told him the good news about Jesus" (Acts 8:35). The New Testament does not treat Isaiah as background reading. It treats Isaiah as a witness — the chief witness — to Jesus.

**Isaiah saw His glory.** John says it plainly. After quoting Isaiah 53:1 and Isaiah 6:10 side by side, John writes: "Isaiah said these things because he saw his glory and spoke of him" (John 12:41). The "him" is Jesus. Which means that when Isaiah stood in the temple in chapter 6 and saw the Lord seated on a throne, high and lifted up, with the train of His robe filling the temple — he saw the glory of Christ. The whole book flows from that vision. The prophet who saw the King in His glory could never again be satisfied with religion that had the form of godliness without its power, and he could never stop pointing forward to the day when the King would come not only in glory but in humility, as a child, as a servant, as a lamb.

**Who wrote it, and when.** Isaiah the son of Amoz prophesied in Judah during the reigns of Uzziah, Jotham, Ahaz, and Hezekiah — roughly from 740 to 680 BC. He was a prophet of Jerusalem, likely of noble birth, with access to kings. He outlived empires' threats: he watched the northern kingdom of Israel fall to Assyria in 722 BC, stood with Hezekiah when Sennacherib's army surrounded Jerusalem, and lived to warn of a Babylon that had not yet risen. Tradition says he was martyred under Manasseh. He wrote to a people who were religious on the outside and rotten on the inside — a people with sacrifices on their lips and injustice on their hands — and he wrote, at the same time, the most comfort-laden chapters in all of Scripture. That combination is exactly why this book speaks so powerfully to people in recovery and people rebuilding a life: Isaiah never softens the diagnosis, and he never withholds the cure.

**Two great movements.** The book falls into two great movements, with a hinge between them. Chapters 1–39 are dominated by judgment: the indictment of Judah, the oracles against the nations, the folly of trusting in Egypt and in idols, and the shadow of exile. Chapters 40–66 are dominated by comfort: "Comfort, comfort my people, says your God." The hinge is chapters 36–39 — the historical bridge where Hezekiah's story ends, Jerusalem is delivered from Assyria, and yet the prophet announces that Babylon will one day carry everything away. Judgment is real, and it is coming; but it is not the last word. Running under both movements like a river is the theme of the Holy One of Israel — Isaiah's favorite name for God, used more than twenty-five times in this book and rarely anywhere else — who is both the Judge who will not overlook sin and the Redeemer who will not abandon His people.

**The Servant Songs.** Four passages in the second movement stand out like mountain peaks, and the church has called them the Servant Songs: Isaiah 42:1–9, the Servant chosen and upheld, who brings justice to the nations and does not break a bruised reed; Isaiah 49:1–13, the Servant called from the womb, a light to the nations, whose work looks wasted and is not; Isaiah 50:4–11, the Servant with the taught tongue, who gives His back to those who strike Him and sets His face like flint; and Isaiah 52:13–53:12, the Servant exalted precisely through suffering — despised, rejected, pierced, crushed, and yet bearing the sin of many and making intercession for transgressors. Read those four songs slowly. They are the heart of this book, and they are a portrait of Jesus painted seven centuries before He was born.

**Read in the Reformation way.** This commentary reads Isaiah the way the Reformers taught the church to read every book of the Bible, and the five watchwords run through every chapter that follows. *Sola scriptura* — Scripture alone is our rule; Isaiah himself commands, "To the teaching and to the testimony!" (Isaiah 8:20). *Sola gratia* — grace alone saves; Isaiah's people are offered pardon while their sins are like scarlet, with nothing to pay: "Come, buy and eat... without money and without price" (Isaiah 55:1). *Sola fide* — faith alone receives it; Isaiah's word to a frightened king is the Reformers' word too: "If you are not firm in faith, you will not be firm at all" (Isaiah 7:9). *Solus Christus* — Christ alone; the Servant bears our sins, and no one else can. *Soli Deo gloria* — glory to God alone; the seraphim's cry, "Holy, holy, holy," fills the whole earth with His glory, not ours. Calvin wrote a full commentary on Isaiah and called the prophet's teaching a treasure; Luther loved this book and lectured on it; Spurgeon preached Isaiah constantly and called chapter 53 the Bible in miniature. And in every chapter the law and the gospel are kept distinct: the law shows us our sin (the bad news), and the gospel shows us Christ (the good news). Isaiah himself preaches that way — indictment first, then "Comfort, comfort my people." Keep the two straight, and this book will do for you what it did for the Ethiopian in his chariot: it will show you Jesus.

**How to use this commentary.** Each chapter section begins with "Jesus in this chapter" — a short look at how Christ is revealed there. Then the commentary walks the chapter's movements in order, explaining the text plainly. Key verses are quoted in full from the English Standard Version. Each chapter closes with "The bad news and the good news," a note from the Reformers where they genuinely shed light, and one reflection question to carry into prayer. Read it slowly, with your Bible open, and remember: the Christ of Isaiah is not a symbol or an idea. He is the living Lord who saw you before you saw Him — Isaiah saw His glory, and by grace, so can you.

---

## How to Use This Commentary

This commentary was built to be used, not just read. Here is how to get the most out of it.

**The order of every session.** Work through each chapter the same way, in this order:

1. **Read the chapter in your own Bible first.** Open your Bible to the chapter of Isaiah and read it all the way through before you read a word of commentary. The commentary serves the Scripture; it never replaces it. Isaiah wrote poetry meant to be heard, so read it out loud if you can — the comfort of chapter 40 and the suffering of chapter 53 land differently in the ears than on the eyes.
2. **Then read the commentary section for that chapter.** Keep your Bible open beside it and look up the verses as they come. Start with "Jesus in this chapter" — before anything else, fix your eyes on how Christ is revealed there. John said Isaiah saw His glory and spoke of Him; our job in every chapter is to look until we see Him too.
3. **Then sit with the reflection question.** Each chapter ends with one reflection question and a "bad news and good news" paragraph. Do not rush past them. Read the bad news honestly — let Isaiah's indictments search you the way they searched Judah — and then read the good news slowly. Answer the reflection question in prayer, out loud to God, not just in your head.
4. **Write in a notebook.** Keep a simple notebook with this study. Write down your answer to the reflection question, your answer to the study guide question for that chapter (see the Isaiah Study Guide below), and the memory verse for the chapter. A truth is not really yours until you have written it, prayed it, and lived it. A dollar notebook and a pen will carry you through all sixty-six chapters.

**The rhythm: three sessions a week.** This fits the study rhythm used across The Hope Connection: three sessions a week, one book at a time, until the book is done. At one chapter per session, three sessions a week, you will walk through all sixty-six chapters of Isaiah in about twenty-two weeks — roughly five months. That sounds long, and it is worth saying plainly: Isaiah is a long book, and it rewards a long, steady walk. If you want to go slower, spread one chapter across the whole week: session one, read the chapter in your Bible and the "Jesus in this chapter" opening; session two, read the commentary; session three, return to the chapter, work through the questions, and pray it back to God. Either pace is fine. What matters is the steadiness, not the speed.

**No catch-up piles.** This rule is not negotiable, because it protects the whole habit: if you miss a day, you do not owe anybody two chapters. You simply pick up at the next chapter at the next session. Life happens — sickness, family, work, hard weeks. A study rhythm that piles up guilt every time life interrupts will not survive a month. This one bends and keeps going. Missed a week? Start again at the next chapter. The Lord is not keeping a tally, and neither are we.

**Using it alone.** Everything above is written for one reader, one Bible, one notebook, and the Lord. Pray before you open the book — a short, real prayer: "Lord, open my eyes, and let this Word search me." Pray again when you finish, turning the chapter's good news into thanksgiving and its bad news into confession.

**Using it with a spouse or a friend.** Read the chapter out loud together at the start of the session — take turns reading. Then read the commentary, or take turns reading it aloud too. Talk through the reflection question and the study guide question together, honestly. Pray for each other out of what the chapter showed you. Isaiah is full of pairs and companies — the remnant, the watchmen, the ransomed returning together — and two people studying it together will see things one reader alone will miss.

**Using it in a small group.** One chapter per meeting. Have someone read the chapter aloud from the Bible to open. Walk through the commentary section together, stopping wherever the text raises a question. Save real time at the end for the reflection question and the study guide question — that is where study becomes life. Close in prayer, and let the chapter's memory verse be the group's verse for the week: read it together at the start of the next meeting and see who can say it from memory. Keep the group small enough that everybody talks, and keep the door open — Isaiah is good news for people who have never opened a Bible, and every chapter here is written plain enough for a brand-new believer to follow. Chapters 40–66 especially are medicine for hurting people; do not be surprised if the group wants to linger there.

**The memory verses.** The study guide below gives one memory verse for each chapter — sixty-six verses, the spine of Isaiah's gospel. If you take one verse a week alongside the chapter rhythm, by the time you finish the book you will carry its whole story in your heart: scarlet sins made white as snow, a child born who is Mighty God, a bruised reed He will not break, a Servant pierced for our transgressions, and a highway home for the ransomed. That is treasure no one can take from you.

---
## Isaiah Study Guide

Sixty-six chapters, sixty-six sessions (or sixty-six weeks at the slower pace). For each chapter: the theme, the key verse to watch for, one memory verse to hide in your heart, and one study question to work through in your notebook after the commentary's reflection question.

| Chapter | Theme | Key Verse | Memory Verse (ESV) |
|---|---|---|---|
| Isaiah 1 | Scarlet Made White | Isaiah 1:18 — scarlet sins white as snow | "Though your sins are like scarlet, they shall be as white as snow; though they are red like crimson, they shall become like wool." — Isaiah 1:18 |
| Isaiah 2 | The Mountain of the LORD | Isaiah 2:3 — let us go up to the mountain | "O house of Jacob, come, let us walk in the light of the LORD." — Isaiah 2:5 |
| Isaiah 3 | When the Props Are Kicked Away | Isaiah 3:1 — support and supply removed | "For Jerusalem has stumbled, and Judah has fallen, because their speech and their deeds are against the LORD." — Isaiah 3:8 |
| Isaiah 4 | The Branch of the LORD | Isaiah 4:2 — the Branch beautiful and glorious | "In that day the branch of the LORD shall be beautiful and glorious." — Isaiah 4:2 |
| Isaiah 5 | The Song of the Vineyard | Isaiah 5:4 — what more was there to do? | "Woe to those who call evil good and good evil, who put darkness for light and light for darkness." — Isaiah 5:20 |
| Isaiah 6 | Holy, Holy, Holy | Isaiah 6:3 — the seraphim's cry | "Holy, holy, holy is the LORD of hosts; the whole earth is full of his glory!" — Isaiah 6:3 |
| Isaiah 7 | Immanuel | Isaiah 7:14 — the virgin shall conceive | "Behold, the virgin shall conceive and bear a son, and shall call his name Immanuel." — Isaiah 7:14 |
| Isaiah 8 | Fear God, Not the Conspiracy | Isaiah 8:13 — let Him be your fear | "To the teaching and to the testimony! If they will not speak according to this word, it is because they have no dawn." — Isaiah 8:20 |
| Isaiah 9 | Unto Us a Child Is Born | Isaiah 9:6 — the Child's names | "For to us a child is born, to us a son is given; and the government shall be upon his shoulder." — Isaiah 9:6 |
| Isaiah 10 | The Rod and the Remnant | Isaiah 10:21 — a remnant will return | "A remnant will return, the remnant of Jacob, to the mighty God." — Isaiah 10:21 |
| Isaiah 11 | The Shoot from the Stump | Isaiah 11:1 — a shoot from Jesse's stump | "There shall come forth a shoot from the stump of Jesse, and a branch from his roots shall bear fruit." — Isaiah 11:1 |
| Isaiah 12 | With Joy You Will Draw Water | Isaiah 12:2 — God is my salvation | "Behold, God is my salvation; I will trust, and will not be afraid." — Isaiah 12:2 |
| Isaiah 13 | The Day of the LORD Against Babylon | Isaiah 13:11 — the pomp of the arrogant laid low | "I will put an end to the pomp of the arrogant, and lay low the pompous pride of the ruthless." — Isaiah 13:11 |
| Isaiah 14 | How the Oppressor Has Ceased | Isaiah 14:32 — the LORD has founded Zion | "The LORD has founded Zion, and in her the afflicted of his people find refuge." — Isaiah 14:32 |
| Isaiah 15 | The Weeping over Moab | Isaiah 15:5 — My heart cries out for Moab | "My heart cries out for Moab." — Isaiah 15:5 |
| Isaiah 16 | Send the Lamb | Isaiah 16:5 — a throne established in steadfast love | "Then a throne will be established in steadfast love." — Isaiah 16:5 |
| Isaiah 17 | Damascus Falls | Isaiah 17:7 — man will look to his Maker | "In that day man will look to his Maker, and his eyes will look on the Holy One of Israel." — Isaiah 17:7 |
| Isaiah 18 | A Quiet God | Isaiah 18:4 — I will keep still and I will look | "I will keep still and I will look from my dwelling place." — Isaiah 18:4 |
| Isaiah 19 | The Enemy Blessed | Isaiah 19:25 — Blessed be Egypt my people | "Blessed be Egypt my people, and Assyria the work of my hands, and Israel my inheritance." — Isaiah 19:25 |
| Isaiah 20 | The Prophet Walks Barefoot | Isaiah 20:6 — what happened to those in whom we hoped | "As my servant Isaiah has walked naked and barefoot for three years as a sign and a portent." — Isaiah 20:3 |
| Isaiah 21 | Watchman, What of the Night? | Isaiah 21:12 — morning comes, and also the night | "Morning comes, and also the night. If you will inquire, inquire; come back again." — Isaiah 21:12 |
| Isaiah 22 | The Key of David | Isaiah 22:22 — He shall open, and none shall shut | "And I will place on his shoulder the key of the house of David. He shall open, and none shall shut." — Isaiah 22:22 |
| Isaiah 23 | Tyre Brought Low | Isaiah 23:9 — the LORD of hosts has purposed it | "Her merchandise and her wages will be holy to the LORD." — Isaiah 23:18 |
| Isaiah 24 | The Earth Is Laid Waste | Isaiah 24:23 — the LORD of hosts will reign | "The LORD of hosts will reign on Mount Zion and in Jerusalem, and before his elders he will be glorified." — Isaiah 24:23 |
| Isaiah 25 | The Feast on the Mountain | Isaiah 25:8 — death swallowed up forever | "He will swallow up death forever; and the Lord GOD will wipe away tears from all faces." — Isaiah 25:8 |
| Isaiah 26 | Perfect Peace | Isaiah 26:3 — kept in perfect peace | "You keep him in perfect peace whose mind is stayed on you, because he trusts in you." — Isaiah 26:3 |
| Isaiah 27 | The Vineyard Kept | Isaiah 27:3 — I keep it night and day | "I, the LORD, am its keeper; every moment I water it." — Isaiah 27:3 |
| Isaiah 28 | The Cornerstone in Zion | Isaiah 28:16 — a tested stone, a precious cornerstone | "Whoever believes will not be in haste." — Isaiah 28:16 |
| Isaiah 29 | The Book Unsealed | Isaiah 29:18 — the deaf shall hear the words of a book | "The meek shall obtain fresh joy in the LORD, and the poor among mankind shall exult in the Holy One of Israel." — Isaiah 29:19 |
| Isaiah 30 | The God Who Waits to Be Gracious | Isaiah 30:15 — in returning and rest you shall be saved | "In returning and rest you shall be saved; in quietness and in trust shall be your strength." — Isaiah 30:15 |
| Isaiah 31 | Woe to Those Who Go Down to Egypt | Isaiah 31:3 — the Egyptians are man, and not God | "Like birds hovering, so the LORD of hosts will protect Jerusalem." — Isaiah 31:5 |
| Isaiah 32 | A King Will Reign in Righteousness | Isaiah 32:17 — the effect of righteousness will be peace | "And the effect of righteousness will be peace, and the result of righteousness, quietness and trust forever." — Isaiah 32:17 |
| Isaiah 33 | The LORD Is Our King | Isaiah 33:22 — judge, lawgiver, king, Savior | "For the LORD is our judge; the LORD is our lawgiver; the LORD is our king; he will save us." — Isaiah 33:22 |
| Isaiah 34 | Judgment on Edom | Isaiah 34:16 — seek and read from the book of the LORD | "For the LORD has a day of vengeance, a year of recompense for the cause of Zion." — Isaiah 34:8 |
| Isaiah 35 | The Highway of Holiness | Isaiah 35:8 — the Way of Holiness | "And the ransomed of the LORD shall return and come to Zion with singing; everlasting joy shall be upon their heads." — Isaiah 35:10 |
| Isaiah 36 | The Rabshakeh at the Wall | Isaiah 36:5 — on what do you rest this trust? | "Do not let Hezekiah make you trust in the LORD by saying, 'The LORD will surely deliver us.'" — Isaiah 36:18 (the enemy's line — answered in chapter 37) |
| Isaiah 37 | The Letter Spread Before the LORD | Isaiah 37:35 — I will defend this city | "Save us from his hand, that all the kingdoms of the earth may know that you alone are the LORD." — Isaiah 37:20 |
| Isaiah 38 | Fifteen Years Added | Isaiah 38:5 — I have heard your prayer, seen your tears | "I have heard your prayer; I have seen your tears. Behold, I will add fifteen years to your life." — Isaiah 38:5 |
| Isaiah 39 | Hezekiah Shows Everything | Isaiah 39:6 — all shall be carried to Babylon | "The word of the LORD that you have spoken is good." — Isaiah 39:8 (a warning in the king's own mouth: good news for me is not enough) |
| Isaiah 40 | Comfort, Comfort My People | Isaiah 40:1 — her iniquity is pardoned | "But they who wait for the LORD shall renew their strength; they shall mount up with wings like eagles." — Isaiah 40:31 |
| Isaiah 41 | Fear Not, I Am With You | Isaiah 41:10 — I will strengthen, help, uphold | "Fear not, for I am with you; be not dismayed, for I am your God." — Isaiah 41:10 |
| Isaiah 42 | The Bruised Reed | Isaiah 42:3 — He will not break the bruised reed | "A bruised reed he will not break, and a faintly burning wick he will not quench." — Isaiah 42:3 |
| Isaiah 43 | Called by Name | Isaiah 43:1 — I have redeemed you, you are mine | "I, I am he who blots out your transgressions for my own sake, and I will not remember your sins." — Isaiah 43:25 |
| Isaiah 44 | Water on the Thirsty Land | Isaiah 44:22 — return to Me, for I have redeemed you | "I have blotted out your transgressions like a cloud and your sins like mist; return to me, for I have redeemed you." — Isaiah 44:22 |
| Isaiah 45 | Turn to Me and Be Saved | Isaiah 45:22 — all the ends of the earth | "Turn to me and be saved, all the ends of the earth! For I am God, and there is no other." — Isaiah 45:22 |
| Isaiah 46 | Carried from the Womb | Isaiah 46:4 — to gray hairs I will carry you | "Even to your old age I am he, and to gray hairs I will carry you. I have made, and I will bear; I will carry and will save." — Isaiah 46:4 |
| Isaiah 47 | Babylon Sits in the Dust | Isaiah 47:4 — Our Redeemer, the LORD of hosts | "Our Redeemer — the LORD of hosts is his name — is the Holy One of Israel." — Isaiah 47:4 |
| Isaiah 48 | For My Name's Sake | Isaiah 48:18 — your peace like a river | "Oh that you had paid attention to my commandments! Then your peace would have been like a river." — Isaiah 48:18 |
| Isaiah 49 | Engraved on His Palms | Isaiah 49:16 — I will not forget you | "Behold, I have engraved you on the palms of my hands; your walls are continually before me." — Isaiah 49:16 |
| Isaiah 50 | The Taught Tongue | Isaiah 50:4 — to sustain the weary with a word | "I have set my face like a flint, and I know that I shall not be put to shame." — Isaiah 50:7 |
| Isaiah 51 | The Rock You Were Hewn From | Isaiah 51:1 — look to the rock | "I, I am he who comforts you." — Isaiah 51:12 |
| Isaiah 52 | Beautiful Feet | Isaiah 52:7 — Your God reigns | "How beautiful upon the mountains are the feet of him who brings good news, who publishes peace." — Isaiah 52:7 |
| Isaiah 53 | The Suffering Servant | Isaiah 53:5 — pierced for our transgressions | "But he was pierced for our transgressions; he was crushed for our iniquities; upon him was the chastisement that brought us peace, and with his wounds we are healed." — Isaiah 53:5 |
| Isaiah 54 | Sing, O Barren One | Isaiah 54:10 — my steadfast love shall not depart | "For the mountains may depart and the hills be removed, but my steadfast love shall not depart from you." — Isaiah 54:10 |
| Isaiah 55 | Come, Buy Without Money | Isaiah 55:1 — come to the waters | "Seek the LORD while he may be found; call upon him while he is near." — Isaiah 55:6 |
| Isaiah 56 | A House of Prayer for All Peoples | Isaiah 56:7 — a house of prayer for all peoples | "The Lord GOD, who gathers the outcasts of Israel, declares, 'I will gather yet others to him besides those already gathered.'" — Isaiah 56:8 |
| Isaiah 57 | With the Contrite | Isaiah 57:15 — I dwell with the contrite | "I dwell in the high and holy place, and also with him who is of a contrite and lowly spirit." — Isaiah 57:15 |
| Isaiah 58 | The Fast God Chooses | Isaiah 58:6 — loose the bonds of wickedness | "Then shall your light break forth like the dawn, and your healing shall spring up speedily." — Isaiah 58:8 |
| Isaiah 59 | The Redeemer Comes to Zion | Isaiah 59:20 — to those who turn from transgression | "And a Redeemer will come to Zion, to those in Jacob who turn from transgression, declares the LORD." — Isaiah 59:20 |
| Isaiah 60 | Arise, Shine | Isaiah 60:1 — your light has come | "Arise, shine, for your light has come, and the glory of the LORD has risen upon you." — Isaiah 60:1 |
| Isaiah 61 | The Year of the LORD's Favor | Isaiah 61:1 — the Spirit of the Lord is upon me | "He has sent me to bind up the brokenhearted, to proclaim liberty to the captives." — Isaiah 61:1 |
| Isaiah 62 | A New Name | Isaiah 62:4 — My Delight Is in Her | "And you shall be called Sought Out, A City Not Forsaken." — Isaiah 62:12 |
| Isaiah 63 | Mighty to Save | Isaiah 63:1 — speaking in righteousness, mighty to save | "It is I, speaking in righteousness, mighty to save." — Isaiah 63:1 |
| Isaiah 64 | Rend the Heavens | Isaiah 64:1 — oh that You would come down | "But now, O LORD, you are our Father; we are the clay, and you are our potter; we are all the work of your hand." — Isaiah 64:8 |
| Isaiah 65 | New Heavens and a New Earth | Isaiah 65:17 — I create new heavens and a new earth | "Before they call I will answer; while they are yet speaking I will hear." — Isaiah 65:24 |
| Isaiah 66 | Heaven Is My Throne | Isaiah 66:2 — the one to whom I will look | "But this is the one to whom I will look: he who is humble and contrite in spirit and trembles at my word." — Isaiah 66:2 |

### Study Questions — One per Chapter

Work each question in your notebook after the chapter's reflection question. Many follow the Bible's own pattern of putting off the old and putting on the new (Ephesians 4:22–24; Colossians 3:8–10).

1. **Isaiah 1 — Come and reason.** Put off one untreated wound you have been covering with religious activity. Put on Isaiah 1:17: one act of justice or mercy toward someone powerless this week. Name the person.
2. **Isaiah 2 — Walk in the light.** Put off one exalted thing — a trust, a fear, a pride — that rivals the mountain of the LORD. Put on one step of walking in His light: a path from His teaching you have been postponing. Write it down with a day.
3. **Isaiah 3 — Props removed.** Put off leaning on one prop as if it were God. Put on Isaiah 3:15's question as your own audit: is anyone being crushed by your choices? Make one repair.
4. **Isaiah 4 — Washed by fire.** Put off asking God to shelter an unconfessed filth. Put on honesty: name the thing that needs His washing, out loud to Him, and receive the canopy of Isaiah 4:6 as a promise, not a feeling.
5. **Isaiah 5 — Fruit inspection.** Put off one stink-fruit habit — greed, drink, cynicism, moral confusion — from the six woes. Put on abiding: fifteen unhurried minutes in John 15 this week, asking the true Vine to do in you what the old vine could not.
6. **Isaiah 6 — Undone, cleansed, sent.** Put off serving God from an uncleansed conscience. Put on the order of Isaiah 6: confess until you say "Woe is me," receive the coal, then write one "Here am I, send me" step you will take this month.
7. **Isaiah 7 — Stand firm in faith.** Put off one self-salvation plan you are quietly funding. Put on Isaiah 7:9 as a daily sentence for one week: say it every morning about your biggest fear, by name.
8. **Isaiah 8 — Fear Him, not them.** Put off one conspiracy-fear that has been setting your mood. Put on Isaiah 8:20: one decision this week made by "the teaching and the testimony" instead of by the loudest voice. Record both.
9. **Isaiah 9 — Bring the need to the Name.** Put off carrying one burden under your own government. Put on the matching name of the Child — Counselor, Mighty God, Everlasting Father, Prince of Peace — and pray that name over the burden daily for a week.
10. **Isaiah 10 — Lean in truth.** Put off leaning on the thing that struck you — the old alliance, the old coping. Put on remnant-faith: one concrete act of leaning on the mighty God "in truth," not in slogan.
11. **Isaiah 11 — Stump hope.** Put off pronouncing one cut-down area of your life dead. Put on stump-watching: write down the stump, and one small shoot of grace you can already see, however small.
12. **Isaiah 12 — Draw with joy.** Put off living on an old drink of grace. Put on a daily drawing: one verse from Isaiah 12 said aloud each morning this week, with thanks for anger turned away.
13. **Isaiah 13 — Babylon's lease.** Put off treating one proud system or habit as permanent. Put on the long view: write its name and Isaiah 13:11 over it, and refuse one participation in it this week.
14. **Isaiah 14 — Retire the five "I wills."** Put off one "I will ascend" ambition of the flesh. Put on refuge: say Isaiah 14:32 as your answer when the messengers ask what your hope is built on.
15. **Isaiah 15 — Weep like God.** Put off gloating or shrugging over one judged or fallen person. Put on God's heart for Moab: pray for that person by name three times this week, and do one kindness if a door opens.
16. **Isaiah 16 — Walk to the throne.** Put off one pride that keeps you wailing instead of bowing. Put on approach: come to the throne established in steadfast love (and Hebrews 4:16) with the exact request you have been too proud to bring.
17. **Isaiah 17 — Turn the gaze.** Put off looking to one altar of your own hands. Put on Isaiah 17:7: begin each day this week by literally saying, "Today I look to my Maker," and note what changes.
18. **Isaiah 18 — Honor the stillness.** Put off demanding that God perform on your timetable. Put on heat-of-sunshine faith: one area where you will work and wait quietly for thirty days without forcing an outcome.
19. **Isaiah 19 — Bless the enemy.** Put off one settled contempt for an "Egypt" or "Assyria" in your life. Put on Isaiah 19:25 as a prayer: ask God to make that person "my people... the work of my hands."
20. **Isaiah 20 — Barefoot obedience.** Put off one Egypt-flight you are planning. Put on Isaiah's kind of obedience: one costly, dignity-expensive act of faithfulness you have been avoiding. Do it this week.
21. **Isaiah 21 — Stay at the post.** Put off demanding a sunrise on schedule. Put on the watchman's answer: name your night honestly, affirm the morning, and return to your post — prayer, Word, fellowship — without quitting.
22. **Isaiah 22 — Hang on the Peg.** Put off tomb-carving: one place you are building your name. Put on vessel-life: transfer one specific weight from your shoulders to the Peg of Isaiah 22:23 — and to Jesus of Revelation 3:7 — in prayer, by name.
23. **Isaiah 23 — Consecrate the merchandise.** Put off assuming God cannot use your worst years' earnings — skills, experience, knowledge of the far country. Put on an offering: name one such thing and give it a holy use this month.
24. **Isaiah 24 — Start with the reign.** Put off reading the world's shaking as the final fact. Put on Isaiah 24:23: when the news frightens you this week, say first, "The LORD of hosts reigns on Mount Zion," and pray from there.
25. **Isaiah 25 — Wait at the table.** Put off one self-hosted feast — a comfort that cannot lift the veil. Put on waiting: set aside one meal this week to thank God in advance for the feast of Isaiah 25:6–8 and the tears He will wipe away.
26. **Isaiah 26 — Stay the mind.** Put off leaning your mind on the news, the symptom, or the balance. Put on *shalom shalom*: ten minutes daily this week with your mind deliberately stayed on Isaiah 26:3–4, read aloud.
27. **Isaiah 27 — Smash the altar stones.** Put off relating to God as an inspector to fear. Put on Keeper-trust — and take a hammer to one idol: a literal, physical act of removing one altar stone from your life this week.
28. **Isaiah 28 — Move your weight.** Put off one covenant with death — a lie you hide in. Put on the Cornerstone: name the lie, renounce it to God and one trusted person, and rehearse Isaiah 28:16 until panic loses its argument.
29. **Isaiah 29 — Unseal the book.** Put off lip-honor with a far heart in one habit of worship. Put on fresh joy for the meek: read one chapter of Isaiah aloud this week as if hearing it for the first time, and write what you actually heard.
30. **Isaiah 30 — Returning and rest.** Put off one swift-horse scheme. Put on quietness and trust: cancel one frantic effort, and take the saved time as literal rest before God — no phone, no fixing — this week.
31. **Isaiah 31 — Change the direction of the look.** Put off consulting one fleshly helper before God. Put on Isaiah 31:1's order: for your next real decision, consult the LORD first — in prayer and the Word — before any other counsel. Note the difference.
32. **Isaiah 32 — Take shelter.** Put off complacency about one guaranteed tomorrow. Put on the King's shelter: choose your needed image — hiding place, shelter, streams, shade — and pray from inside it daily this week.
33. **Isaiah 33 — Arm every morning.** Put off stockpiling anxiety as your treasure. Put on Isaiah 33:2 as a morning prayer for seven days, and Isaiah 33:22 as your confession whenever you need God to be one of His four offices.
34. **Isaiah 34 — Hand over the cause.** Put off nursing one Edom-grudge. Put on Romans 12:19 with Isaiah 34:8: write the cause down, pray it into God's day of vengeance, and do one act of release toward the person.
35. **Isaiah 35 — Walk the highway.** Put off believing your shut senses are permanent. Put on messenger-duty: say Isaiah 35:3–4 to one anxious-hearted person this week, in person, and mean it.
36. **Isaiah 36 — Silence at the wall.** Put off debating your Rabshakeh. Put on the king's command: identify the mocking voice's main argument, refuse it an answer, and set a time to carry it to God instead.
37. **Isaiah 37 — Spread the letter.** Put off re-reading your threatening letter alone. Put on Hezekiah's act: take the actual document, diagnosis, bill, or message, spread it before the LORD in prayer, and pray Isaiah 37:16–20 over it in your own words.
38. **Isaiah 38 — Turn to the wall.** Put off stoicism about one sentence you are living under. Put on Hezekiah's tears: pray honestly about it — and then write your own short testimony of one past bitterness God turned to welfare (Isaiah 38:17).
39. **Isaiah 39 — Show God, not treasure.** Put off one display of your assets, recovery, or knowledge. Put on testimony: the next time you speak of God's deliverance, name Him five times more than you name yourself. Count.
40. **Isaiah 40 — Wait for renewal.** Put off concluding that your way is hidden from God. Put on *qavah*-waiting: choose one promise from Isaiah 40 and wait on it deliberately — daily, aloud — for the length of this chapter's study.
41. **Isaiah 41 — Take the held hand.** Put off feeding one fear with arguments. Put on the five "I wills": write Isaiah 41:10 on a card, carry it a week, and answer the fear with the verse each time it speaks.
42. **Isaiah 42 — Let Him tend the wick.** Put off hiding your bruised, smoldering state from God. Put on the Servant's gentleness toward one other bruised reed: an act of patience with someone you would normally snap off or write off.
43. **Isaiah 43 — Hear your name.** Put off living as if your record introduces you to God. Put on Isaiah 43:1 and 43:25 together: say each morning, "He calls me by name; He will not remember my sins," and live one day as if both are fully true.
44. **Isaiah 44 — Ask the question.** Put off feeding on ashes. Put on Isaiah 44:20's question about one attachment — "Is there not a lie in my right hand?" — and act on the answer: return, because you have been redeemed.
45. **Isaiah 45 — Look and live.** Put off striving like clay against the Potter in one circumstance. Put on Spurgeon's simplicity: one honest look to Jesus about that circumstance, repeated every time the striving starts.
46. **Isaiah 46 — Be carried.** Put off hauling one burden-god — guilt, religion, self-salvation. Put on being carried: pray Isaiah 46:4 over your age, your weariness, and your future, and accept one act of help from God's people without apologizing for it.
47. **Isaiah 47 — Dethrone the "I am."** Put off one sentence of self-sufficiency ("I don't need..."). Put on Isaiah 47:4 as a confession said aloud: "Our Redeemer — the LORD of hosts is his name." Name one need to one person this week.
48. **Isaiah 48 — Go out from Babylon.** Put off trusting your refining as your standing. Put on name's-sake trust — and take one literal exit-step from your Babylon: a place, a habit, a subscription, a route you will no longer take.
49. **Isaiah 49 — Answer the feeling with the engraving.** Put off letting "forsaken and forgotten" go unchallenged. Put on Isaiah 49:15–16: every time the feeling comes this week, answer it aloud with the verse — and encourage one other "Zion" with it.
50. **Isaiah 50 — Kindle no torches.** Put off one self-lit torch — a scheme for light God has not given. Put on morning-by-morning teachability: fifteen minutes daily, ear first, mouth second, for the week of this chapter.
51. **Isaiah 51 — Look to the quarry.** Put off measuring God's future by your present smallness. Put on quarry-memory: write your Abraham-and-Sarah list — the small beginnings God has already multiplied in your life — and thank Him over it.
52. **Isaiah 52 — Run with the news.** Put off silence about the reign of God. Put on beautiful feet: carry Isaiah 52:7's headline — "Your God reigns" — to one person in darkness this week, in words they can hear.
53. **Isaiah 53 — Live at the exchange.** Put off carrying iniquity the Servant already bore. Put on Isaiah 53:5–6 as a daily confession and thanksgiving for one week — and memorize verse 5. If you do nothing else in this study, do this.
54. **Isaiah 54 — Enlarge the tent.** Put off measuring God's love by the brief moment. Put on tent-enlarging faith: prepare for one growth you cannot yet see — lengthen one cord, strengthen one stake — as an act of trust in Isaiah 54:10.
55. **Isaiah 55 — Bring your thirst.** Put off spending on one thing that is not bread. Put on the free market: redirect that money, time, or hunger toward the feast — and invite one thirsty person to come with you.
56. **Isaiah 56 — Gather, don't gatekeep.** Put off one way you have assumed the door was not for you — or not for someone else. Put on the Gatherer's work: welcome one outcast concretely this week, in Jesus' name.
57. **Isaiah 57 — Give God His address.** Put off presenting your improved self for God's approval. Put on contrition: confess one thing until it is pulverized — no softening words — and then receive Isaiah 57:18: "I have seen his ways, but I will heal him."
58. **Isaiah 58 — Loose one yoke.** Put off one religious performance that coexists with a burden you could lift. Put on the chosen fast: share bread, house, clothing, or freedom with one specific person this week — and watch for the dawn of verse 8.
59. **Isaiah 59 — Confess in the first person.** Put off "they" language about the culture's sins. Put on Isaiah 59's "we": write a confession of your own part — your separation, your silence — and then claim Isaiah 59:20–21 for your children and grandchildren by name.
60. **Isaiah 60 — Arise and shine.** Put off sitting in the dark as if the light had not come. Put on rising: one public, unhidden act of light this week — a kindness, a witness, a generosity — done because your Light has come.
61. **Isaiah 61 — Work the exchange.** Put off keeping your ashes as identity. Put on the Anointed's trade: bring ashes, mourning, or faintness to Him in prayer by name — then take up one rebuilding task among ruins, however small, as an oak of righteousness.
62. **Isaiah 62 — Give Him no rest.** Put off answering to an old name. Put on watchman-duty: choose one promise of God and pray it back to Him daily this week — take no rest, give Him no rest — and answer only to "Sought Out, A City Not Forsaken."
63. **Isaiah 63 — Recount the steadfast love.** Put off praying complaints-first. Put on Isaiah 63:7: before your next hard prayer, list aloud the steadfast loves of the LORD in your own history — then plead "You are our Father" over the present trouble.
64. **Isaiah 64 — Come as clay.** Put off bringing God a résumé. Put on Isaiah 64:6 and 64:8 together: confess the rags, claim the Potter, and ask Him — plainly, in prayer — to rend the heavens in one situation where improvement is not enough.
65. **Isaiah 65 — Leave the table of Fortune.** Put off one reliance on luck, fate, or omens — even joking ones. Put on Isaiah 65:24's confidence: pray one prayer this week expecting to be answered mid-sentence, and record what happens.
66. **Isaiah 66 — Tremble and worship.** Put off one way you have tried to house or manage God. Put on the three qualifications — humility, contrition, trembling at the Word — and close this study where the book closes: on your knees in worship, giving Him the last word.

---
## Isaiah 1 — Scarlet Made White: The Indictment and the Invitation

### Jesus in this chapter

Jesus stands in this chapter as the wounded Physician calling His own sick people home. Isaiah's Judah has "forsaken the LORD" and is covered head to toe with unhealed wounds — and yet the chapter ends not with the door slammed but with God pleading, "Come now, let us reason together." That pleading God is the God who would come in the flesh. When Jesus stood over Jerusalem and wept — "How often would I have gathered your children together as a hen gathers her brood under her wings, and you were not willing!" (Matthew 23:37) — He was speaking with the same heart that speaks in Isaiah 1. And the promise at the chapter's center — scarlet sins made white as snow — is a promise only His blood could keep.

### Commentary

**The charge is read in heaven's court (1:1–9).** Isaiah opens like a courtroom. "Hear, O heavens, and give ear, O earth" — the whole creation is summoned as witness. God's complaint is not first about rituals broken but about children lost: "Children have I reared and brought up, but they have rebelled against me." Even an ox knows its owner and a donkey its master's crib, "but Israel does not know, my people do not understand." Sin, at its root, is not ignorance of rules; it is the refusal to know a Father. Then comes the diagnosis, and it reads like a chart from an emergency room: "The whole head is sick, and the whole heart faint. From the sole of the foot even to the head, there is no soundness in it, but bruises and sores and raw wounds; they are not pressed out or bound up or softened with oil." That is the human condition without grace — untreated, unbound, and getting worse. Anyone who has watched addiction or sin run its course knows this picture is not exaggeration. And yet a remnant survives: "If the LORD of hosts had not left us a few survivors, we should have been like Sodom." Survival itself is mercy.

**God is sick of their sacrifices (1:10–15).** Here is the shock of the chapter. God does not say their worship is too small; He says it nauseates Him. "I have had enough of burnt offerings... I do not delight in the blood of bulls." Their incense is "an abomination," their assemblies He "cannot endure," and — hardest of all — "even though you make many prayers, I will not listen; your hands are full of blood." Religion without righteousness is not neutral to God; it is offensive. Isaiah is not against sacrifice — God commanded it — he is against sacrifice used as a cover for a life of injustice. A man can sing every song, sit in every service, and still have blood on his hands. God will not be used as an alibi.

**The remedy: wash, and learn to do good (1:16–17).** Before the promise comes the call: "Wash yourselves; make yourselves clean; remove the evil of your deeds from before my eyes; cease to do evil, learn to do good; seek justice, correct oppression; bring justice to the fatherless, plead the widow's cause." The Hebrew word *rachats* means to wash or bathe — not a token rinse but a full washing. And notice where true washing shows itself: in how we treat the powerless. The fatherless and the widow are the Bible's standing test of whether religion is real. James would later say the same: "Religion that is pure and undefiled before God the Father is this: to visit orphans and widows in their affliction" (James 1:27). Isaiah and James are preaching the same sermon.

**The great invitation (1:18–20).** Then comes one of the most tender verses in the Old Testament:

> "Come now, let us reason together, says the LORD: though your sins are like scarlet, they shall be as white as snow; though they are red like crimson, they shall become like wool." — Isaiah 1:18 (ESV)

Scarlet was a fast dye — it did not wash out. That is the point. These are sins that cannot be undone or rinsed away by effort, and God says they shall be white as snow. Not improved. Not off-white. White. But the invitation has two edges: "If you are willing and obedient, you shall eat the good of the land; but if you refuse and rebel, you shall be eaten by the sword." God reasons with sinners; He does not negotiate with rebellion forever.

**Zion redeemed by justice (1:21–31).** The chapter closes with a lament over the faithful city become a whore, and a promise that God Himself will restore her: "Zion shall be redeemed by justice, and those in her who repent, by righteousness." The oaks and gardens of idol worship will wither. God's way of cleaning a city — or a life — is not to pretend the rot isn't there, but to burn it out and plant righteousness in its place.

### Key verses

> "Ah, sinful nation, a people laden with iniquity, offspring of evildoers, children who deal corruptly! They have forsaken the LORD, they have despised the Holy One of Israel, they are utterly estranged." — Isaiah 1:4 (ESV)

> "Wash yourselves; make yourselves clean; remove the evil of your deeds from before my eyes; cease to do evil, learn to do good; seek justice, correct oppression; bring justice to the fatherless, plead the widow's cause." — Isaiah 1:16–17 (ESV)

> "Come now, let us reason together, says the LORD: though your sins are like scarlet, they shall be as white as snow; though they are red like crimson, they shall become like wool." — Isaiah 1:18 (ESV)

### The bad news and the good news

The bad news of Isaiah 1 is that our sickness is total — head to foot, no soundness in us — and that religious activity cannot cover it; God sees the blood on our hands even while we sing. The good news is that the same God who exposes the wound invites the wounded to come and reason with Him, and He promises a cleansing deeper than the stain: scarlet made white as snow. That promise waited seven hundred years for its means — the blood of Jesus, in which, John says, God cleanses us from all sin (1 John 1:7). The invitation of Isaiah 1:18 is still open, and it is open to you.

**A note from the Reformers.** Spurgeon preached Isaiah 1:18 to sinners all his life and called it a text that had brought thousands home; he loved that God does not say "though your sins are pink" but scarlet — the deepest dye — and yet promises snow. That is grace for the worst cases, which is the only kind there is.

**Reflection question:** Where is your life still carrying an untreated wound you have covered with religious activity instead of bringing to God — and will you come and reason with Him about it today?

---

## Isaiah 2 — The Mountain of the LORD: Pride Brought Low

### Jesus in this chapter

Jesus is the King of the mountain in this chapter — the One to whose house all the nations will one day stream. When Isaiah sees the nations flowing to Zion to learn God's ways and beat their swords into plowshares, he is seeing the finished work of Christ and the mission of His church. Jesus told a Samaritan woman that the hour was coming when worship would no longer be tied to a mountain (John 4:21–24); He was announcing that Isaiah's mountain had come down to meet the world. And the chapter's thunder against human pride — "the haughtiness of man shall be humbled" — is the same word Jesus preached when He said that everyone who exalts himself will be humbled, and the humble exalted (Luke 14:11).

### Commentary

**The mountain exalted (2:1–4).** Isaiah's second vision lifts our eyes from the courtroom to the last days: "It shall come to pass in the latter days that the mountain of the house of the LORD shall be established as the highest of the mountains, and shall be lifted up above the hills; and all the nations shall flow to it." In the ancient world, mountains were where gods lived. Isaiah says the LORD's house will tower over every rival claim, and the nations — not just Israel — will stream uphill like a river flowing the wrong way, drawn by grace. They come saying, "Come, let us go up to the mountain of the LORD... that he may teach us his ways and that we may walk in his paths." Notice what the nations want: not entertainment, not blessing without obedience — teaching, and paths to walk in. And from that teaching comes the most famous peace in the Bible: "they shall beat their swords into plowshares, and their spears into pruning hooks; nation shall not lift up sword against nation, neither shall they learn war anymore." That peace is not man's achievement. It flows from the reign of the Prince of Peace, and the church lives now as its firstfruits — people from every nation learning a new way to live.

**Walk in the light (2:5).** The vision ends with an appeal: "O house of Jacob, come, let us walk in the light of the LORD." The future is certain; the question is whether God's own people will walk in it now. John picks up the same words: "If we walk in the light, as he is in the light, we have fellowship with one another, and the blood of Jesus his Son cleanses us from all sin" (1 John 1:7).

**The day of the LORD against pride (2:6–22).** But before the mountain is exalted, everything man exalts must fall. Isaiah turns on Judah's present: full of silver and gold, horses and chariots, fortune-tellers from the east, and idols of their own making. The charge is precise — they trusted in wealth, weapons, and worship of their own design. Against all of it stands one refrain: "the LORD alone will be exalted in that day." The cedars of Lebanon, the oaks of Bashan, the lofty mountains, the fortified walls, the ships of Tarshish — every symbol of human height and strength — "shall be brought low." And the chapter ends with a sentence worth memorizing for a lifetime: "Stop regarding man in whose nostrils is breath, for of what account is he?" Man's breath is borrowed. The Hebrew word for breath here is *neshamah*, the same breath God breathed into Adam. We are one withheld breath away from dust, and Isaiah says: build your life on Someone sturdier.

### Key verses

> "It shall come to pass in the latter days that the mountain of the house of the LORD shall be established as the highest of the mountains, and shall be lifted up above the hills; and all the nations shall flow to it." — Isaiah 2:2 (ESV)

> "He shall judge between the nations, and shall decide disputes for many peoples; and they shall beat their swords into plowshares, and their spears into pruning hooks; nation shall not lift up sword against nation, neither shall they learn war anymore." — Isaiah 2:4 (ESV)

> "For the LORD of hosts has a day against all that is proud and lofty, against all that is lifted up — and it shall be brought low." — Isaiah 2:12 (ESV)

> "Stop regarding man in whose nostrils is breath, for of what account is he?" — Isaiah 2:22 (ESV)

### The bad news and the good news

The bad news of Isaiah 2 is that everything we lift up in God's place — money, strength, reputation, self — is scheduled for demolition; the day of the LORD humbles all human height. The good news is that the same God is raising one mountain that will never fall, and He invites the nations — including nobodies like us — to stream to it and learn His ways. In Christ, the humble are not crushed by that day; they are carried by it, because the King of the mountain first humbled Himself for them.

**A note from the Reformers.** Calvin, commenting on this chapter, pressed home that the church's glory is not in its height but in its Head — the mountain is exalted because Christ is exalted, and men stream to it only where His Word is taught plainly. Pride builds towers; grace builds a mountain with Christ at the top.

**Reflection question:** What have you been "regarding" — trusting, fearing, or building on — that is only breath in the nostrils? What would walking in the light of the LORD look like instead, this week?

---

## Isaiah 3 — When the Props Are Kicked Away

### Jesus in this chapter

Jesus appears in this chapter mostly by contrast — He is the righteous Judge and the true Bread that Judah is about to lose. Isaiah announces that God will take away from Jerusalem "support and supply" — the Hebrew phrase *mash'en umash'enah* covers both the staff of bread and the staff of water — and with them every class of leader the city leans on: the mighty man, the soldier, the judge, the prophet, the elder, the counselor. When God removes props, He is not being cruel; He is showing a people that their props were never God. Jesus is the prop that cannot be removed: "I am the bread of life" (John 6:35), and the government that rests on His shoulders does not collapse into the chaos of Isaiah 3, where boys are princes and infants rule.

### Commentary

**Support and supply removed (3:1–7).** The judgment Isaiah describes here is subtle and devastating. God does not at first send an army; He removes competence. Bread and water go scarce, and leadership goes worse — "I will make boys their princes, and infants shall rule over them." A society unravels not only when it is attacked but when it is abandoned to its own folly. The scene at the end is almost comic and entirely sad: a man grabs his brother in their father's house and says, "You have a cloak; you shall be our leader," and the brother refuses: "I will not be a healer of wounds... you shall not make me leader of the people." Nobody wants the job because everybody knows there is nothing left to lead with. That is what it looks like when God gives a people over — not fire from heaven, but the quiet removal of everything they trusted instead of Him.

**The cause named plainly (3:8–15).** Why? "For Jerusalem has stumbled, and Judah has fallen, because their speech and their deeds are against the LORD, defying his glorious presence." Isaiah refuses to let them blame circumstances: "they proclaim their sin like Sodom; they do not hide it." Shamelessness is itself a stage of judgment — when sin stops blushing, the fall is far along. Then God rises to judge, and His indictment lands on the leaders: "It is you who have devoured the vineyard. The spoil of the poor is in your houses. What do you mean by crushing my people, by grinding the face of the poor?" declares the Lord GOD of hosts. God always asks the powerful about the poor. A faith that grinds the faces of the poor while keeping up appearances has already been weighed, whatever its attendance numbers say.

**The daughters of Zion (3:16–4:1).** The last movement pictures the proud daughters of Zion — haughty, mincing, tinkling with jewelry — and lists their finery piece by piece, twenty-one items of it, only to say it will all be replaced with sackcloth, branding, and baldness. The point is not that God despises beauty; it is that beauty used as pride's costume will be stripped. The chapter spills into 4:1 with seven women seizing one man, begging only for his name — a picture of a city so emptied that shame replaces dignity. Isaiah wants Judah to feel the end of the road she is on while there is still time to turn.

### Key verses

> "For behold, the Lord GOD of hosts is taking away from Jerusalem and from Judah support and supply, all support of bread, and all support of water." — Isaiah 3:1 (ESV)

> "For Jerusalem has stumbled, and Judah has fallen, because their speech and their deeds are against the LORD, defying his glorious presence." — Isaiah 3:8 (ESV)

> "What do you mean by crushing my people, by grinding the face of the poor?" declares the Lord GOD of hosts. — Isaiah 3:15 (ESV)

### The bad news and the good news

The bad news of Isaiah 3 is that God can judge a life without lifting a finger — simply by removing the props we leaned on instead of Him, and letting our own folly run the house. The good news is that the removal of props is often the first mercy: when bread, water, and leaders fail, a man finally learns Whom he actually needs. Jesus is the Bread that is never taken away and the King whose reign never dissolves into boys and chaos — and He receives everyone whose props have been kicked out from under them.

**A note from the Reformers.** Luther often warned that the worst judgment is when God stops restraining and lets men have their own way — Isaiah 3 is that doctrine in narrative form. The Reformers read this chapter as a mirror for any church or nation that keeps the shell of religion after losing its substance.

**Reflection question:** Which "prop" — a person, a habit, a resource, a reputation — have you been leaning on as if it were God? If He removed it, what would you discover about your trust?

---

## Isaiah 4 — The Branch of the LORD: Washed and Sheltered

### Jesus in this chapter

Jesus is named here before He is named: "the branch of the LORD shall be beautiful and glorious." The Hebrew word is *tsemach* — a shoot, a sprout, new growth out of what looked dead. Jeremiah will call Him "a righteous Branch" (Jeremiah 23:5), Zechariah will call God's Servant "the Branch" (Zechariah 3:8), and Matthew will note that Jesus' hometown of Nazareth fulfilled what was spoken — "He shall be called a Nazarene," a branch-man from a stump-town (Matthew 2:23). After Isaiah 3's stripping, chapter 4 is the sprout pushing up through burned ground: Christ, beautiful and glorious, and a remnant washed clean by "a spirit of judgment and by a spirit of burning."

### Commentary

**Beauty after burning (4:2).** "In that day the branch of the LORD shall be beautiful and glorious, and the fruit of the land shall be the pride and honor of the survivors of Israel." Everything chapter 3 said would be stripped — the pride, the finery, the false glory — is answered here with a glory that is not false. The survivors' pride will not be in themselves but in the Branch and in the fruit God grows. This is the pattern of grace: God does not restore our old glory; He gives us His.

**A remnant recorded for life (4:3–4).** "And he who is left in Zion and remains in Jerusalem will be called holy, everyone who has been recorded for life in Jerusalem." There is a book, and there are names in it, and being left — being part of the remnant — is not an accident but a recording. Then the washing: "when the Lord shall have washed away the filth of the daughters of Zion and cleansed the bloodstains of Jerusalem from its midst by a spirit of judgment and by a spirit of burning." God's cleansing is not cosmetic. The filth of chapter 3 is washed by fire — judgment faced and passed through, not avoided. That is how God cleans a life, too. He does not airbrush the past; He burns out the corruption and washes what remains, and what remains is holy.

**Canopy and shelter (4:5–6).** The chapter ends with one of the warmest pictures in Isaiah: God will recreate the glory of the Exodus over Mount Zion — cloud by day, shining flaming fire by night, "a canopy" over all the glory. "There will be a booth for shade by day from the heat, and for a refuge and a shelter from the storm and rain." The God who led Israel through the wilderness with a pillar of cloud and fire will Himself be the shelter of His remnant. A canopy in Hebrew is *chuppah* — the same word used for the bridal canopy at a wedding. Over the washed remnant, God spreads a wedding canopy. Judgment ends in marriage. Burning ends in shelter. That is where this whole book is heading.

### Key verses

> "In that day the branch of the LORD shall be beautiful and glorious, and the fruit of the land shall be the pride and honor of the survivors of Israel." — Isaiah 4:2 (ESV)

> "And he who is left in Zion and remains in Jerusalem will be called holy, everyone who has been recorded for life in Jerusalem, when the Lord shall have washed away the filth of the daughters of Zion and cleansed the bloodstains of Jerusalem from its midst by a spirit of judgment and by a spirit of burning." — Isaiah 4:3–4 (ESV)

> "There will be a booth for shade by day from the heat, and for a refuge and a shelter from the storm and rain." — Isaiah 4:6 (ESV)

### The bad news and the good news

The bad news of Isaiah 4 is that no one enters the shelter unwashed — the filth must be dealt with, and God's cleansing comes by judgment and burning, not by pretending. The good news is that God does the washing Himself, records names for life, and spreads His own glory as a canopy over the very people who were stripped in chapter 3. The Branch who is beautiful and glorious is Jesus, and everyone washed in His blood gets the shelter.

**A note from the Reformers.** Calvin loved this chapter's teaching that the church is not gathered by human purity but created by God's washing — the remnant is holy because the Lord cleansed it, not because it cleansed itself. That is the order of grace in every age.

**Reflection question:** Have you let God wash you His way — through honest judgment on your sin — or are you still asking Him to shelter a filth you refuse to name? What needs the fire today?

---

## Isaiah 5 — The Song of the Vineyard: Fruit God Has a Right to Expect

### Jesus in this chapter

Jesus takes this chapter onto His own lips. When He tells the parable of the wicked tenants — a man plants a vineyard, builds a fence, digs a winepress, builds a tower, and leases it to tenants who beat his servants and kill his son (Matthew 21:33–41) — He is preaching Isaiah 5, and every hearer in the temple knew it. Jesus is the Son the vineyard owner finally sends. He is also the true Vine (John 15:1) — what Israel failed to be, He is. And the chapter's six woes fall from His lips too in Matthew 23, because the Judge of Isaiah 5 and the Jesus of the Gospels are one and the same Lord.

### Commentary

**The beloved's vineyard (5:1–7).** Isaiah begins with a love song that turns into an indictment: "Let me sing for my beloved my love song concerning his vineyard." God planted His vineyard on a fertile hill, cleared its stones, planted choice vines, built a tower, hewed a wine vat — He did everything. "And he looked for it to yield grapes, but it yielded wild grapes." The wild grapes in Hebrew are *be'ushim* — stink-fruit, worthless and rotten. Then God Himself appeals to the jury: "And now, O inhabitants of Jerusalem and men of Judah, judge between me and my vineyard. What more was there to do for my vineyard, that I have not done in it?" There is no answer, because there is no excuse. The verdict comes in verse 7, and it is a wordplay that stings in Hebrew: God "looked for justice (*mishpat*), but behold, bloodshed (*mispach*); for righteousness (*tsedaqah*), but behold, an outcry (*tse'aqah*)!" The sounds are nearly the same; the realities are opposites. God got the echo of righteousness without the thing itself.

**Six woes (5:8–23).** Then come the woes, and they read like a tour of a society rotting from the top: land-grabbing greed that joins house to house until there is no room for anyone else; drunkenness that starts early and burns late, with music at the feasts but no regard for the deeds of the LORD; cynics who drag sin along "with cords of falsehood," daring God to hurry up and act; moral confusion that "calls evil good and good evil, who put darkness for light and light for darkness"; intellectual pride, "wise in their own eyes"; and corrupt courts where the wicked are acquitted for a bribe and the innocent are denied their rights. Isaiah is not listing random sins. He is describing what a vineyard looks like when it produces stink-fruit at every level — economic, spiritual, moral, judicial. A nation does not fall all at once. It ferments.

**The distant army (5:24–30).** The chapter ends with fire and a whistle. Because they "have rejected the law of the LORD of hosts, and have despised the word of the Holy One of Israel," God's anger kindles, the mountains tremble, and He whistles for a nation at the ends of the earth — swift, tireless, roaring like a lion, from whom no one can rescue the prey. Judgment in Isaiah is never God losing His temper. It is God handing a people over, at last, to the consequences they chose, by means of nations He sovereignly summons. The darkness at the chapter's end — "distress and darkness, the gloom of anguish" — is what a land looks like after the Vineyard Owner removes His hedge.

### Key verses

> "What more was there to do for my vineyard, that I have not done in it? When I looked for it to yield grapes, why did it yield wild grapes?" — Isaiah 5:4 (ESV)

> "For the vineyard of the LORD of hosts is the house of Israel, and the men of Judah are his pleasant planting; and he looked for justice, but behold, bloodshed; for righteousness, but behold, an outcry!" — Isaiah 5:7 (ESV)

> "Woe to those who call evil good and good evil, who put darkness for light and light for darkness, who put bitter for sweet and sweet for bitter!" — Isaiah 5:20 (ESV)

### The bad news and the good news

The bad news of Isaiah 5 is that God has done everything for His vineyard — for us — and He has a right to fruit; privilege without fruitfulness ends in a removed hedge and a trampled field. The good news is that the Owner did not finally abandon the vineyard. He sent His Son into it — and though the tenants killed Him, His death became the planting of a new vineyard, the true Vine with branches grafted in from every nation, bearing fruit by simply abiding in Him. If your life has produced stink-fruit, the answer is not to try harder in the old vine. It is to be grafted into the new one.

**A note from the Reformers.** Spurgeon preached "What more was there to do?" as one of the most searching questions in Scripture — a text that leaves the sinner without excuse and the saint without any ground but grace. If God did all this for Israel, and Israel still failed, then no man's hope can rest in what God finds in him, but only in what God gives him in Christ.

**Reflection question:** God asks, "What more was there to do for my vineyard, that I have not done in it?" What has God done for you — and what fruit, honestly, has it produced so far?

---

## Isaiah 6 — Holy, Holy, Holy: The Prophet Undone and Sent

### Jesus in this chapter

This is the chapter where Isaiah saw Jesus. John tells us so: "Isaiah said these things because he saw his glory and spoke of him" (John 12:41) — and John quotes this very chapter, the commission to preach to hearts that would not hear. The King on the throne, high and lifted up, is Christ in His glory. The coal that touches Isaiah's lips and takes away his guilt is a picture of the atonement Christ would accomplish — cleansing that comes from the altar, not from the sinner. And the "holy seed" in the stump at the chapter's end is the promise that from a felled nation, a shoot would come — the stump of Jesse, from which the Branch would grow (Isaiah 11:1).

### Commentary

**The King is not dead (6:1–4).** "In the year that King Uzziah died I saw the Lord sitting upon a throne, high and lifted up; and the train of his robe filled the temple." Uzziah had reigned fifty-two years. His death meant uncertainty, transition, and fear. Isaiah's comfort — and ours — is that the throne of heaven was not vacant for a single day. Above the throne stand seraphim, burning ones, each with six wings: with two they cover their faces, with two their feet, and with two they fly. Even sinless angels veil themselves before this holiness. And they call to one another: "Holy, holy, holy is the LORD of hosts; the whole earth is full of his glory!" The Hebrew word is *qadosh* — set apart, utterly other. Said three times, it is holiness raised to the highest power. God's holiness is not one attribute among others; it is the blaze in which all the others burn. The foundations shake, the house fills with smoke, and Isaiah is about to find out what happens when a sinful man gets a clear view of a holy God.

**Woe is me (6:5–7).** Isaiah does not applaud. He collapses: "Woe is me! For I am lost; for I am a man of unclean lips, and I dwell in the midst of a people of unclean lips; for my eyes have seen the King, the LORD of hosts!" Notice: the prophet's crisis is not about his circumstances but about his lips — his words, his speech, the very tool of his ministry. A true sight of God always exposes us at the point of our pride. And God does not leave him in the collapse. A seraph flies to him with a burning coal taken with tongs from the altar, touches his mouth, and says: "Behold, this has touched your lips; your guilt is taken away, and your sin atoned for." The cleansing comes from the altar — from the place of sacrifice — and it comes to the sinner; the sinner does not climb to it. Guilt taken away, sin atoned for: that is the whole gospel in one verse, and it happens before Isaiah is asked to do anything at all.

**Here am I, send me (6:8–13).** Only now comes the call: "Whom shall I send, and who will go for us?" And the cleansed man volunteers: "Here am I! Send me." Grace precedes service — always. Then the commission itself is sobering: preach to a people who will hear and not understand, see and not perceive, until cities lie waste and the land is desolate. Isaiah asks the question every faithful preacher eventually asks: "How long, O Lord?" The answer: until the judgment has run its course. And yet the final word is not ash but seed: "The holy seed is its stump." The tree will be felled to a stump — but the stump is not dead, and the seed is holy. Out of that stump, in God's time, comes the Branch of chapter 11 and the child of chapter 9. God always keeps a stump.

### Key verses

> "And one called to another and said: 'Holy, holy, holy is the LORD of hosts; the whole earth is full of his glory!'" — Isaiah 6:3 (ESV)

> "And I said: 'Woe is me! For I am lost; for I am a man of unclean lips, and I dwell in the midst of a people of unclean lips; for my eyes have seen the King, the LORD of hosts!'" — Isaiah 6:5 (ESV)

> "And he touched my mouth and said: 'Behold, this has touched your lips; your guilt is taken away, and your sin atoned for.'" — Isaiah 6:7 (ESV)

> "And I heard the voice of the Lord saying, 'Whom shall I send, and who will go for us?' Then I said, 'Here am I! Send me!'" — Isaiah 6:8 (ESV)

### The bad news and the good news

The bad news of Isaiah 6 is that a real sight of God's holiness undoes us — "Woe is me" is the only honest first response of a sinner before a holy God, and no one volunteers for God's work until he has first collapsed under God's holiness. The good news is that the same God who undoes also cleanses: from His own altar He sends the coal, guilt is taken away, sin is atoned for, and the undone man is made the sent man. The altar that cleansed Isaiah's lips is a shadow of the cross, where the cleansing of sinners was finished once for all — and everyone who has been undone and cleansed there can say, "Here am I! Send me."

**A note from the Reformers.** Luther said that Isaiah 6 teaches every preacher his order of business: first be terrified, then be comforted, then be sent — and he warned that a man who has never said "Woe is me" has nothing to say to anybody else. Calvin likewise saw in the coal from the altar a picture of cleansing applied by God alone, received by faith alone.

**Reflection question:** When did you last say "Woe is me" before God — not about your circumstances, but about your sin? And if He has cleansed you, what is keeping you from saying "Here am I! Send me"?

---
## Isaiah 7 — Immanuel: The Sign God Gives to a Frightened King

### Jesus in this chapter

This chapter contains the most famous birth announcement in the Old Testament: "Behold, the virgin shall conceive and bear a son, and shall call his name Immanuel." Matthew quotes it directly of Jesus' birth (Matthew 1:23) — God with us. But notice the setting. The sign is not given to a saint in a sanctuary; it is given to Ahaz, a cowardly, unbelieving king who is about to refuse it. Jesus is introduced to us in Isaiah as God's sign to people who would not ask for one — grace offered to a man already planning to save himself another way. Immanuel means God refuses to be God at a distance. In Christ, He came near — nearer than Ahaz wanted, and nearer than we dared hope.

### Commentary

**Hearts shaking like trees (7:1–9).** Syria and the northern kingdom of Israel have allied against Judah, and Jerusalem panics: the heart of Ahaz and the heart of his people "shook as the trees of the forest shake before the wind." Isaiah meets the king at the conduit of the upper pool — at the water supply, where a frightened king is inspecting his defenses — and brings him a word from God: "Be careful, be quiet, do not fear, and do not let your heart be faint because of these two smoldering stumps of firebrands." Two kings with armies are, in God's sight, two burnt-out stubs. The plot to set up a puppet king will fail: "It shall not stand, and it shall not come to pass." Then comes the line the whole chapter hangs on, and in Hebrew it is a wordplay: *im lo ta'aminu, ki lo te'amenu* — "If you are not firm in faith, you will not be firm at all" (7:9). Faith is not decoration on stability; it is the only stability there is. A king — or a recovering man, or a frightened church — stands by believing, or does not stand at all.

**The sign refused (7:10–13).** God then does something extraordinary: He invites the unbelieving king to ask for a sign, "deep as Sheol or high as heaven." Ahaz refuses, hiding behind piety: "I will not ask, and I will not put the LORD to the test." It sounds humble. It is not. Ahaz has already decided to buy Assyria's help instead of trusting God's promise; his refusal to ask is unbelief dressed in religious language. Isaiah sees through it: "Is it too little for you to weary men, that you weary my God also?" There is a kind of "humility" that is really just a closed door. God offers; man declines politely; and heaven calls it wearisome.

**Immanuel (7:14–16).** So God gives the sign anyway — not to Ahaz's faith but to the house of David, and to us: "Therefore the Lord himself will give you a sign. Behold, the virgin shall conceive and bear a son, and shall call his name Immanuel." The Hebrew word is *almah*, a young woman of marriageable age, and Matthew, guided by the Spirit, reads it with the Greek *parthenos* — virgin — and applies it to Mary. The child will eat curds and honey — the plain food of a land under judgment — and before He reaches the age of knowing how to refuse evil and choose good, the two kings Ahaz fears will be gone. The sign has a near horizon (the threat will pass within a few years) and a far horizon (the Child is God with us). That double horizon is how Isaiah's prophecy often works: a promise for the present crisis that opens out into the promise of the ages.

**The razor of Assyria (7:17–25).** The chapter ends darkly, because Ahaz's chosen savior will become Judah's scourge. The king of Assyria whom Ahaz is courting will come like a hired razor and shave the land bare — head, beard, and body, total humiliation. In that day a man will keep alive a young cow and two sheep and eat curds and honey in a land of briers and thorns, where vineyards worth a thousand pieces of silver become wasteland. This is the harvest of self-salvation: the help you buy when you will not trust God becomes the chain you wear. Isaiah holds both truths at once — Immanuel is coming, and so is the razor. God with us, and judgment on unbelief. The gospel does not cancel the warning; it outlasts it.

### Key verses

> "Be careful, be quiet, do not fear, and do not let your heart be faint because of these two smoldering stumps of firebrands, at the fierce anger of Rezin and Syria and the son of Remaliah." — Isaiah 7:4 (ESV)

> "If you are not firm in faith, you will not be firm at all." — Isaiah 7:9 (ESV)

> "Therefore the Lord himself will give you a sign. Behold, the virgin shall conceive and bear a son, and shall call his name Immanuel." — Isaiah 7:14 (ESV)

### The bad news and the good news

The bad news of Isaiah 7 is that unbelief dressed up as humility still brings the razor — when we refuse God's way of standing, we end up shaved bare by the very helps we bought instead. The good news is that God gives His sign anyway: to a house that would not ask, He promised Immanuel, God with us, and in the fullness of time the virgin's Son was born. You do not stand by your grip on God. The promise stands because God gave it — and He is with us.

**A note from the Reformers.** Calvin marveled at this chapter that God offers His sign to a hypocrite — proving that the promise rests on God's goodness, not on the worthiness of the hearer. That is why the same promise can be preached today to people who feel as far from God as Ahaz was.

**Reflection question:** Where are you shaking like a tree in the wind right now — and are you, like Ahaz, quietly planning to save yourself instead of standing firm in faith? What would standing look like?

---

## Isaiah 8 — Maher-shalal-hash-baz: Fear God, Not the Conspiracy

### Jesus in this chapter

Jesus is the sanctuary and the stone in this chapter. Isaiah says the LORD of hosts "will become a sanctuary and a stone of offense and a rock of stumbling to both houses of Israel, a trap and a snare to the inhabitants of Jerusalem." Peter quotes those very words of Jesus: "A stone of stumbling, and a rock of offense" (1 Peter 2:8), and Paul quotes them too (Romans 9:33). The same Christ is either your sanctuary or your stumbling stone — there is no third option and no neutral ground. And tucked into the chapter is Isaiah's testimony that his children are signs — a pattern Hebrews 11 picks up and puts on Jesus' own lips: "Behold, I and the children God has given me" (Hebrews 2:13; Isaiah 8:18).

### Commentary

**A name written large (8:1–4).** God tells Isaiah to take a large tablet and write on it in common characters: *Maher-shalal-hash-baz* — "The spoil speeds, the prey hastens." It is the name God gives Isaiah's son, and it is a sermon in a birth announcement: before the child can cry "My father" or "My mother," the wealth of Damascus and the spoil of Samaria will be carried away by the king of Assyria. God's judgments are not vague moods; He writes them in letters big enough for everybody to read and dates them by a baby's first words. The two kings Ahaz feared would be gone almost before the boy could talk.

**The waters refused and the waters rising (8:5–8).** Here is the chapter's great image. "Because this people has refused the waters of Shiloah that flow gently" — the quiet stream that fed Jerusalem from the Gihon spring — "therefore, behold, the Lord is bringing up against them the waters of the River, mighty and many, the king of Assyria." They refused the gentle water, so they get the flood. Shiloah's stream is a picture of God's quiet, sufficient provision — His promise, His presence, His way.
