The Euphrates flood is the picture of the violent help men choose instead. The flood rises "up to the neck" — and then, astonishingly, the prophecy turns: the flood's outspread wings "will fill the breadth of your land, O Immanuel." Even in judgment the land is still called Immanuel's. The child of chapter 7 owns the country the flood is drowning. God with us is not a slogan for good times; it is the name of the land in the middle of the flood.

**Fear Him, not them (8:9–15).** The nations rage and are shattered; the counselors counsel and it comes to nothing, "for God is with us." Then God instructs His prophet with a strong hand: do not call conspiracy all that this people calls conspiracy, and do not fear what they fear. "But the LORD of hosts, him you shall honor as holy. Let him be your fear, and let him be your dread." Fear is not optional in a human life; the only question is its object. Fear man, and you will be snared. Fear the LORD, and He becomes your sanctuary — the same God who is a stumbling stone to the proud is a holy shelter to those who tremble at His word. Many "will stumble, and fall, and be broken, and be snared, and be taken." The stone does not move. We either fall on it in repentance or it falls on our pride.

**Bind up the testimony (8:16–22).** The chapter closes with Isaiah gathering his disciples: "Bind up the testimony; seal the teaching among my disciples. I will wait for the LORD... I will hope in him." When the nation will not hear, the prophet preserves the word for a remnant and waits. And his rule for the waiting is the church's rule in every dark age: "To the teaching and to the testimony! If they will not speak according to this word, it is because they have no dawn." Against mediums and necromancers and every whispering alternative, Isaiah sets the written word. The chapter ends with the people who refused the word cursing their king and their God, looking upward and downward and finding only "distress and darkness, the gloom of anguish" — the exact darkness chapter 9 is about to answer with a great light.

### Key verses

> "Because this people has refused the waters of Shiloah that flow gently, and rejoice over Rezin and the son of Remaliah, therefore, behold, the Lord is bringing up against them the waters of the River, mighty and many, the king of Assyria and all his glory." — Isaiah 8:6–7 (ESV)

> "But the LORD of hosts, him you shall honor as holy. Let him be your fear, and let him be your dread. And he will become a sanctuary and a stone of offense and a rock of stumbling to both houses of Israel, a trap and a snare to the inhabitants of Jerusalem." — Isaiah 8:13–14 (ESV)

> "To the teaching and to the testimony! If they will not speak according to this word, it is because they have no dawn." — Isaiah 8:20 (ESV)

### The bad news and the good news

The bad news of Isaiah 8 is that refusing God's gentle waters means getting the flood — and that Christ Himself, the stone God laid, will be a stumbling stone to everyone who will not have Him as sanctuary. The good news is that the flood does not own the land; Immanuel does. To those who fear the LORD instead of the conspiracy, the same God becomes a sanctuary, and the sealed testimony is preserved for a waiting remnant — and the dawn that chapter 8 says the wordless lack is about to break in chapter 9.

**A note from the Reformers.** "To the teaching and to the testimony" was a Reformers' text before the Reformation had a name — the standing rule that every spirit, every tradition, and every new whisper is tested by the written Word. Luther's stand at Worms was Isaiah 8:20 with a German accent.

**Reflection question:** What are you fearing right now that "this people" fears — and what would change if the LORD of hosts, and not that thing, became your fear and your dread?

---

## Isaiah 9 — Unto Us a Child Is Born: The Great Light and the Prince of Peace

### Jesus in this chapter

This is the chapter Handel could not leave alone, and neither can the church. Jesus is the great light shining in the darkness of Galilee — Matthew quotes Isaiah 9:1–2 over the start of Jesus' ministry in Capernaum (Matthew 4:14–16) — and He is the Child born and the Son given whose names are heaven's résumé: Wonderful Counselor, Mighty God, Everlasting Father, Prince of Peace. Every throne men build wobbles; His government increases forever, "with justice and with righteousness," upheld not by our effort but by "the zeal of the LORD of hosts." If you only memorize one chapter of Isaiah's first half, memorize this one. It is Christmas and the kingdom in nine verses.

### Commentary

**Light in Galilee of the nations (9:1–2).** "But there will be no gloom for her who was in anguish." The darkness of chapter 8's ending breaks here. The land of Zebulun and Naphtali — the northern tribes first humbled, first invaded, first carried off — will be the first made glorious. God's geography of grace often works that way: the place of deepest shame becomes the place of earliest glory. "The people who walked in darkness have seen a great light; those who dwelt in a land of deep darkness, on them has light shone." Not a small light, and not a light they kindled. The light shone *on* them. Matthew saw this fulfilled when Jesus left Nazareth and lived in Capernaum by the sea — the light physically arrived in the darkest district of Israel. People who have dwelt in deep darkness — addiction's darkness, depression's darkness, the darkness of a life that fell apart — should underline this verse. The light does not wait for the darkness to lift. It shines into it.

**Joy like harvest, yoke broken (9:3–5).** The light brings joy — joy like harvest-time, like soldiers dividing spoil — and it brings liberation: "For the yoke of his burden, and the staff for his shoulder, the rod of his oppressor, you have broken as on the day of Midian." Midian is Gideon's battle, where God won with three hundred men and trumpets, so no one could mistake whose victory it was. Every boot of the tramping warrior and every garment rolled in blood will be burned as fuel for the fire. War's equipment becomes firewood. That is what this King does with the instruments of oppression: He doesn't redeploy them. He burns them.

**The Child and His names (9:6–7).** Then the center of the chapter, and one of the highest peaks in the Old Testament: "For to us a child is born, to us a son is given; and the government shall be upon his shoulder, and his name shall be called Wonderful Counselor, Mighty God, Everlasting Father, Prince of Peace." Notice the two verbs: a child is *born* — truly human, born of a woman; a son is *given* — He existed before He was born, and He is the Father's gift. "To us" — the gift is addressed to the very people who walked in darkness. His names are not decorations. Wonderful Counselor: He needs no advisor, and His counsel never fails — bring Him the decision you cannot make. Mighty God: He is not a near-god or a junior partner; the Child is God Almighty, able to save to the uttermost. Everlasting Father: He fatherly cares for His people with a love that does not expire — the Hebrew *avi-ad* means "Father of eternity," a King whose fatherly reign has no term limit. Prince of Peace: *sar-shalom* — the ruler whose reign is wholeness, the end of the war between God and man and the beginning of peace in the human heart. And verse 7: "Of the increase of his government and of peace there will be no end, on the throne of David and over his kingdom, to establish it and to uphold it with justice and with righteousness from this time forth and forevermore. The zeal of the LORD of hosts will do this." The guarantee is not our zeal. It is His.

**The outstretched hand against pride (9:8–21).** The chapter's second half turns hard: judgment on the northern kingdom's arrogance — "The bricks have fallen, but we will build with dressed stones" — the defiant rebuild-it-bigger speech of a people who will not learn. Four times the refrain falls: "For all this his anger has not turned away, and his hand is stretched out still." Leaders who lead astray, prophets who prophesy lies, and a society devouring itself like fire in thorns — "each eats the flesh of his own arm," Manasseh devouring Ephraim and both against Judah. The contrast is the sermon: verses 6–7 promise a government of endless peace; verses 8–21 show the government of man eating itself. Choose your king.

### Key verses

> "The people who walked in darkness have seen a great light; those who dwelt in a land of deep darkness, on them has light shone." — Isaiah 9:2 (ESV)

> "For to us a child is born, to us a son is given; and the government shall be upon his shoulder, and his name shall be called Wonderful Counselor, Mighty God, Everlasting Father, Prince of Peace." — Isaiah 9:6 (ESV)

> "Of the increase of his government and of peace there will be no end, on the throne of David and over his kingdom, to establish it and to uphold it with justice and with righteousness from this time forth and forevermore. The zeal of the LORD of hosts will do this." — Isaiah 9:7 (ESV)

### The bad news and the good news

The bad news of Isaiah 9 is that man's government devours itself — pride rebuilds with dressed stones, leaders lead astray, and God's hand stays stretched out against a people who will not turn. The good news is that into the darkest district, to the people who walked in darkness, God gave a Son whose government and peace will never end — and His zeal, not ours, guarantees it. The light has shone. The Child has come. The throne is occupied forever.

**A note from the Reformers.** Spurgeon called Isaiah 9:6 "a great arsenal of truth" and preached each of the Child's names as a separate sermon of comfort — his point being that the names are not poetry but promises, each one a door into some need of the believer. Bring your need to the matching name.

**Reflection question:** Which of the Child's four names does your life most need right now — Wonderful Counselor, Mighty God, Everlasting Father, or Prince of Peace — and what would it mean to let Him be that to you this week?

---

## Isaiah 10 — The Rod of God's Anger, and the Remnant That Returns

### Jesus in this chapter

Jesus stands behind this chapter as the sovereign Lord who wields empires like tools and then judges the tools for their pride. Assyria is "the rod of my anger," yet Assyria will be punished for the arrogance of its heart — which means God rules history at a depth no tyrant can reach. And at the chapter's end, the camera zooms to a stump again: the forest of Assyria's glory felled, and in the next breath (chapter 11) a shoot from the stump of Jesse. Christ is that shoot. He is also the One in whom the remnant's return finds its meaning: "a remnant will return, the remnant of Jacob, to the mighty God" (10:21) — and the Mighty God they return to is the Child of Isaiah 9:6, who is Jesus.

### Commentary

**Woe to the lawmakers of injustice (10:1–4).** The chapter begins at home: "Woe to those who decree iniquitous decrees... to turn aside the needy from justice and to rob the poor of my people of their right, that widows may be their spoil." Legality is no defense when the law itself is rigged. Isaiah asks the question that haunts every comfortable sinner: "What will you do on the day of punishment, in the ruin that will come from afar? To whom will you flee for help, and where will you leave your wealth?" Every man needs an answer to that question before the day arrives.

**Assyria, the rod — and the rod judged (10:5–19).** Then the famous turn: "Ah, Assyria, the rod of my anger; the staff in their hands is my fury!" God sends Assyria against "a godless nation" — His own people — as an instrument of discipline. But the instrument has its own ideas: "By the strength of my hand I have done it, and by my wisdom, for I have understanding." God answers the arrogance with a parable of tools: "Shall the axe boast over him who hews with it, or the saw magnify itself against him who wields it?" An axe that boasts is a fool. God uses nations without endorsing them, and He holds the tool accountable for the pride in its swing. The end of the passage is a forest fire: the Light of Israel becomes a flame, and the glory of Assyria's forest is consumed "in a single day" — as indeed happened when the angel of the LORD struck down 185,000 in one night (Isaiah 37:36). Empires that look eternal on Monday can be ash by Friday when God has finished with them.

**The remnant returns (10:20–27).** In that day, the remnant of Israel "will lean on the LORD, the Holy One of Israel, in truth" — no longer leaning on the empire that struck them or the alliances that failed them. "A remnant will return... to the mighty God. For though your people Israel be as the sand of the sea, only a remnant of them will return." Paul quotes this in Romans 9:27–28 as God's pattern in every age: He saves through a remnant, by grace, not by majority. And to the remnant God speaks the tender word: "O my people, who dwell in Zion, do not be afraid of the Assyrians when they strike with the rod... For in a very little while my fury will come to an end." The yoke will be broken "because of the fatness" — the Hebrew suggests the anointing, the oil. Anointed necks break yokes.

**The advance and the felling (10:28–34).** The chapter closes with Assyria's march described town by town, closer and closer to Jerusalem, shaking its fist at the mount of the daughter of Zion. And then: "Behold, the Lord GOD of hosts will lop the boughs with terrifying power; the great in height will be hewn down, and the lofty will be brought low. He will cut down the thickets of the forest with an axe, and Lebanon will fall by the Majestic One." The axe of verse 15 ends in God's own hand, and the forest that falls is Assyria. But a felled forest in Isaiah always makes us ask about stumps — and chapter 11 opens with one.

### Key verses

> "Shall the axe boast over him who hews with it, or the saw magnify itself against him who wields it? As if a rod should wield him who lifts it, or as if a staff should lift him who is not wood!" — Isaiah 10:15 (ESV)

> "In that day the remnant of Israel and the survivors of the house of Jacob will no more lean on him who struck them, but will lean on the LORD, the Holy One of Israel, in truth. A remnant will return, the remnant of Jacob, to the mighty God." — Isaiah 10:20–21 (ESV)

> "Therefore thus says the Lord GOD of hosts: 'O my people, who dwell in Zion, do not be afraid of the Assyrians when they strike with the rod and lift up their staff against you as the Egyptians did. For in a very little while my fury will come to an end, and my anger will be directed to their destruction.'" — Isaiah 10:24–25 (ESV)

### The bad news and the good news

The bad news of Isaiah 10 is that God really does use hard things — even hostile powers — as rods, and that He judges the proud tools as surely as the people they struck; no one in the chapter is safe in their own strength. The good news is that the rod has a time limit ("in a very little while"), the axe never boasts for long, and a remnant always returns to the mighty God. If you feel like a remnant of one — what's left of a life the rod has struck — this chapter says the remnant is exactly who God saves.

**A note from the Reformers.** Calvin drew from this chapter his settled doctrine that God governs even the wicked actions of men without being the author of their wickedness — Assyria means it for conquest, God means it for discipline, and both intentions come to pass, with God righteous in His and Assyria guilty in hers. That distinction has comforted believers in every century of tyrants since.

**Reflection question:** What "rod" has struck your life — and have you been leaning on the thing that struck you, or on the LORD who limited it? What would leaning on Him "in truth" look like today?

---

## Isaiah 11 — The Shoot from the Stump of Jesse

### Jesus in this chapter

If Isaiah 9 is the promise of the Child, Isaiah 11 is the portrait of the King. Jesus is the shoot from the stump of Jesse and the Branch from his roots — the new growth on a dynasty that looked dead. The Spirit rests on Him in sevenfold fullness; His delight is in the fear of the LORD; He judges the poor with righteousness and strikes the earth with the rod of His mouth. Paul quotes this chapter of Jesus directly: "The root of Jesse will come, even he who arises to rule the Gentiles; in him will the Gentiles hope" (Romans 15:12). And the peaceable kingdom of verses 6–9 — wolf with lamb, child at the adder's den — is a preview of the world His reign is bringing, where "the earth shall be full of the knowledge of the LORD as the waters cover the sea."

### Commentary

**The shoot and the Spirit (11:1–5).** "There shall come forth a shoot from the stump of Jesse, and a branch from his roots shall bear fruit." Jesse, not David — Isaiah reaches behind the famous king to the obscure father, because by Isaiah's later horizon the royal tree of David has been felled to a stump: exiled, throne-less, forgotten. Out of that stump, a shoot. The Hebrew for branch is *netser* — the same root Matthew plays on with Nazareth. On this shoot rests "the Spirit of the LORD... the Spirit of wisdom and understanding, the Spirit of counsel and might, the Spirit of knowledge and the fear of the LORD" — a sevenfold Spirit for a complete King. His delight shall be in the fear of the LORD; He does not judge by what His eyes see or decide by what His ears hear — no spin, no surface, no bribe moves Him — "but with righteousness he shall judge the poor, and decide with equity for the meek of the earth; and he shall strike the earth with the rod of his mouth, and with the breath of his lips he shall kill the wicked." His word is His weapon. Jesus fulfilled this at His baptism, when the Spirit descended and remained on Him (John 1:32–34), and He will fulfill it finally when He returns with a sharp sword from His mouth (Revelation 19:15). And His uniform? "Righteousness shall be the belt of his waist, and faithfulness the belt of his loins." The King's character is His clothing.

**The peaceable kingdom (11:6–9).** Then the vision that has haunted and comforted the church ever since: "The wolf shall dwell with the lamb, and the leopard shall lie down with the young goat, and the calf and the lion and the fattened calf together; and a little child shall lead them." Predator and prey reconciled; the cow and the bear grazing together; the nursing child playing over the hole of the asp without a mother's terror. "They shall not hurt or destroy in all my holy mountain, for the earth shall be full of the knowledge of the LORD as the waters cover the sea." This is Eden restored and surpassed — creation itself healed of the curse. We taste it now wherever Christ reconciles enemies and makes violent men gentle; we will see it whole when He returns. A man in recovery knows this picture personally: the wolf in him lying down with the lamb is not fantasy. It is the King's specialty.

**The root and the second exodus (11:10–16).** "In that day the root of Jesse, who shall stand as a signal for the peoples — of him shall the nations inquire, and his resting place shall be glorious." The root becomes a banner. And God promises a second exodus greater than the first: He will recover the remnant "a second time" from Assyria, Egypt, Cush, Elam, Shinar, Hamath, and the coastlands — from everywhere His people have been scattered. He will dry up the Euphrates with His scorching wind, make the deep a highway, and gather the outcasts. The God who made a road through the sea for slaves will make a road through history for exiles. There is no place so far that His recovering hand cannot reach — a verse every prodigal and every family praying for one should hold onto.

### Key verses

> "There shall come forth a shoot from the stump of Jesse, and a branch from his roots shall bear fruit. And the Spirit of the LORD shall rest upon him, the Spirit of wisdom and understanding, the Spirit of counsel and might, the Spirit of knowledge and the fear of the LORD." — Isaiah 11:1–2 (ESV)

> "The wolf shall dwell with the lamb, and the leopard shall lie down with the young goat, and the calf and the lion and the fattened calf together; and a little child shall lead them." — Isaiah 11:6 (ESV)

> "They shall not hurt or destroy in all my holy mountain, for the earth shall be full of the knowledge of the LORD as the waters cover the sea." — Isaiah 11:9 (ESV)

> "In that day the root of Jesse, who shall stand as a signal for the peoples — of him shall the nations inquire, and his resting place shall be glorious." — Isaiah 11:10 (ESV)

### The bad news and the good news

The bad news of Isaiah 11 is that by nature the royal tree is a stump and the world is a place of wolves and asps — left to ourselves, the strong devour the weak and even children are not safe. The good news is that God grows Kings out of stumps: the Spirit-anointed shoot of Jesse has come, He judges the poor with righteousness, He is gathering a remnant from every coastland, and His endgame is a world where nothing hurts or destroys. If your life looks like a stump, you are exactly the kind of ground this King grows from.

**A note from the Reformers.** Luther loved the stump: he said the church and the Christian life alike are always a felled tree to human eyes, and God's whole way is to make life come out of what men have written off as dead. The shoot from Jesse's stump was his proof that God specializes in hopeless stock.

**Reflection question:** Where does your life or family look like a stump — cut down, written off? What would it mean to believe a shoot can come from exactly there?

---

## Isaiah 12 — With Joy You Will Draw Water: The Song of the Saved

### Jesus in this chapter

This short chapter is the first song of the redeemed in Isaiah, and its center is a name: "Behold, God is my salvation; I will trust, and will not be afraid; for the LORD GOD is my strength and my song, and he has become my salvation." The Hebrew behind "salvation" is *yeshuah* — the very name given to Mary's son: "You shall call his name Jesus, for he will save his people from their sins" (Matthew 1:21). Jesus' name is Isaiah 12:2 in a word. And His promise in John 7 echoes this chapter's well: standing at the Feast of Booths — where water was drawn and poured out in memory of God's provision — Jesus cried, "If anyone thirsts, let him come to me and drink" (John 7:37). The wells of salvation have a name and a face.

### Commentary

**Anger turned away (12:1).** "You will say in that day: 'I will give thanks to you, O LORD, for though you were angry with me, your anger turned away, that you might comfort me.'" This is the testimony of everyone God has saved: not "You were never angry" — sin is real, and God's anger against it is real — but "Your anger turned away." The question every sinner must ask is *how* it turned away. Isaiah's whole book answers: it turned away onto the Servant. The comfort of verse 1 is purchased by the suffering of chapter 53. When a believer gives thanks, he is not thanking God for overlooking sin; he is thanking God for a wrath that was borne by Another and is therefore gone.

**Strength, song, and salvation (12:2–3).** Verse 2 quotes the song of Moses at the Red Sea (Exodus 15:2) — deliberately. The God who drowned Pharaoh's army is the God who saves Isaiah's remnant; the second exodus will be like the first, only greater. "The LORD GOD is my strength and my song" — when God saves a man, He does not just give him strength; He becomes his strength, and He becomes his song, the thing his mouth is full of. Then verse 3, the picture of the whole Christian life: "With joy you will draw water from the wells of salvation." Wells, plural — deep, plural, and never dry. Salvation in Isaiah is not a one-time sip but a well you return to daily, drawing with joy. Thirsty people, recovering people, weary people: there is a well, and the bucket is joy.

**Tell the nations (12:4–6).** The song turns outward: "Give thanks to the LORD, call upon his name, make known his deeds among the peoples, proclaim that his name is exalted." Saved people become telling people. The chapter ends with Zion shouting: "Shout, and sing for joy, O inhabitant of Zion, for great in your midst is the Holy One of Israel." *Great in your midst* — not far off, not abstract. The Holy One has moved into the neighborhood. That is Immanuel again, now with singing.

### Key verses

> "I will give thanks to you, O LORD, for though you were angry with me, your anger turned away, that you might comfort me." — Isaiah 12:1 (ESV)

> "Behold, God is my salvation; I will trust, and will not be afraid; for the LORD GOD is my strength and my song, and he has become my salvation." — Isaiah 12:2 (ESV)

> "With joy you will draw water from the wells of salvation." — Isaiah 12:3 (ESV)

> "Shout, and sing for joy, O inhabitant of Zion, for great in your midst is the Holy One of Israel." — Isaiah 12:6 (ESV)

### The bad news and the good news

The bad news of Isaiah 12 is that God's anger against our sin was real — the song only makes sense if the wrath was deserved and deadly. The good news is that His anger has turned away, turned onto the Servant, and what remains for His people is comfort, strength, a song, and wells that never run dry. If you are thirsty today, you do not need a new well. You need to draw, with joy, from the one already dug.

**A note from the Reformers.** Spurgeon preached "With joy you will draw water" as the believer's daily portion — salvation is not a cistern we fill once but a well we draw from continually, and the drawing itself is done with joy, not dread. The Christian life is bucket-work at a full well.

**Reflection question:** When did God's anger turning away become real to you — and are you still drawing daily from the wells of salvation, or trying to live on an old drink?

---
## Isaiah 13 — The Day of the LORD Against Babylon: The First Oracle

### Jesus in this chapter

Jesus stands in this chapter as the Judge of the nations — the LORD of hosts mustering an army against Babylon, the city that stands in Scripture for man's organized defiance of God from Babel to Revelation. When Jesus describes His own return, He borrows this chapter's sky: "the stars of heaven will fall" language of Matthew 24 grows out of Isaiah 13's darkened sun and shaken heavens. Babylon fell in 539 BC to the Medes, and it will fall finally when Christ returns and the angel cries, "Fallen, fallen is Babylon the great" (Revelation 18:2). Every earthly empire that sets itself against God is living on borrowed time, and the One who keeps the calendar is Jesus.

### Commentary

**The banner on the bare hill (13:1–5).** The oracles against the nations begin with the biggest name first: "The oracle concerning Babylon which Isaiah the son of Amoz saw." Babylon in Isaiah's day was not yet the superpower it would become — Assyria was — which makes this prophecy a mark of the book's foresight: Isaiah names Babylon's rise and fall a century and a half early. God raises a signal on a bare mountain and calls His "consecrated ones" — the Medes and Persians, sanctified for this task without knowing it — and the sound is like a great multitude. "The LORD of hosts is mustering a host for battle." History's armies march in parades men plan, but they arrive on a schedule God sets.

**The day of the LORD (13:6–16).** "Wail, for the day of the LORD is near; as destruction from the Almighty it comes!" Here the phrase "day of the LORD" expands beyond Judah: it is the day God settles accounts with arrogant empire. Every heart melts, hands go feeble, the sun is darkened at its rising, the stars withhold their light — cosmic language for a world order collapsing. "I will punish the world for its evil, and the wicked for their iniquity; I will put an end to the pomp of the arrogant, and lay low the pompous pride of the ruthless." The specific sins named are the standing sins of empire: arrogance, ruthlessness, pride. And God's purpose clause is worth noticing: He will make man "more rare than fine gold" — judgment thins the ranks of the proud until humility is precious again.

**Babylon made like Sodom (13:17–22).** "Behold, I am stirring up the Medes against them, who have no regard for silver and do not delight in gold." Cyrus's coalition cannot be bought off — that is the terror of it. The glory of kingdoms becomes "as when God overthrew Sodom and Gomorrah": uninhabited forever, a haunt of wild animals and owls, never again pitched with an Arab's tent. Babylon, the most splendid city on earth, is today exactly that — ruins in the Iraqi desert. Isaiah's word outlasted the empire by two and a half millennia. When God says a proud thing will fall, bank on the falling, not the pride.

### Key verses

> "Wail, for the day of the LORD is near; as destruction from the Almighty it comes!" — Isaiah 13:6 (ESV)

> "I will punish the world for its evil, and the wicked for their iniquity; I will put an end to the pomp of the arrogant, and lay low the pompous pride of the ruthless." — Isaiah 13:11 (ESV)

> "And Babylon, the glory of kingdoms, the splendor and pomp of the Chaldeans, will be like Sodom and Gomorrah when God overthrew them." — Isaiah 13:19 (ESV)

### The bad news and the good news

The bad news of Isaiah 13 is that no empire, system, or personal kingdom built on arrogance is too big to fall — the day of the LORD reaches Babylon at the height of its glory. The good news is that the Judge of Babylon is not chaos but the LORD of hosts, who rules the rise and fall of nations with moral purpose — and who, in the same book, promises to have compassion on Jacob and gather strangers to His house. The fall of Babylon means the world is not run by the proud. It is run by God.

**A note from the Reformers.** Calvin taught from this chapter that providence is not a doctrine for quiet times only — God musters armies and topples capitals, and the believer reading the headlines of any century should read them the way Isaiah read Babylon: as sermons about who actually reigns.

**Reflection question:** What "Babylon" — a system, a habit, a personal kingdom of pride — have you been treating as permanent? What changes when you believe God has already scheduled its fall?

---

## Isaiah 14 — How the Oppressor Has Ceased: The King Who Fell from Heaven

### Jesus in this chapter

Jesus is the rest-giver of this chapter's opening and the true King set against Babylon's fallen tyrant. "The LORD will have compassion on Jacob... and they will take captive those who were their captors" — the great reversal that Christ completes when He leads captivity captive (Ephesians 4:8). Against the king of Babylon, who said in his heart, "I will ascend to heaven... I will make myself like the Most High," stands Jesus, who being in the form of God "emptied himself, by taking the form of a servant" (Philippians 2:6–7) — and was therefore exalted. Isaiah 14 and Philippians 2 are the two ladders of Scripture: one man climbs by pride and is thrown down to Sheol; the Son descends in humility and is raised above every name.

### Commentary

**Compassion and rest (14:1–3).** Before the taunt comes the promise: "For the LORD will have compassion on Jacob and will again choose Israel, and will set them in their own land, and sojourners will join them." God's judgments on the nations are never His last word to His people. "When the LORD has given you rest from your pain and turmoil and the hard service with which you were made to serve," then Israel takes up the taunt song. Rest first, song second. That order matters for worn-out people: God gives rest to the slaves before He asks them to sing about it.

**The taunt against the king (14:4–21).** "How the oppressor has ceased, the insolent fury ceased! The LORD has broken the staff of the wicked, the scepter of rulers." The whole earth is at rest and quiet; even the cypress trees rejoice over the tyrant's fall — no logger comes up against them now. Then Sheol itself stirs: the shades of dead kings rise from their thrones to meet the newcomer, and their welcome is acid: "You too have become as weak as we! You have become like us!" The pomp is brought down, the maggots are the bed, the worms the covering. And then the five "I wills" of the proudest heart in Scripture: "I will ascend to heaven; above the stars of God I will set my throne on high... I will ascend above the heights of the clouds; I will make myself like the Most High." The Hebrew word for the fallen one in verse 12 is *helel* — "shining one," rendered "Lucifer" in the Latin. The church has long read here both the historical king of Babylon and, behind him, the fall of Satan himself — the pattern of every rebellion since: created brightness grasping at the Creator's throne. The answer is one verse: "But you are brought down to Sheol, to the far reaches of the pit." Five "I wills" climb; one "but you are brought down" ends it. The onlookers can hardly believe it: "Is this the man who made the earth tremble, who shook kingdoms?" Tyrants shrink in the rear-view mirror of history. They were never as big as their fear made them.

**Assyria broken, Philistia warned (14:24–31).** God swears an oath: "As I have planned, so shall it be" — Assyria will be broken "in my land," its yoke removed from God's people. The purpose that "is purposed concerning the whole earth" cannot be thwarted: "who will annul it?" Then Philistia is warned not to rejoice that the rod that struck her is broken, "for from the serpent's root will come forth an adder." And tucked at the end is a word for the poorest: "The firstborn of the poor will graze, and the needy lie down in safety." Even in the oracles, God keeps mentioning the poor. He always does.

**The answer for the messengers (14:32).** The chapter ends with instruction for diplomats: "What will one answer the messengers of the nation? 'The LORD has founded Zion, and in her the afflicted of his people find refuge.'" That is the whole foreign policy of faith. Empires fall, tyrants rot, adders hatch — but Zion is founded by the LORD, and the afflicted find refuge in her. When the world's messengers ask what your hope is built on, that is the answer.

### Key verses

> "How you are fallen from heaven, O Day Star, son of Dawn! How you are cut down to the ground, you who laid the nations low! You said in your heart, 'I will ascend to heaven; above the stars of God I will set my throne on high; I will sit on the mount of assembly in the far reaches of the north.'" — Isaiah 14:12–13 (ESV)

> "But you are brought down to Sheol, to the far reaches of the pit." — Isaiah 14:15 (ESV)

> "What will one answer the messengers of the nation? 'The LORD has founded Zion, and in her the afflicted of his people find refuge.'" — Isaiah 14:32 (ESV)

### The bad news and the good news

The bad news of Isaiah 14 is that the five "I wills" of pride — in a Babylonian king, in Satan, or in our own hearts — always end the same way: brought down to the pit, smaller in death than they looked in life. The good news is that God has founded Zion as a refuge for the afflicted, and He sent a King who climbed down instead of up — who took the form of a servant and was exalted by the Father. The afflicted do not need to ascend. They need to take refuge, and the refuge is open.

**A note from the Reformers.** Luther read the five "I wills" as the anatomy of original sin itself — the creature's heart forever whispering "I will be like the Most High" — and set against them Christ's opposite descent in Philippians 2. Pride climbs and falls; humility descends and is raised. There is no third ladder.

**Reflection question:** Which of the five "I wills" sounds most like your own heart when you are honest — and what would it look like to take refuge in founded Zion instead of climbing?

---

## Isaiah 15 — The Weeping over Moab

### Jesus in this chapter

Jesus is the compassionate Judge in this chapter — the God who weeps over the very nation He judges. Moab is not Israel; Moab is the neighbor, the outsider, the descendant of Lot, sometimes enemy and sometimes kin (Ruth the Moabitess is in Jesus' own family line). And God's heart breaks over Moab's fall: "My heart cries out for Moab." Centuries later Jesus weeps over Jerusalem (Luke 19:41) and over the grave of Lazarus (John 11:35). The tears of Isaiah 15 teach us that divine judgment is never divine delight. The Judge of all the earth has a crying heart, and in Christ that heart would bleed as well as weep.

### Commentary

**Overnight ruin (15:1).** "An oracle concerning Moab. Because Ar of Moab is laid waste in a night, Moab is undone; because Kir of Moab is laid waste in a night, Moab is undone." Twice in one verse: in a night. Moab's security was the kind every nation trusts — strong cities, high places, wealth — and it evaporates between sundown and sunrise. Isaiah's oracles keep teaching the same arithmetic: whatever can be lost in a night was never a foundation.

**A nation in mourning (15:2–5).** The grief is total and public: every head shaved, every beard cut off, sackcloth in the streets, wailing on the housetops, soldiers crying aloud, "the heart of Moab cries out." Even the fleeing strong men weep as they run. And into this catalogue of Moabite sorrow comes the chapter's most startling line — God's own voice: "My heart cries out for Moab; her fugitives flee to Zoar." The prophet does not gloat, because his God does not gloat. Isaiah will say it again in chapter 16: "my eyes shed tears" over Moab. This is the pattern for how God's people should speak of judgment falling on anyone — even enemies, even the deserving. If our doctrine of judgment makes us smirk, we have learned it from someone other than Isaiah's God.

**Waters of blood and lions for the survivors (15:6–9).** The grass withers, the waters of Nimrim become desolate, the abundance is carried across the brook — a whole economy draining away. "For the waters of Dibon are full of blood; yet I will bring upon Dibon even more, a lion for those of Moab who escape." Even escape is not escape when God has decreed judgment. And yet — the next chapter opens with Moab told exactly where refuge can be found: send a lamb to the ruler of the land, to the mount of the daughter of Zion. Judgment and invitation sit side by side in Isaiah, even for Moab.

### Key verses

> "An oracle concerning Moab. Because Ar of Moab is laid waste in a night, Moab is undone; because Kir of Moab is laid waste in a night, Moab is undone." — Isaiah 15:1 (ESV)

> "My heart cries out for Moab; her fugitives flee to Zoar, to Eglath-shelishiyah. For at the ascent of Luhith they go up weeping; on the road to Horonaim they raise a cry of destruction." — Isaiah 15:5 (ESV)

> "For the waters of Dibon are full of blood; yet I will bring upon Dibon even more, a lion for those of Moab who escape, for the remnant of the land." — Isaiah 15:9 (ESV)

### The bad news and the good news

The bad news of Isaiah 15 is that security built on anything but God can be undone in a single night — cities, economies, and reputations included. The good news is that the God who decrees the judgment also cries over the judged: "My heart cries out for Moab." The heart that wept over Moab is the heart that wept over Jerusalem and went to a cross for outsiders — Moabites like Ruth, and sinners like us. Judgment is God's strange work. Tears are closer to His heart.

**A note from the Reformers.** Calvin insisted that the prophets' tears over judged nations teach ministers their manner: threaten boldly, but never cruelly — the man who announces judgment without grief has not stood close enough to the God who announces it with a crying heart.

**Reflection question:** When you think of people under judgment — enemies, outsiders, the far gone — do you gloat, shrug, or weep? What would it mean to have God's heart for one specific "Moab" in your life?

---

## Isaiah 16 — Send the Lamb: Refuge Offered, Pride Refused

### Jesus in this chapter

Jesus is the Lamb and the throne in this chapter. Moab is counseled to "send the lamb to the ruler of the land" — tribute, yes, but the language reaches further in a book where a Lamb is coming who takes away the sin of the world (John 1:29). And the chapter's center is a throne: "then a throne will be established in steadfast love, and on it will sit in faithfulness in the tent of David one who judges and seeks justice and is swift to do righteousness." That is a throne description no son of David met until Jesus — established not by conquest but by *hesed*, steadfast love, and occupied by a King swift to do righteousness. Moab is being told: shelter exists, under that throne, for fleeing people. All they have to do is come.

### Commentary

**The counsel: send the lamb, give shelter (16:1–5).** The oracle turns into advice — remarkable grace for a judged nation. Send the lamb to Zion. "Give counsel; grant justice; make your shade like night at the height of noon; shelter the outcasts; do not reveal the fugitive; let the outcasts of Moab sojourn among you; be a shelter to them from the destroyer." Whether these words are Isaiah counseling Judah to shelter Moabite refugees or Moab to seek Zion's protection, the direction is the same: in the day of ruin, the way of life is humility before God's throne and mercy toward the fleeing. Then the promise of the throne in verse 5 — steadfast love, faithfulness, justice, swiftness to righteousness. Every earthly throne in Isaiah's oracles is falling; this one is being established. The contrast is the gospel in seed form: man's thrones wobble on pride, God's throne stands on love.

**The refusal: pride (16:6–12).** But Moab will not come. "We have heard of the pride of Moab — how proud he is! — of his arrogance, his pride, and his insolence; in his idle boasting he is not right." Six words for pride pile up in one verse. Moab would rather wail over its ruined vines and cakes of raisins than bow to Zion's throne. So the wailing spreads from Heshbon to Sibmah, the fruitful fields languish, the shouts over the harvest are gone, "joy and gladness are taken away from the fruitful field." And Isaiah weeps again: "Therefore I weep with the weeping of Jazer... I drench you with my tears, O Heshbon." The prophet's bowels moan like a lyre for Moab. Then the saddest picture: Moab comes to his high place to pray, "but he will not prevail." Prayer to a powerless god at a powerless shrine — much prayer, no answer. Pride's final address is an empty altar.

**The dated sentence (16:13–14).** The oracle closes with a deadline: "Within three years, according to the years of a hired worker, the glory of Moab will be brought into contempt... and the remnant will be very small and feeble." God counts judgment in hired-worker years — exact, measured, not a day late. Pride had three years to change its mind. That is more mercy than Moab deserved and more warning than Moab used.

### Key verses

> "Send the lamb to the ruler of the land, from Sela, by way of the desert, to the mount of the daughter of Zion." — Isaiah 16:1 (ESV)

> "Then a throne will be established in steadfast love, and on it will sit in faithfulness in the tent of David one who judges and seeks justice and is swift to do righteousness." — Isaiah 16:5 (ESV)

> "We have heard of the pride of Moab — how proud he is! — of his arrogance, his pride, and his insolence; in his idle boasting he is not right." — Isaiah 16:6 (ESV)

### The bad news and the good news

The bad news of Isaiah 16 is that pride can stand three years from a deadline and still choose wailing over bowing — a man can drown in his own glory within sight of a throne of steadfast love. The good news is that the throne is real and it is established in steadfast love: Jesus sits on David's throne judging with justice and swift to do righteousness, and outcasts who come to Him find shelter from the destroyer. Moab's tragedy was never that refuge didn't exist. It was that pride wouldn't walk to it.

**A note from the Reformers.** Spurgeon seized on the throne "established in steadfast love" as a text for trembling sinners: the throne you must approach is not founded on your merit, your mood, or your record, but on *hesed* — covenant love that does not let go. That is why sinners may come boldly (Hebrews 4:16).

**Reflection question:** Is there a refuge you know God has opened that your pride keeps refusing to walk to — a confession, an apology, a surrender? What is the pride protecting, and what is it costing?

---

## Isaiah 17 — Damascus Falls, and Jacob's Glory Thins

### Jesus in this chapter

Jesus appears in this chapter as the God who is forgotten — and that is the indictment. "For you have forgotten the God of your salvation and have not remembered the Rock of your refuge." Israel's core sin is not exotic wickedness but amnesia: they forgot God. Jesus is that Rock of refuge (1 Corinthians 10:4), the God of our salvation, and the chapter is a standing warning that a people — or a person — can be busy, planted, and flourishing while having forgotten the only One who matters. Against the forgetting stands the chapter's end: in that day man will look to his Maker, and the altars his own hands made will be abandoned. Looking to the Maker instead of the made — that turn is the whole life of faith in Jesus.

### Commentary

**Damascus and Ephraim fall together (17:1–3).** The oracle joins Syria's capital and Israel's northern kingdom in one sentence of doom, because they joined themselves in one alliance: "Behold, Damascus will cease to be a city and will become a heap of ruins." And "the fortress will disappear from Ephraim, and the kingdom from Damascus." The alliance of Isaiah 7 — the two smoldering stumps that terrified Ahaz — is swept away in a paragraph. What fear magnifies, God miniaturizes. The glory of Jacob "will be made thin, and the fat of his flesh will grow lean," like a harvest field where the reaper gathers only scattered ears in the Valley of Rephaim. Two or three berries on the topmost branch, four or five on the fruit tree's boughs — that is the remnant arithmetic of judgment. God sifts, and what remains is little, but it is His.

**The turning of the eyes (17:7–8).** Judgment aims at a look: "In that day man will look to his Maker, and his eyes will look on the Holy One of Israel. He will not look to the altars, the work of his hands." The Hebrew verb for "look" here, *sha'ah*, means to gaze with regard, to look to someone for help. Man's eyes are always looking somewhere for rescue. Sin turns them to the work of our own hands — our altars, our Asherim, our incense stands, our self-built systems. Grace turns them back to the Maker. This is repentance described as a redirection of the gaze — which is exactly how the New Testament describes faith in Christ: "looking to Jesus, the founder and perfecter of our faith" (Hebrews 12:2).

**The forgotten Rock and the rushing nations (17:9–14).** Israel planted pleasant plants and set out slips of a foreign god — horticulture as idolatry, hoping for a harvest from a strange vine — "therefore... the harvest will flee away in a day of grief and incurable pain." You reap what you plant, and strange vines bear grief. Then the oracle widens to the nations themselves: "Ah, the thunder of many peoples; they thunder like the thundering of the sea!" But God rebukes them and they flee like chaff before the wind. "At evening time, behold, terror! Before morning, they are no more!" That evening-and-morning pattern is God's signature on proud armies — Sennacherib's host will learn it literally (Isaiah 37). The nations rush like water; the Rock does not notice the weather.

### Key verses

> "For you have forgotten the God of your salvation and have not remembered the Rock of your refuge; therefore, though you plant pleasant plants and sow the vine-branch of a stranger, though you make them grow on the day that you plant them... the harvest will flee away in a day of grief and incurable pain." — Isaiah 17:10–11 (ESV)

> "In that day man will look to his Maker, and his eyes will look on the Holy One of Israel." — Isaiah 17:7 (ESV)

> "At evening time, behold, terror! Before morning, they are no more! This is the portion of those who loot us, and the lot of those who plunder us." — Isaiah 17:14 (ESV)

### The bad news and the good news

The bad news of Isaiah 17 is that forgetting God is enough — a life can be busy, planted, and productive, and still be reaping incurable grief because it forgot the Rock of its refuge. The good news is that God uses the thinning to turn our eyes: when the altars of our own hands fail, man looks to his Maker again. Jesus is that Maker and that Rock, and no one who looks to Him — even from a field of scattered berries — looks in vain.

**A note from the Reformers.** Calvin noted that Isaiah calls forgetting, not atheism, the root sin: men rarely deny God outright; they simply stop remembering Him, and the altars of their own hands quietly take His place. The cure is the same in both Testaments — a turned gaze, eyes fixed on the Maker.

**Reflection question:** What altar "of your own hands" — a system, a habit, a self-rescue plan — have your eyes been looking to instead of your Maker? What would looking to Him mean, practically, today?

---

## Isaiah 18 — Cush: Tall Messengers and a Quiet God

### Jesus in this chapter

Jesus is the quietly watching King of this chapter — "I will look from my dwelling place... I will keep still and I will look" — and He is the destination of the chapter's last scene: gifts brought to Mount Zion by "a people tall and smooth, a people feared far and wide." The far-off nation of Cush, beyond the rivers of Ethiopia, will one day bring presents to the place of the Name of the LORD of hosts. That stream of distant peoples bringing gifts to Zion runs straight to the magi at Bethlehem, to the Ethiopian eunuch baptized on the Gaza road (Acts 8 — a treasurer of Cush's queen, carrying Isaiah home in his chariot), and to the multitude of Revelation 7 from every nation. The quiet God is not idle. He is waiting for the harvest, and the ends of the earth are in it.

### Commentary

**Ambassadors on the water (18:1–2).** "Ah, land of whirring wings that is beyond the rivers of Cush, which sends ambassadors by the sea, in vessels of papyrus on the waters!" Cush — the upper Nile, modern Sudan and Ethiopia — was a great power in Isaiah's century; a Cushite dynasty ruled Egypt itself. Its envoys came up to Jerusalem seeking an alliance against Assyria, swift messengers in reed boats. Isaiah addresses the whole world in response: "All you inhabitants of the world, you who dwell on the earth, when a signal is raised on the mountains, look! When a trumpet is blown, hear!" God's dealings with the nations are public. He raises His banner where everyone can see it — if they will look.

**The God who keeps still (18:3–6).** Then one of the most remarkable self-descriptions in the Bible: "For thus the LORD said to me: 'I will keep still and I will look from my dwelling place, like the clear heat in sunshine, like a cloud of dew in the heat of harvest.'" God compares His stillness to summer heat — invisible, unhurried, and doing more than any storm. Before the harvest, "when the blossom is over, and the flower becomes a ripening grape," He cuts off the sprigs with pruning hooks. The picture is of a Gardener who does not panic at the size of the vine. Assyria's growth looks unstoppable; God watches it the way a farmer watches grapes — knowing the exact day of the knife. The cut branches are left "to the birds of prey of the mountains and to the beasts of the earth" — summer and winter carrion. God's stillness is not indifference; it is the confidence of the only One who knows the schedule.

**Gifts to Zion (18:7).** The oracle ends not with Cush destroyed but with Cush arriving: "At that time tribute will be brought to the LORD of hosts from a people tall and smooth... to the place of the Name of the LORD of hosts, the mount Zion." The nation that sent ambassadors seeking a political deal ends by sending worshipers bearing gifts. That is Isaiah's vision of missions in one verse — the feared and far-off, brought near, not as corpses but as givers. The firstfruits appeared when a Cushite treasurer, reading Isaiah on his way home, believed and went on his way rejoicing.

### Key verses

> "For thus the LORD said to me: 'I will keep still and I will look from my dwelling place, like the clear heat in sunshine, like a cloud of dew in the heat of harvest.'" — Isaiah 18:4 (ESV)

> "At that time tribute will be brought to the LORD of hosts from a people tall and smooth, from a people feared near and far, a nation mighty and conquering, whose land the rivers divide — to the place of the Name of the LORD of hosts, the mount Zion." — Isaiah 18:7 (ESV)

### The bad news and the good news

The bad news of Isaiah 18 is that God's stillness can look like absence while proud vines are ripening — and nations (and men) mistake His patience for permission. The good news is that the quiet God is a Gardener with pruning hooks and a schedule, and His end for the far-off nations is not carrion but conversion: gifts at Zion, worshipers from beyond the rivers. If God seems still in your life, remember the heat in sunshine — unseen, and doing the ripening work nothing else can do.

**A note from the Reformers.** Spurgeon preached God's "stillness" here as a rebuke to our panic: the Lord is never in a hurry because He is never behind. Faith learns to work and wait in the heat of His quiet, rather than demanding He perform on our timetable.

**Reflection question:** Where does God's stillness feel like absence in your life right now — and what would change if you read it as the heat that ripens, and the patience of a God who has already set the day of the knife?

---

## Isaiah 19 — Egypt: The Idol-Maker Judged, the Enemy Blessed

### Jesus in this chapter

Jesus is the cloud-riding Lord of this chapter's opening — "Behold, the LORD is riding on a swift cloud and comes to Egypt" — the same cloud imagery He claims for His own coming (Matthew 24:30). But the astonishing place to find Him is at the chapter's end, in the future God swears by oath: "In that day there will be an altar to the LORD in the midst of the land of Egypt... When they cry to the LORD because of oppressors, he will send them a savior and defender, and deliver them... In that day Israel will be the third with Egypt and Assyria, a blessing in the midst of the earth, whom the LORD of hosts has blessed, saying, 'Blessed be Egypt my people, and Assyria the work of my hands, and Israel my inheritance.'" A Savior sent to Egypt; Egypt called "my people"; Assyria — the terror of the world — called "the work of my hands." That is the gospel of Jesus Christ, preached to the two superpowers, seven hundred years early.

### Commentary

**The idols tremble (19:1–4).** God arrives and "the idols of Egypt will tremble at his presence, and the heart of the Egyptians will melt within them." Egypt's religion was the oldest and most sophisticated on earth, and it melts in a verse. Then comes civil breakdown: "I will stir up Egyptians against Egyptians, and they will fight, each against another... city against city, kingdom against kingdom." And the spirit of Egypt drains away; its counsel is confounded; and the Egyptians — the world's experts in the occult — run to "the idols and the sorcerers, and the mediums and the necromancers." When a nation's wisdom fails, it does not become humble; it becomes superstitious. God gives them into the hand of "a hard master" — history gave them a succession of them.

**The Nile fails (19:5–15).** Egypt was its river. So the judgment strikes the river: waters drying, canals stinking, reeds withering, fishermen mourning, flax workers crushed, the whole economy unraveling from the water up. Then the counselors of Zoan and Memphis are shown to be fools — "How can you say to Pharaoh, 'I am a son of the wise, a son of ancient kings'?" Ancient pedigree is not wisdom. "The LORD has mingled within her a spirit of confusion" — the experts stagger like drunkards in their vomit, and there is no work Egypt can do, "either head or tail, palm branch or reed." A civilization can be brilliant for three thousand years and still end here, when God withdraws the wisdom He lent it.

**The altar in Egypt (19:16–25).** Then the oracle turns — and the turn is one of the most breathtaking in all prophecy. Egypt will tremble like women before the LORD, and five cities in Egypt will speak "the language of Canaan" and swear allegiance to the LORD of hosts. An altar will stand in Egypt's midst and a pillar at its border. "And it will be a sign and a witness to the LORD of hosts in the land of Egypt." When Egypt cries out under oppression, God "will send them a savior and defender, and deliver them. And the LORD will make himself known to the Egyptians... and they will know the LORD in that day and worship with sacrifice and offering." The Hebrew word for the savior is *moshia* — a deliverer, the same root as the judges' titles and cousin to *yeshuah*, salvation. Then the highway: "In that day there will be a highway from Egypt to Assyria, and Assyria will come into Egypt, and Egypt into Assyria, and the Egyptians will worship with the Assyrians." The two empires that crushed Israel between them, worshiping together, with Israel as the third partner in blessing. Paul's vision of Jew and Gentile in one body (Ephesians 2) has roots right here. God does not merely spare His enemies. He adopts them, names them, and blesses them in the midst of the earth.

### Key verses

> "Behold, the LORD is riding on a swift cloud and comes to Egypt; and the idols of Egypt will tremble at his presence, and the heart of the Egyptians will melt within them." — Isaiah 19:1 (ESV)

> "And it will be a sign and a witness to the LORD of hosts in the land of Egypt. When they cry to the LORD because of oppressors, he will send them a savior and defender, and deliver them." — Isaiah 19:20 (ESV)

> "In that day Israel will be the third with Egypt and Assyria, a blessing in the midst of the earth, whom the LORD of hosts has blessed, saying, 'Blessed be Egypt my people, and Assyria the work of my hands, and Israel my inheritance.'" — Isaiah 19:24–25 (ESV)

### The bad news and the good news

The bad news of Isaiah 19 is that the oldest wisdom, the strongest economy, and the most impressive religion on earth all melt when God rides in on His cloud — nothing human is too established to fall. The good news is almost too much to believe: the fallen enemy gets an altar, a Savior, and a name — "Egypt my people." If God can say that over Egypt and Assyria, there is no wreckage of a life — yours included — that He cannot call His own work and His own people.

**A note from the Reformers.** Calvin called the end of this chapter one of the clearest Old Testament windows into the calling of the Gentiles, and he warned the church never to narrow God's "my people" to the people who already look like us. The highway from Egypt to Assyria runs through places the respectable would never build a road.

**Reflection question:** Who is your "Egypt" or "Assyria" — the enemy you cannot imagine God blessing? What would it do to your prayers to hear Him say over them, "my people... the work of my hands"?

---
## Isaiah 20 — The Prophet Walks Barefoot: A Sign Nobody Wanted to See

### Jesus in this chapter

Jesus is present in this chapter as the true sign-bearing Servant. Isaiah walks naked and barefoot for three years as a living sermon: "as my servant Isaiah has walked naked and barefoot for three years as a sign and a portent against Egypt and Cush, so shall the king of Assyria lead away the Egyptian captives and the Cushite exiles." Isaiah is called "my servant" — the title that will crown the Servant Songs — and his humiliation prefigures a greater one: the true Servant stripped and led away publicly, bearing shame He did not deserve, as God's sign to a world that trusted in the wrong helpers. Egypt and Cush were Judah's hoped-for rescuers. Isaiah's bare feet preach that every rescuer but God ends led away in chains.

### Commentary

**Three years of embodied preaching (20:1–3).** In the year the Assyrian commander took Ashdod, the LORD spoke by Isaiah — and the word came with a wardrobe command: "Go, and loose the sackcloth from your waist and take off your sandals from your feet." Isaiah obeyed, walking naked — the Hebrew *arom* likely means stripped to an undergarment, the dress of a captive or a slave — and barefoot for three years. Prophets preached with their bodies: Ezekiel lay on his side, Hosea married his sermon, and Isaiah walked his. Three years of daily humiliation, every market day a reminder. That is what obedience can cost, and Isaiah pays it without a recorded complaint. Before we ask God for a platform, Isaiah asks us whether we would walk barefoot for three years with nothing but a message nobody wanted.

**The shame of the trusted helpers (20:4–6).** The interpretation is plain: Assyria will lead Egypt and Cush away captive, "young and old, naked and barefoot, with buttocks uncovered, the nakedness of Egypt." Then comes the verse aimed at Jerusalem: "They shall be dismayed and ashamed because of Cush their hope and of Egypt their boast. And the inhabitants of this coastland will say in that day, 'Behold, this is what has happened to those in whom we hoped and to whom we fled for help to be delivered from the king of Assyria! And we, how shall we escape?'" The coastland is Judah itself. The whole chapter is a mirror held up to alliance-politics: the helpers you boast in can be paraded naked, and then where is your boast? Every generation of God's people faces the same temptation — to flee for help to the nearest Egypt instead of waiting on the LORD. Isaiah's bare feet are God's footnote on every such flight: your Egypt is more fragile than it looks.

### Key verses

> "At that time the LORD spoke by Isaiah the son of Amoz, saying, 'Go, and loose the sackcloth from your waist and take off your sandals from your feet,' and he did so, walking naked and barefoot." — Isaiah 20:2 (ESV)

> "Then the LORD said, 'As my servant Isaiah has walked naked and barefoot for three years as a sign and a portent against Egypt and Cush, so shall the king of Assyria lead away the Egyptian captives and the Cushite exiles, both the young and the old, naked and barefoot, with buttocks uncovered, the nakedness of Egypt.'" — Isaiah 20:3–4 (ESV)

> "And the inhabitants of this coastland will say in that day, 'Behold, this is what has happened to those in whom we hoped and to whom we fled for help to be delivered from the king of Assyria! And we, how shall we escape?'" — Isaiah 20:6 (ESV)

### The bad news and the good news

The bad news of Isaiah 20 is that every Egypt we flee to for help can be led away naked — our hoped-for rescuers are themselves captives waiting to happen, and the day they fall, our boast falls with them. The good news is that God sends the warning before He sends the fall, and He has provided a Helper who cannot be stripped or chained: His Servant, who was Himself stripped and led away in shame so that everyone who flees to Him — instead of to Egypt — will never ask "how shall we escape?" without an answer.

**A note from the Reformers.** Calvin pointed out that Isaiah's obedience shames our comfort: the prophet preached three years with his body what we will not preach three minutes with our mouths if it costs us dignity. The Reformers used this chapter to ask what a man will endure to be believed.

**Reflection question:** What is your Egypt right now — the help you are fleeing to instead of waiting on God? What would Isaiah's bare feet say to your plan?

---

## Isaiah 21 — Watchman, What of the Night? Babylon Falls in a Vision

### Jesus in this chapter

Jesus is the coming Rider and the Keeper of the night watch in this chapter. Twice the cry comes: "Fallen, fallen is Babylon" — the cry Revelation 18 takes up at the end of history, when Christ's kingdom stands and Babylon's does not. And the chapter's most human moment belongs to the watchman of Dumah: "Watchman, what time of the night? Watchman, what time of the night?" Every suffering believer has asked that question — *how much longer?* — and the watchman's answer is the posture of faith until Jesus returns: "Morning comes, and also the night. If you will inquire, inquire; come back again." Christ is the Morning that is certainly coming; the watchman's job — and ours — is to stay at the post until He does.

### Commentary

**The storm from the desert (21:1–5).** "A stern vision is told to me; the traitor betrays, and the destroyer destroys." Isaiah sees Elam and Media besieging, hears the groaning God will end, and his own body convulses at the sight: "My heart staggers; horror has appalled me; the twilight I longed for has been turned for me into trembling." Prophets do not enjoy their visions of judgment. Meanwhile Babylon feasts: "They prepare the table, they spread the rugs, they eat, they drink" — and the cry interrupts the banquet: "Rise up, you commanders, oil the shields!" Belshazzar's feast in Daniel 5 is this scene fulfilled: a city drinking from God's vessels on the night it falls. Comfort at the edge of judgment is the most dangerous seat in the world.

**Fallen, fallen is Babylon (21:6–10).** God sets the watchman: "Go, set a watchman; let him announce what he sees." The watchman sees horsemen in pairs, riders on donkeys and camels, and then the lion's roar of a report: "Fallen, fallen is Babylon; and all the carved images of her gods he has shattered to the ground." The doubled "fallen" is finality — said twice, done forever. Then Isaiah turns tender toward his own people, pictured as grain on a threshing floor: "O my threshed and winnowed one, what I have heard from the LORD of hosts, the God of Israel, I announce to you." God's people may be threshed — beaten to separate grain from husk — but they are *His* threshed ones, and the news he brings them is that their oppressor has fallen. Threshing is not abandonment. It is harvest work.

**The cry from Seir (21:11–12).** The oracle concerning Dumah — Edom — is only two verses and contains one of Scripture's great questions: "Watchman, what time of the night?" The answer refuses both despair and cheap comfort: "Morning comes, and also the night. If you will inquire, inquire; come back again." Morning is real and coming; night is real and not yet over. The honest watchman will not fake a sunrise, and he will not deny one either. Come back again — keep asking, keep watching, keep returning to the post. That is the whole duty of God's people in the long night before Christ returns.

**Arabia's thirst (21:13–17).** The last oracle sends fugitives into the Arabian desert, and commands Tema to "meet the thirsty with water... meet the fugitive with bread." Even in judgment oracles, God stations mercy on the escape routes and commands His people to be the ones carrying water. Within a year, "according to the years of a hired worker," Kedar's glory ends and its archers dwindle — God's deadlines again, exact as a labor contract.

### Key verses

> "And behold, here come riders, horsemen in pairs!" And he answered, "Fallen, fallen is Babylon; and all the carved images of her gods he has shattered to the ground." — Isaiah 21:9 (ESV)

> "He calls to me out of Seir, 'Watchman, what time of the night? Watchman, what time of the night?' The watchman says: 'Morning comes, and also the night. If you will inquire, inquire; come back again.'" — Isaiah 21:11–12 (ESV)

### The bad news and the good news

The bad news of Isaiah 21 is that the night is real and not yet over — Babylon feasts while it falls, God's people are threshed, and the watchman will not lie about the hour. The good news is that morning comes: Babylon's fall is announced before it happens, the threshed ones are still God's own, and the Morning Himself — Christ, the bright morning star (Revelation 22:16) — is on His way. Inquire, and come back again. He is worth the watch.

**A note from the Reformers.** Spurgeon preached "Watchman, what time of the night?" to anxious believers as the question of every dark season, and he loved the watchman's honesty — morning is coming, night is here, and the right response to both facts is to stay at the post and keep asking.

**Reflection question:** Where are you asking "how much longer?" right now — and will you take the watchman's answer: stay at your post, keep inquiring, and trust that morning comes?

---

## Isaiah 22 — The Valley of Vision: Shebna's Tomb and Eliakim's Nail

### Jesus in this chapter

Jesus is the true Eliakim of this chapter — and more than Eliakim. Of Eliakim God says: "And I will place on his shoulder the key of the house of David. He shall open, and none shall shut; and he shall shut, and none shall open." Jesus quotes those words of Himself in Revelation 3:7: "The words of the holy one, the true one, who has the key of David, who opens and no one will shut, who shuts and no one opens." And again: "I will fasten him like a peg in a secure place" — a peg the Father Himself fastened, from which all the glory of the house hangs. Every vessel in God's house, "from the cups to all the flagons," hangs on that one peg — and the peg is Christ. Shebna carved himself a tomb; Eliakim received a throne. Jesus received both tomb and throne, and left the tomb empty.

### Commentary

**Reveling on the edge of ruin (22:1–14).** Jerusalem is called "the valley of vision," and she is on the housetops shouting — celebrating a deliverance she did not earn and does not understand, while her slain are "not slain with the sword" but dead of siege and flight. Isaiah weeps bitterly and will not be comforted. The city had a narrow escape and learned nothing from it: "In that day the Lord GOD of hosts called for weeping and mourning... but behold, joy and gladness, killing oxen and slaughtering sheep, eating flesh and drinking wine. 'Let us eat and drink, for tomorrow we die.'" Paul quotes that motto in 1 Corinthians 15:32 as the creed of people who deny the resurrection. Isaiah exposes it as the creed of people who deny judgment. The specific failure is named in verse 11: they fortified the walls and collected the waters "but you did not look to him who did it, or see him who planned it long ago." Engineering without repentance. Preparation without looking to God. It is possible to be very busy getting ready and still miss the only One who matters.

**Shebna: the tomb-carver (22:15–19).** Shebna is the steward "who is over the household," a high official using his office for his monument: "What have you to do here, and whom have you here, that you have cut out here a tomb for yourself, you who cut out a tomb on the height and carve a dwelling for yourself in the rock?" God answers the self-made memorial with a demotion and a death in exile: "Behold, the LORD will hurl you away violently, O you strong man. He will seize firm hold on you and whirl you around and around, and throw you like a ball into a wide land." The man who carved his name in rock will die where no one carves anything for him. Office is a trust, not a quarry.

**Eliakim: the father, the throne, the peg (22:20–25).** In Shebna's place God calls Eliakim son of Hilkiah: "I will clothe him with your robe, and will bind your sash on him, and will commit your authority to his hand. And he shall be a father to the inhabitants of Jerusalem and to the house of Judah." A father, not a monument-builder. The key of David goes on his shoulder; he opens and none shuts. "And I will fasten him like a peg in a secure place, and he will become a throne of honor to his father's house." The chapter's final note is honest, though: even the peg in the secure place, loaded with all the vessels of the house, is spoken of in a day when the load is cut off — because no merely human steward can bear the whole weight of God's house forever. Eliakim was a good man and a true type, but the peg that never gives way had to be God Himself in the flesh. That is why Jesus takes Eliakim's job description onto His own shoulders in Revelation 3. All the vessels hang on Him, and He holds.

### Key verses

> "And in that day the Lord GOD of hosts called for weeping and mourning, for baldness and wearing sackcloth; but behold, joy and gladness, killing oxen and slaughtering sheep, eating flesh and drinking wine. 'Let us eat and drink, for tomorrow we die!'" — Isaiah 22:12–13 (ESV)

> "And I will place on his shoulder the key of the house of David. He shall open, and none shall shut; and he shall shut, and none shall open." — Isaiah 22:22 (ESV)

> "And I will fasten him like a peg in a secure place, and he will become a throne of honor to his father's house." — Isaiah 22:23 (ESV)

### The bad news and the good news

The bad news of Isaiah 22 is that a city can be delivered and learn nothing — fortifying walls while refusing to look to the God who planned it all — and that self-made monuments like Shebna's end in exile, while even the best human peg bends under the whole weight of God's house. The good news is that God has fastened a Peg who does not bend: Jesus, who holds the key of David, opens what no one can shut, and carries every vessel in the house — the cups and the flagons, the least and the greatest — on His own shoulder. Hang your whole weight on Him. He holds.

**A note from the Reformers.** Calvin treated Eliakim as one of the clearest types of Christ's government in the prophets — a steward whose authority, key, and fatherhood all point past himself to the Son of David, in whom the type becomes the reality and the peg becomes unbreakable.

**Reflection question:** Are you carving a tomb like Shebna — building a name for yourself — or hanging like a vessel on God's peg? What is one thing you need to transfer from your own shoulders to His?

---

## Isaiah 23 — Tyre: The Marketplace of the Nations Brought Low

### Jesus in this chapter

Jesus is the Lord who humbles the world's marketplace in this chapter — and the Redeemer of its profits at the end. Tyre was the Amazon and the shipping empire of the ancient world, "the merchant of the nations," whose traders were princes. God decrees her seventy years of forgottenness, and then — the twist no economist would predict — her merchandise and her wages "will be holy to the LORD," stored up "to provide abundant food and fine clothing for those who dwell before the LORD." The wealth of the nations ends up clothing God's people. That is the pattern Christ completes: the kings of the earth bring their glory into the New Jerusalem (Revelation 21:24), and the marketplace that served itself ends by serving the saints.

### Commentary

**The wail of the ships (23:1–7).** "Wail, O ships of Tarshish, for Tyre is laid waste, without house or harbor!" The news reaches Cyprus before it reaches home; sailors learn their city is gone while they are still at sea. Sidon is told to be ashamed — the sea itself speaks: "I have neither labored nor given birth." Egypt's revenue, the grain of the Shihor, was Tyre's harvest, "and she became the marketplace of the nations." Isaiah asks the question every commercial empire dreads: "Is this your exultant city whose origin is from days of old?" The proudest trading floor on earth becomes a lesson in how fast the market can close forever.

**The LORD of hosts has purposed it (23:8–13).** Who did this? Not chance, not merely Assyria or Babylon: "The LORD of hosts has purposed it, to defile the pompous pride of all glory, to dishonor all the honored of the earth." Tyre's specific sin is not trade — trade is not evil — it is *pride in* trade: crown-giving merchants who mistook wealth for worth and influence for immunity. God's purpose clause is aimed with precision: pride, pomp, and the honored of the earth. The daughter of Sidon is told to cross to Cyprus — "even there you will find no rest." There is no offshore haven from the judgment of God. The Chaldeans — Babylon, still rising — are named as the instrument: the empire that will humble the marketplace is itself already on God's leash, and chapter 13 has already announced its own fall.

**Seventy years, then holiness (23:14–18).** Tyre will be forgotten seventy years — "according to the days of one king" — the same measure as Judah's exile (Jeremiah 25:11). At the end of the seventy years, Tyre will return to her trade "like a song about a prostitute" — she will ply her business with all the kingdoms of the world. And then the last verse, which no one saw coming: "Her merchandise and her wages will be holy to the LORD. It will not be stored or hoarded, but her merchandise will supply abundant food and fine clothing for those who dwell before the LORD." The profits of the world's most self-serving marketplace end up consecrated — feeding and clothing the people of God. God does not merely judge the marketplace; He redeems its output. Nothing surrendered to Him is wasted — not Tyre's silver, and not the years, skills, and hard-won experience of a wasted life either. What was earned in the far country can end up holy to the LORD.

### Key verses

> "The LORD of hosts has purposed it, to defile the pompous pride of all glory, to dishonor all the honored of the earth." — Isaiah 23:9 (ESV)

> "At the end of seventy years, the LORD will visit Tyre, and she will return to her wages and will prostitute herself with all the kingdoms of the world on the face of the earth. Her merchandise and her wages will be holy to the LORD." — Isaiah 23:17–18 (ESV)

### The bad news and the good news

The bad news of Isaiah 23 is that the marketplace of the nations is not exempt: wealth, influence, and honored names are all scheduled for humbling when they swell into pride, and there is no Cyprus to flee to. The good news is that God's purpose with Tyre's profits is holiness, not waste — He ends the oracle with the world's wages feeding and clothing His people. Bring Him the earnings of your worst years. He knows how to make merchandise holy.

**A note from the Reformers.** Spurgeon drew comfort from the last verse for working people: the gains of honest trade, and even the gains of a misspent life, when surrendered to God, become provision for His household. God is not embarrassed by where His provision has traveled; He consecrates what is brought to Him.

**Reflection question:** What in your life — skill, experience, even profit from the bad years — have you assumed God could never use? What would it mean to see it become "holy to the LORD"?

---

## Isaiah 24 — The Earth Is Laid Waste: Judgment That Reaches the Whole World

### Jesus in this chapter

Jesus is the King who reigns in glory on Mount Zion at this chapter's end — "the LORD of hosts will reign on Mount Zion and in Jerusalem, and before his elders he will be glorified" — and He is the reason a song can be sung in a chapter about the whole earth unraveling. Isaiah 24 is the doorway into the "Isaiah Apocalypse" (chapters 24–27), and its scope is universal: priest and people, master and maid, buyer and seller, all leveled. Jesus takes up this chapter's imagery for His own teaching on the end (Matthew 24) — cosmic shaking, a world mourning — and He stands at the far side of it as the reigning King.
