The chapter asks the question every generation asks when the world shakes: is anyone actually in charge at the end? Isaiah's answer: the LORD of hosts, reigning on Zion, glorified before His elders.

### Commentary

**The great leveling (24:1–6).** "Behold, the LORD will empty the earth and make it desolate, and he will twist its surface and scatter its inhabitants." Then the pairs: "as with the people, so with the priest; as with the slave, so with his master; as with the maid, so with her mistress; as with the buyer, so with the seller." Judgment in Isaiah 24 is a leveler — no rank buys exemption, no class finds a private exit. The cause is covenant-breaking at the root of creation itself: "The earth lies defiled under its inhabitants; for they have transgressed the laws, violated the statutes, broken the everlasting covenant." The "everlasting covenant" reaches back to Noah (Genesis 9) — the whole human race stands in it, and the whole earth groans under its breach. "Therefore a curse devours the earth, and its inhabitants suffer for their guilt; therefore the inhabitants of the earth are scorched, and few men are left." Paul will say the whole creation groans (Romans 8:22). Isaiah said it first.

**The city of chaos and the silenced song (24:7–13).** The wine mourns, the vine languishes, the merry-hearted sigh. "The mirth of the tambourines is stilled, the noise of the jubilant has ceased." There is a special sadness in Isaiah's eye for parties ending: they drink wine but it is bitter; the gates are battered; "the wasted city is broken down; every house is shut up so that none can enter." The city left standing is called *tohu* — the Hebrew word for the formless waste of Genesis 1:2. Judgment is un-creation: God letting a rebellious world slide back toward the chaos it chose. And in the middle of the desolation, an olive tree image: "as when an olive tree is beaten, as at the gleaning when the grape harvest is done" — a few olives left at the top. Even here, gleanings remain. God always leaves gleanings.

**Songs from the ends of the earth (24:14–20).** And then — astonishingly — singing: "They lift up their voices, they sing for joy; over the majesty of the LORD they shout from the west. Therefore in the east give glory to the LORD... From the ends of the earth we hear songs of praise, in honor of the Righteous One." While the earth reels like a drunkard and sways like a hut, a remnant is singing from the coastlands. But Isaiah himself breaks down under what he sees: "I waste away, I waste away. Woe is me!" The prophet cannot sing and sob at the same time without cost. The earth "falls, and will not rise again" under the transgression that lies heavy upon it — the old order's collapse is total and irreversible.

**The host of heaven and the reigning King (24:21–23).** "On that day the LORD will punish the host of heaven, in heaven, and the kings of the earth, on the earth." Judgment reaches both realms — rebellious spiritual powers and rebellious earthly thrones shut up in a pit together, punished after many days. And then the last verse, the sunrise: "Then the moon will be confounded and the sun ashamed, for the LORD of hosts will reign on Mount Zion and in Jerusalem, and before his elders he will be glorified." The created lights go dim with embarrassment because a greater Light is reigning. Revelation ends in a city that needs no sun, "for the glory of God gives it light, and its lamp is the Lamb" (Revelation 21:23). Isaiah saw the lamp first.

### Key verses

> "The earth lies defiled under its inhabitants; for they have transgressed the laws, violated the statutes, broken the everlasting covenant. Therefore a curse devours the earth, and its inhabitants suffer for their guilt; therefore the inhabitants of the earth are scorched, and few men are left." — Isaiah 24:5–6 (ESV)

> "From the ends of the earth we hear songs of praise, in honor of the Righteous One." — Isaiah 24:16 (ESV)

> "Then the moon will be confounded and the sun ashamed, for the LORD of hosts will reign on Mount Zion and in Jerusalem, and before his elders he will be glorified." — Isaiah 24:23 (ESV)

### The bad news and the good news

The bad news of Isaiah 24 is that judgment is universal and leveling — priest and people, buyer and seller, the whole defiled earth under a broken covenant, reeling like a drunkard toward a fall from which it will not rise. The good news is that the fall is not the end of the story: gleanings remain, songs rise from the ends of the earth, and on Mount Zion the LORD of hosts reigns in glory — the Lamb is the lamp of the world to come. When everything shakes, the reign does not.

**A note from the Reformers.** Calvin read Isaiah 24 as God's answer to the illusion that judgment is local or class-bound: the curse reaches the whole earth because the covenant broken was the whole earth's — and therefore the remedy, when it comes in chapters 25–27, must be just as wide. It is, because it is Christ.

**Reflection question:** When the world shakes — news, markets, nations, your own household — where does your mind go first: to the reeling, or to the reigning King on Zion? What would change if you started with the reign?

---

## Isaiah 25 — The Feast on the Mountain: Death Swallowed Up Forever

### Jesus in this chapter

Jesus is the host of this chapter's feast and the swallower of death. "On this mountain the LORD of hosts will make for all peoples a feast of rich food, a feast of well-aged wine." Jesus picks up the image for His parables of the wedding feast (Matthew 22) and serves a foretaste of it in the Last Supper: "I tell you I will not drink again of this fruit of the vine until that day when I drink it new with you in my Father's kingdom" (Matthew 26:29). And the chapter's thunderclap — "He will swallow up death forever" — is quoted by Paul at the climax of the resurrection chapter: "Death is swallowed up in victory. O death, where is your victory? O death, where is your sting?" (1 Corinthians 15:54–55). The swallowing happened at an empty tomb outside Jerusalem, on the mountain's own slopes.

### Commentary

**You have done wonderful things (25:1–5).** The chapter opens as personal praise, the prophet speaking for every saved person: "O LORD, you are my God; I will exalt you; I will praise your name, for you have done wonderful things, plans formed of old, faithful and sure." Notice: "you are *my* God." After the universal judgment of chapter 24, faith becomes personal again. God has made the city a heap, silenced the ruthless, and — the line the weary should underline — "you have been a stronghold to the poor, a stronghold to the needy in his distress, a shelter from the storm and a shade from the heat." God's power in this chapter is not abstract; it is measured by what it does for the poor and needy. The breath of the ruthless is like a storm against a wall; God's shelter stands in exactly that weather.

**The feast and the veil (25:6–8).** Then the mountain fills with tables: "On this mountain the LORD of hosts will make for all peoples a feast of rich food, a feast of well-aged wine, of rich food full of marrow, of aged wine well refined." *All peoples* — the menu is universal; the God who judged all nations in chapter 24 feasts all nations in chapter 25. "And he will swallow up on this mountain the covering that is cast over all peoples, the veil that is spread over all nations." The veil is mourning, blindness, and death itself — the shroud over the human race. God's answer is not to lift it politely but to swallow the swallower: "He will swallow up death forever; and the Lord GOD will wipe away tears from all faces, and the reproach of his people he will take away from all the earth, for the LORD has spoken." Wipe away tears from *all* faces — Revelation 21:4 quotes it for the new creation. The Hebrew verb for swallow, *bala*, is the same word used for the earth swallowing Korah and for Jonah's great fish. Death has swallowed everyone since Adam. Here God announces the day death gets swallowed. Easter morning was that day, in firstfruits; the feast is still coming in full.

**It will be said in that day (25:9–12).** "Behold, this is our God; we have waited for him, that he might save us. This is the LORD; we have waited for him; let us be glad and rejoice in his salvation." Waiting is the posture of the whole book — and here the waiting ends in recognition: *this* is our God, the One we waited for, not another. Meanwhile Moab — pride again — is trodden down "as straw is trodden down in a dunghill," spreading out his hands to swim in it. The contrast is total: on the mountain, a feast and wiped tears; in the dunghill, pride swimming in filth. Two ends for the human race, set side by side, and the difference is whether a man waited for God or exalted himself.

### Key verses

> "For you have been a stronghold to the poor, a stronghold to the needy in his distress, a shelter from the storm and a shade from the heat." — Isaiah 25:4 (ESV)

> "On this mountain the LORD of hosts will make for all peoples a feast of rich food, a feast of well-aged wine, of rich food full of marrow, of aged wine well refined." — Isaiah 25:6 (ESV)

> "He will swallow up death forever; and the Lord GOD will wipe away tears from all faces, and the reproach of his people he will take away from all the earth, for the LORD has spoken." — Isaiah 25:8 (ESV)

> "It will be said on that day, 'Behold, this is our God; we have waited for him, that he might save us. This is the LORD; we have waited for him; let us be glad and rejoice in his salvation.'" — Isaiah 25:9 (ESV)

### The bad news and the good news

The bad news of Isaiah 25 is that a veil of death lies over all peoples and no feast we throw can lift it — pride's portion is the dunghill, and every face on earth has tears. The good news is that God Himself hosts the feast, swallows death forever, and wipes the tears away with His own hand. In Christ the swallowing has already begun: death died in His tomb, and the rest of the promise — every tear, every face — is as certain as the LORD who spoke it.

**A note from the Reformers.** Spurgeon called Isaiah 25:8 the death of death and preached it at funerals all his life: the Christian does not merely survive death or escape death — in Christ, death itself is the thing that gets eaten. He told mourners to read the verse as a menu and a obituary at once: a feast for the living, and death's name in the obituaries.

**Reflection question:** Whose tears are you carrying today — your own or someone else's? What does it mean to you that God promises to wipe them away Himself, not send someone else to do it?

---

## Isaiah 26 — The Song of the Strong City: Perfect Peace for the Mind Stayed on Him

### Jesus in this chapter

Jesus is the Rock of Ages in this chapter — "the LORD, the LORD himself, is the Rock eternal" — and the One who keeps in perfect peace everyone whose mind is stayed on Him. He is also the resurrection at the chapter's center: "Your dead shall live; their bodies shall rise. You who dwell in the dust, awake and sing for joy!" Jesus takes that promise and makes it personal and present: "An hour is coming when all who are in the tombs will hear his voice and come out" (John 5:28–29) — and then He proved it by walking out of His own tomb. And He is the hidden shelter of verse 20: "Come, my people, enter your chambers, and shut your doors behind you; hide yourselves for a little while until the fury has passed by" — the voice of the Shepherd gathering His own out of the storm, as He gathered His disciples into an upper room the night the fury fell on Him instead.

### Commentary

**The strong city and the open gates (26:1–6).** "In that day this song will be sung in the land of Judah: 'We have a strong city; he sets up salvation as walls and bulwarks. Open the gates, that the righteous nation that keeps faith may enter in.'" The walls of this city are not stone but salvation itself — God is the fortification. And the gates stand open, not to the powerful but to "the righteous nation that keeps faith." Then the verse that has carried more believers through more nights than almost any other in the Old Testament: "You keep him in perfect peace whose mind is stayed on you, because he trusts in you. Trust in the LORD forever, for the LORD GOD is an everlasting rock." Perfect peace in Hebrew is doubled — *shalom shalom*, peace peace, peace squared. The mind "stayed" is *samak* — leaned, supported, propped up. Peace is not the absence of trouble in this chapter; the chapter is full of trouble. Peace is a mind leaned hard on a Rock that cannot move. The proud city is brought low in the same breath — "the foot tramples it, the feet of the poor, the steps of the needy" — God's reversals again: the lofty city dust, the needy walking on top of it.

**The path of the righteous and the long night of seeking (26:7–18).** "The path of the righteous is level; you, the Upright One, make level the way of the righteous. In the path of your judgments, O LORD, we wait for you; your name and remembrance are the desire of our soul." Then one of the most honest prayers in Scripture: "My soul yearns for you in the night; my spirit within me earnestly seeks you." This is night-faith — seeking God in the dark, before the evidence arrives. The chapter is equally honest about the nation's failure: "We have as it were brought forth wind" — all their labor produced nothing; "we have accomplished no deliverance in the earth." Confession of total inability sits right next to hope, and that is exactly where resurrection enters.

**Your dead shall live (26:19–21).** Verse 19 is one of the Old Testament's clearest resurrection promises: "Your dead shall live; their bodies shall rise. You who dwell in the dust, awake and sing for joy! For your dew is a dew of light, and the earth will give birth to the dead." Dust-dwellers singing — that is God's endgame for His people, and it is why Christian funerals are not hopeless. Then the shelter command: enter your chambers, shut the doors, hide "for a little while until the fury has passed by. For behold, the LORD is coming out from his place to punish the inhabitants of the earth for their iniquity." The fury is real, it is coming, and it has a duration — "a little while." God's people are not promised exemption from the storm's weather, but they are promised a chamber, a door, and a Father who knows when the fury has passed.

### Key verses

> "You keep him in perfect peace whose mind is stayed on you, because he trusts in you. Trust in the LORD forever, for the LORD GOD is an everlasting rock." — Isaiah 26:3–4 (ESV)

> "My soul yearns for you in the night; my spirit within me earnestly seeks you. For when your judgments are in the earth, the inhabitants of the world learn righteousness." — Isaiah 26:9 (ESV)

> "Your dead shall live; their bodies shall rise. You who dwell in the dust, awake and sing for joy! For your dew is a dew of light, and the earth will give birth to the dead." — Isaiah 26:19 (ESV)

> "Come, my people, enter your chambers, and shut your doors behind you; hide yourselves for a little while until the fury has passed by." — Isaiah 26:20 (ESV)

### The bad news and the good news

The bad news of Isaiah 26 is that our own labors bring forth wind — we accomplish no deliverance in the earth — and that a fury is coming on the inhabitants of the earth for their iniquity, from which no lofty city can protect us. The good news is that the LORD Himself is an everlasting Rock, a strong city whose walls are salvation, a peace that doubles for minds stayed on Him, and a resurrection for dust-dwellers who will wake up singing. Lean your mind on Him. The Rock has never moved.

**A note from the Reformers.** Luther called Isaiah 26:3 a verse to write on the wall of every troubled conscience — peace, he said, is not found by emptying the mind but by fixing it: the mind stayed on God is kept, the way a ship is kept by its anchor, not by the absence of sea.

**Reflection question:** Where is your mind "stayed" right now — what is it leaning on through the day and in the night? What would deliberately re-leaning it on the LORD look like this week?

---
## Isaiah 27 — The Vineyard Kept: Leviathan Slain and Israel Blossoming

### Jesus in this chapter

Jesus is the Keeper of the vineyard in this chapter — "I, the LORD, am its keeper; every moment I water it. Lest anyone punish it, I keep it night and day." The Owner who in chapter 5 looked for fruit and found wild grapes now, in the day of redemption, tends the vineyard Himself, moment by moment. That is the difference the cross makes: the vineyard is no longer under a disappointed inspection but under a Keeper's constant care. Christ also stands behind the slaying of Leviathan — "the fleeing serpent... the twisting serpent... the dragon that is in the sea" — the ancient chaos-power, Satan himself (Revelation 12:9 names the dragon "that ancient serpent"), whom Jesus bound at the cross and will destroy at His return. And the chapter's end — worshipers gathered from Assyria and Egypt by a great trumpet — is the gathering Jesus describes in Matthew 24:31, where His angels sound the trumpet and collect the elect from the four winds.

### Commentary

**Leviathan and the pleasant vineyard (27:1–6).** "In that day the LORD with his hard and great and strong sword will punish Leviathan the fleeing serpent, Leviathan the twisting serpent, and he will kill the dragon that is in the sea." Three descriptions, one enemy: the chaos-serpent who stands behind every empire that devours God's people. God's sword is described with three adjectives — hard, great, strong — because the enemy is real; but the outcome is stated without suspense. Then the song: "In that day, 'A pleasant vineyard, sing of it! I, the LORD, am its keeper.'" Set this beside chapter 5 and weep with relief. There, God sang a love song over a vineyard that failed. Here, God sings over a vineyard He keeps — watering every moment, guarding night and day, with no wrath left: "I have no wrath. Would that I had thorns and briers to battle! I would march against them, I would burn them up together. Or let them lay hold of my protection, let them make peace with me, let them make peace with me." Twice: make peace with me. Even the thorns are offered terms. And the promise lands: "In days to come Jacob shall take root, Israel shall blossom and put forth shoots and fill the whole face of the world with fruit." From wild grapes to a world filled with fruit — that is the arc of grace in this book, and in every life God keeps.

**Measured judgment and the altar stones (27:7–11).** God's discipline of Israel was measured — "by exile you contended" — never the annihilation He poured on their oppressors. The fruit of it: Jacob's guilt is atoned for when he makes "all the stones of the altars like chalkstones crushed to pieces" — repentance that smashes its own idols is the evidence of pardon. A people without understanding, branches dried and broken for firewood, is the alternative picture: the Maker shows no favor to those who will not know Him. Understanding — knowing God — is not academic in Isaiah. It is the difference between a kept vineyard and kindling.

**The trumpet and the gathering (27:12–13).** "In that day from the river Euphrates to the Brook of Egypt the LORD will thresh out the grain, and you will be gleaned one by one, O people of Israel." One by one — the gathering is personal, not bulk. "And in that day a great trumpet will be blown, and those who were lost in the land of Assyria and those who were driven out to the land of Egypt will come and worship the LORD on the holy mountain at Jerusalem." The lost and the driven out, from the two lands of Israel's bondage, come home to worship. Every prodigal geography is named. Nobody is too lost in Assyria or too driven out in Egypt for the trumpet.

### Key verses

> "In that day, 'A pleasant vineyard, sing of it! I, the LORD, am its keeper; every moment I water it. Lest anyone punish it, I keep it night and day.'" — Isaiah 27:2–3 (ESV)

> "In days to come Jacob shall take root, Israel shall blossom and put forth shoots and fill the whole face of the world with fruit." — Isaiah 27:6 (ESV)

> "And in that day a great trumpet will be blown, and those who were lost in the land of Assyria and those who were driven out to the land of Egypt will come and worship the LORD on the holy mountain at Jerusalem." — Isaiah 27:13 (ESV)

### The bad news and the good news

The bad news of Isaiah 27 is that Leviathan is real, thorns grow in the vineyard, and a people that refuses to know its Maker becomes dry branches fit for burning. The good news is that God slays the dragon, keeps the vineyard moment by moment, offers peace twice to the very thorns, and blows a trumpet that reaches the lost in Assyria and the driven out in Egypt — gathering them one by one. If He keeps the vineyard night and day, He can keep you.

**A note from the Reformers.** Spurgeon preached "every moment I water it" as the answer to believers who fear God has forgotten them between Sundays: the Keeper's care is not weekly but momently, and the proof of pardon is a man smashing his own altar stones — repentance with a hammer in its hand.

**Reflection question:** Chapter 5's vineyard failed under inspection; chapter 27's flourishes under keeping. Are you relating to God as an inspector to fear or a Keeper to trust — and what altar stones of yours still need crushing?

---

## Isaiah 28 — The Cornerstone in Zion: Woe to the Drunk, Glory to the Remnant

### Jesus in this chapter

Jesus is named in this chapter with one of His great titles: "Behold, I am the one who has laid as a foundation in Zion, a stone, a tested stone, a precious cornerstone, of a sure foundation: 'Whoever believes will not be in haste.'" Peter quotes it of Christ directly: "Behold, I am laying in Zion a stone, a cornerstone chosen and precious, and whoever believes in him will not be put to shame" (1 Peter 2:6), and Paul builds Romans 9:33 on it. Everything in Isaiah 28 hangs on the contrast between two foundations: the covenant with death that Judah's rulers made, and the cornerstone God laid. Jesus is the tested stone — tested in the wilderness, tested in Gethsemane, tested on the cross, and never cracked. Build on Him and you will not panic when the flood comes.

### Commentary

**Woe to the drunkards of Ephraim (28:1–8).** The woes begin again, this time on the northern kingdom's proud crown — Samaria, set on a rich valley, its glorious beauty "a fading flower." Drunkards is Isaiah's blunt word: the leadership of Israel is intoxicated, literally and spiritually, and the fading flower will be swallowed like the first fig of summer, eaten the moment it is seen. Priests and prophets reel too — "they see in confusion, they stumble in giving judgment" — and the tables are "full of filthy vomit, with no space left." It is an ugly picture on purpose: when the teachers of a nation are drunk, the nation drinks confusion with its doctrine. And yet, verse 5 turns: "In that day the LORD of hosts will be a crown of glory, and a diadem of beauty, to the remnant of his people." Samaria's crown fades; the remnant's crown is God Himself.

**Precept upon precept — and the rest refused (28:9–13).** The drunk scoffers mock Isaiah's teaching like children mocking a lesson: "precept upon precept, line upon line, here a little, there a little" — baby talk, they say. Isaiah turns their mockery into their sentence: because they despised the plain word, the word will come to them in stammering lips and a foreign tongue — Assyrian — "that they may go, and fall backward, and be broken, and snared, and taken." Buried in the mockery is one of God's tenderest offers, quoted in the middle of their scorn: "This is rest; give rest to the weary; and this is repose" — "yet they would not hear." Paul quotes this passage in 1 Corinthians 14 about tongues as a sign of judgment. The tragedy of Isaiah 28 is not that God offered nothing; it is that He offered rest, plainly, in simple lines, and drunk men laughed at the simplicity.

**The covenant with death and the Cornerstone (28:14–22).** Jerusalem's rulers have made their own arrangement: "We have made a covenant with death, and with Sheol we have an agreement, when the overwhelming whip passes through it will not come to us, for we have made lies our refuge, and in falsehood we have taken shelter." It is the eternal human strategy — negotiate with death, hide in lies. God's answer is twofold: He will sweep the refuge of lies away with hail and flood, annulling the covenant with death by simply outlasting it — and He lays His own foundation: "Behold, I am the one who has laid as a foundation in Zion, a stone, a tested stone, a precious cornerstone, of a sure foundation: 'Whoever believes will not be in haste.'" The Hebrew verb for "be in haste" (*yachish*) means to panic, to flee in alarm. Faith in the Stone ends panic. The measuring line for the building is justice, the plumb line righteousness — God builds straight. And the chapter's end warns with the strange works of Gibeon and Perazim: God's judgment is His "strange deed," His "alien work" — alien to His nature, which delights in mercy. Judgment is God's foreign work; mercy is His native tongue.

**The farmer's wisdom (28:23–29).** The chapter closes with a parable of a farmer: he does not plow forever; he sows in order, dill and cumin and wheat each in its place; he threshes each grain with the tool that fits it — dill with a rod, cumin with a stick, bread grain crushed but not forever. "This also comes from the LORD of hosts; he is wonderful in counsel and excellent in wisdom." The God of judgment is a farmer, not a vandal: His plowing has a purpose, His threshing is measured to the grain, and He does not crush forever. If you are under the threshing sledge right now, this parable is for you — the Farmer knows the difference between dill and wheat, and He knows when to stop.

### Key verses

> "Therefore hear the word of the LORD, you scoffers, who rule this people in Jerusalem! Because you have said, 'We have made a covenant with death, and with Sheol we have an agreement, when the overwhelming whip passes through it will not come to us, for we have made lies our refuge, and in falsehood we have taken shelter.'" — Isaiah 28:14–15 (ESV)

> "Behold, I am the one who has laid as a foundation in Zion, a stone, a tested stone, a precious cornerstone, of a sure foundation: 'Whoever believes will not be in haste.'" — Isaiah 28:16 (ESV)

> "This also comes from the LORD of hosts; he is wonderful in counsel and excellent in wisdom." — Isaiah 28:29 (ESV)

### The bad news and the good news

The bad news of Isaiah 28 is that men make covenants with death and call lies a refuge — and God's hail sweeps the refuge away, while His word, mocked as baby talk, becomes the very snare of those who scorned it. The good news is that God has laid a tested, precious Cornerstone in Zion, and whoever believes in Him — Peter applies it to Jesus without hesitation — will never be put to shame and never needs to panic. Two foundations are on offer. Only one of them is a Stone.

**A note from the Reformers.** Luther built his doctrine of faith on verses like Isaiah 28:16: faith is not a frantic work but a quiet resting on a Stone God laid — "whoever believes will not be in haste." The man who is hurrying and panicking, Luther said, has usually moved his weight off the Stone and back onto himself.

**Reflection question:** What "covenant with death" — what lie-refuge — have you been trusting to keep the flood from you? What would it mean to move your whole weight onto the Cornerstone today?

---

## Isaiah 29 — Ariel Besieged and Suddenly Delivered: Blind Eyes and a Book Unsealed

### Jesus in this chapter

Jesus walks through this chapter as the One who opens blind eyes and unseals the book. Isaiah prophesies a spiritual blindness on Jerusalem — "the LORD has poured out upon you a spirit of deep sleep, and has closed your eyes" — and Paul quotes it in Romans 11:8 as the mystery of Israel's partial hardening. But the same chapter promises the reversal: "In that day the deaf shall hear the words of a book, and out of their gloom and darkness the eyes of the blind shall see. The meek shall obtain fresh joy in the LORD, and the poor among mankind shall exult in the Holy One of Israel." Jesus read that kind of promise and then performed it — opening blind eyes, unstopping deaf ears, preaching good news to the poor — and told John's disciples to report exactly those things as the evidence that He was the Coming One (Matthew 11:4–5).

### Commentary

**Ariel: besieged, then suddenly saved (29:1–8).** Ariel is Jerusalem — "the city where David encamped," and possibly a name meaning "lion of God" or "altar hearth." God Himself announces He will encamp against His own city and bring her low "to the dust," until her voice comes up like a ghost's whisper. Then, at the point of extremity: "But in an instant, suddenly, you will be visited by the LORD of hosts with thunder and with earthquake and great noise, with whirlwind and tempest, and the flame of a devouring fire." The besieging multitude becomes "like fine dust," like passing chaff — "and in an instant, suddenly." This is the pattern of Isaiah 37, when Sennacherib's army melts overnight, and it is the pattern of many a personal deliverance: long siege, sudden rescue. The God who allows the siege also schedules the instant.

**The sealed book (29:9–16).** Then the diagnosis deepens: the people stagger, but not with drink — with a God-given deep sleep on their prophets and seers. The vision becomes "like the words of a book that is sealed," handed to a literate man who says "I cannot read it, for it is sealed," and to an illiterate man who says "I cannot read." Religion at its most tragic: the book exists, the words exist, and nobody can read. And God names the root: "Because this people draw near with their mouth and honor me with their lips, while their hearts are far from me, and their fear of me is a commandment taught by men." Jesus quotes that verse verbatim against the Pharisees (Matthew 15:8–9) — lip-honor, heart-distance, man-taught fear. God's response is to do a wonder that destroys the wisdom of the wise: "I will again do wonderful things with this people, with wonder upon wonder; and the wisdom of their wise men shall perish." Paul quotes that too, of the cross (1 Corinthians 1:19). The potter-clay image follows: "Shall the potter be regarded as the clay, that the thing made should say of its maker, 'He did not make me'?" — the upside-down thinking of creatures correcting their Creator, which Isaiah calls exactly what it is: perversion.

**The meek obtain fresh joy (29:17–24).** The chapter's last movement is pure reversal: Lebanon becomes a fruitful field; the deaf hear the words of a book; the blind see out of their gloom; "the meek shall obtain fresh joy in the LORD, and the poor among mankind shall exult in the Holy One of Israel." The ruthless come to nothing, the scoffer is cut off, and those who "err in spirit will come to understanding, and those who murmur will accept instruction." Murmurers accepting instruction — if God can do that, He can do anything with anybody. Jacob's shame is removed; his children's faces no longer grow pale; they sanctify God's name because they have seen what He does with the work of His hands.

### Key verses

> "And the multitude of all the nations that fight against Ariel... shall be like a dream, a vision of the night... But in an instant, suddenly, you will be visited by the LORD of hosts with thunder and with earthquake and great noise." — Isaiah 29:6–7 (ESV)

> "Because this people draw near with their mouth and honor me with their lips, while their hearts are far from me, and their fear of me is a commandment taught by men, therefore, behold, I will again do wonderful things with this people, with wonder upon wonder." — Isaiah 29:13–14 (ESV)

> "In that day the deaf shall hear the words of a book, and out of their gloom and darkness the eyes of the blind shall see. The meek shall obtain fresh joy in the LORD, and the poor among mankind shall exult in the Holy One of Israel." — Isaiah 29:18–19 (ESV)

### The bad news and the good news

The bad news of Isaiah 29 is that a people can hold God's book and be unable to read it — honoring Him with lips while hearts are far away — and that God Himself may seal the sleep of those who prefer their traditions to His presence. The good news is that the same God does wonders that wreck human wisdom: He unseals the book, opens blind eyes out of their gloom, and gives the meek fresh joy and the poor a reason to exult. In Jesus, the sealed book opened and the blind saw. It still happens.

**A note from the Reformers.** Calvin used Isaiah 29:13 as the standing test of all worship: God is not honored by lips at a distance. The Reformers' cry for preaching in the language of the people was, at heart, a cry for the unsealing of this book — that the deaf might hear its words.

**Reflection question:** Is your worship lip-honor with a far heart anywhere — a song, a prayer, a habit? And where do you need God to do His wonder: unseal the book, open the eyes, give fresh joy to the meek in you?

---

## Isaiah 30 — Woe to the Stubborn Children: The Egypt Plan and the God Who Waits to Be Gracious

### Jesus in this chapter

Jesus is the God who waits in this chapter — "Therefore the LORD waits to be gracious to you, and therefore he exalts himself to show mercy to you. For the LORD is a God of justice; blessed are all those who wait for him." That waiting God is the Father of the prodigal watching the road, and He is Jesus Himself, who would not force Jerusalem ("How often would I have gathered your children together... and you were not willing!"). Jesus is also the Teacher who will not be hidden anymore: "your eyes shall see your Teacher. And your ears shall hear a word behind you, saying, 'This is the way, walk in it.'" Every believer who has heard that quiet direction at a crossroads knows Whose voice it is. And the chapter's battle cry of faith — "In returning and rest you shall be saved; in quietness and in trust shall be your strength" — is the strength Jesus Himself lived, silent before His accusers, trusting the Father who judges justly.

### Commentary

**The Egypt plan (30:1–7).** "'Ah, stubborn children,' declares the LORD, 'who carry out a plan, but not mine, and who make an alliance, but not of my Spirit, that they may add sin to sin; who set out to go down to Egypt, without asking for my direction.'" The sin is not panic; it is planning without prayer — a whole foreign policy built without asking God. They send ambassadors to Zoan and Hanes, carrying riches on donkeys and camels to a people that cannot profit them. The verdict on Egypt is a nickname: "Rahab who sits still" — the great sea-monster of chaos, deflated to a do-nothing. "Egypt's help is worthless and empty." Judah will get shame and humiliation from the alliance, and nothing else. Isaiah keeps preaching the same sermon in new clothes: the help you scheme for, without God, costs treasure and returns embarrassment.

**The rebellious people and the written tablet (30:8–17).** God tells Isaiah to write it on a tablet and inscribe it in a book "that it may be for the time to come as a witness forever" — because this people "will not hear the law of the LORD," and they say to the prophets, "Do not prophesy to us what is right; speak to us smooth things, prophesy illusions." It is the first recorded demand for a seeker-sensitive pulpit, and Isaiah records God's answer in the people's own coming ruin: their iniquity will be "like a breach in a high wall, bulging out, and about to collapse, whose breaking comes suddenly, in an instant" — a potter's vessel smashed "so ruthlessly that among its fragments not a shard is found." Against all of it stands the refused offer, one of the greatest verses in the book: "In returning and rest you shall be saved; in quietness and in trust shall be your strength." The Hebrew word for returning, *shuvah*, is repentance itself — turning home. Salvation's address is return and rest; strength's address is quietness and trust. "But you were unwilling, and you said, 'No! We will flee upon horses.'" God's reply is a wordplay with a blade in it: flee upon horses — "therefore you shall flee"; "we will ride upon swift steeds" — "therefore your pursuers shall be swift." Our chosen speed becomes our pursuers' speed.

**The LORD waits to be gracious (30:18–26).** Then the chapter turns on a "therefore" of grace: "Therefore the LORD waits to be gracious to you." He waits — He longs, He delays on purpose — "to be gracious." The God of justice blesses those who wait for Him. And the promises cascade: the people shall dwell in Zion, weep no more; "he will surely be gracious to you at the sound of your cry. As soon as he hears it, he answers you." The bread of adversity and water of affliction may come, "but your Teacher will not hide himself anymore." Idols will be defiled and scattered — "Be gone!" — the sound of a people finally sick of their own gods. Rain for the seed, rich pasture, towers falling in the day of great slaughter, and a healed cosmos: "the light of the moon will be as the light of the sun, and the light of the sun will be sevenfold... in the day when the LORD binds up the brokenness of his people, and heals the wounds inflicted by his blow." God binds up what His own blow broke. The Surgeon and the Striker are the same merciful God.

**Assyria falls by God's breath (30:27–33).** The chapter ends with the LORD coming in burning anger, His voice like a flame of devouring fire, shaking the nations in the sieve of destruction, while His people sing "as in the night when a holy feast is kept." Assyria is beaten down by the rod, to the sound of tambourines and lyres — judgment as a worship service. And Topheth — the burning place in the valley of Hinnom — has long been prepared for the king, its pyre deep and wide, kindled by the breath of the LORD "like a stream of sulfur." Hell in Isaiah is not Satan's kingdom; it is God's fireplace, prepared by God for the proud. The breath that kindles it is the same breath that binds up the broken. Which breath a man meets depends on which side of return he stands.

### Key verses

> "For thus said the Lord GOD, the Holy One of Israel, 'In returning and rest you shall be saved; in quietness and in trust shall be your strength.' But you were unwilling." — Isaiah 30:15 (ESV)

> "Therefore the LORD waits to be gracious to you, and therefore he exalts himself to show mercy to you. For the LORD is a God of justice; blessed are all those who wait for him." — Isaiah 30:18 (ESV)

> "And though the Lord give you the bread of adversity and the water of affliction, yet your Teacher will not hide himself anymore, but your eyes shall see your Teacher. And your ears shall hear a word behind you, saying, 'This is the way, walk in it,' when you turn to the right or when you turn to the left." — Isaiah 30:20–21 (ESV)

### The bad news and the good news

The bad news of Isaiah 30 is that stubborn planning without God — alliances, schemes, smooth sermons on demand — ends in a wall collapsing in an instant and pursuers as swift as our own horses. The good news is that the LORD waits to be gracious: He exalts Himself to show mercy, answers as soon as He hears our cry, gives us a Teacher we can see and a voice we can hear, and binds up the brokenness His own blow inflicted. Returning and rest are still the address of salvation. He is still waiting. Do not make Him wait on you any longer than He already has.

**A note from the Reformers.** Spurgeon called Isaiah 30:18 the most surprising "therefore" in the Bible — after a chapter of rebellion, logic expects "therefore the LORD waits to destroy you," and instead we read that He waits to be gracious. That, Spurgeon said, is the difference between the law's arithmetic and the gospel's.

**Reflection question:** Where have you been riding swift horses — scheming, hurrying, planning without asking? What would "returning and rest" look like there, and will you take it?

---

## Isaiah 31 — Woe to Those Who Go Down to Egypt: Horses, Flesh, and the Lion over Zion

### Jesus in this chapter

Jesus is the LORD who comes down to fight on Mount Zion in this chapter — descending like a lion roaring over its prey, undeterred by the shouting of shepherds, and like birds hovering, protecting Jerusalem and rescuing her. The hovering bird is the image Jesus takes for Himself over the same city: "How often would I have gathered your children together as a hen gathers her brood under her wings" (Matthew 23:37). The God of Isaiah 31 protects by hovering — wings spread over the nest — and that is precisely what Christ longed to do for Jerusalem, and does for everyone who will be gathered. He is also the fire in Zion and the furnace in Jerusalem (31:9), the holy presence that consumes His enemies and warms His people.

### Commentary

**Woe to the Egypt-goers (31:1–3).** The woe is specific: "Woe to those who go down to Egypt for help and rely on horses, who trust in chariots because they are many and in horsemen because they are very strong, but do not look to the Holy One of Israel or consult the LORD!" The sin is not having horses; it is the direction of the look. They look down to Egypt instead of up to the Holy One. And Isaiah's argument is devastatingly simple: "The Egyptians are man, and not God, and their horses are flesh, and not spirit." Man and flesh versus God and Spirit — that is the whole comparison, and it is not close. "When the LORD stretches out his hand, the helper will stumble, and he who is helped will fall, and they will all perish together." Helper and helped, down in one heap. Alliances between two drowning parties do not produce a swimmer.

**The lion and the birds (31:4–5).** Then two of the boldest pictures of God in the Bible, back to back. First, the lion: "As a lion or a young lion growls over his prey, and when a band of shepherds is called out against him he is not terrified by their shouting or daunted at their noise, so the LORD of hosts will come down to fight on Mount Zion and on its hill." A whole band of shepherds shouting cannot move a lion off its prey; neither can the noise of nations move God off His purpose. Second, the birds: "Like birds hovering, so the LORD of hosts will protect Jerusalem; he will protect and deliver it; he will spare and rescue it." The Hebrew for "spare" is *pasoach* — from *pesach*, Passover. God will pass over Jerusalem as He passed over the blood-marked houses in Egypt. Lion and mother bird in consecutive verses: strength that cannot be intimidated, tenderness that cannot be distracted. That is the God who guards His people.

**Return, and the fall of Assyria (31:6–9).** The appeal follows: "Turn to him from whom people have deeply revolted, O children of Israel." On that day every man will cast away his idols of silver and gold, "which your hands have sinfully made for you" — the casting away is done by their own hands, the same hands that made them; repentance uses the same hands as sin did, in the opposite direction. "And the Assyrian shall fall by a sword, not of man; and a sword, not of man, shall devour him" — fulfilled when the angel of the LORD struck the Assyrian camp in a night (Isaiah 37:36), a sword no human forged. The proud king flees, his officers panic at the sight of the battle standard, and the chapter closes on God's own presence as the deciding factor: "whose fire is in Zion and whose furnace is in Jerusalem."

### Key verses

> "Woe to those who go down to Egypt for help and rely on horses, who trust in chariots because they are many and in horsemen because they are very strong, but do not look to the Holy One of Israel or consult the LORD!" — Isaiah 31:1 (ESV)

> "The Egyptians are man, and not God, and their horses are flesh, and not spirit. When the LORD stretches out his hand, the helper will stumble, and he who is helped will fall, and they will all perish together." — Isaiah 31:3 (ESV)

> "Like birds hovering, so the LORD of hosts will protect Jerusalem; he will protect and deliver it; he will spare and rescue it." — Isaiah 31:5 (ESV)

### The bad news and the good news

The bad news of Isaiah 31 is that flesh cannot rescue flesh — the helper stumbles and the helped fall in one heap when God stretches out His hand against our Egypt-plans. The good news is that the LORD comes down Himself: a lion no shouting can move, and a hovering bird no storm can scatter, protecting, delivering, sparing, rescuing. In Christ, God came down and hovered over His people at a cross — and everyone gathered under those wings is passed over in the judgment.

**A note from the Reformers.** Calvin dwelt on "the Egyptians are man, and not God" as the whole doctrine of trust in one sentence: faith's error is never that its object is too weak to notice, but that it is man and not God. Every disappointment in our helps is God teaching the difference between flesh and Spirit.

**Reflection question:** What helper are you looking to that is "man, and not God" — and what would turning your look to the Holy One of Israel change about your next decision?

---

## Isaiah 32 — Behold, a King Will Reign in Righteousness: Shelter, and the Spirit Poured Out

### Jesus in this chapter

Jesus is the King who reigns in righteousness at this chapter's head, and He is described with four shelter-images that every hurting person should memorize: "a hiding place from the wind, a shelter from the storm, like streams of water in a dry place, like the shade of a great rock in a weary land." Hiding place, shelter, streams, shade — that is Christ for weary people in a weary land, and the hymn-writers knew it ("Rock of Ages, cleft for me, let me hide myself in Thee"). He is also the One who pours out the Spirit "from on high" (32:15) — the promise Jesus claims at Pentecost's doorstep (Acts 2:33), turning the wilderness into a fruitful field. And the fruit of His reign is named plainly: "the effect of righteousness will be peace, and the result of righteousness, quietness and trust forever" — the same quietness and trust Judah refused in chapter 30, now given as a gift by the King.

### Commentary

**The King and His princes (32:1–8).** "Behold, a king will reign in righteousness, and princes will rule in justice." After chapters of failing kings — Uzziah dead, Ahaz cowardly, Hezekiah faltering — Isaiah lifts our eyes to a King who cannot fail. Under His reign the senses of the people heal: "the eyes of those who see will not be closed, and the ears of those who hear will give attention. The heart of the hasty will understand and know, and the tongue of the stammerers will hasten to speak distinctly." A stammerer's tongue made clear — the King's reign reaches even into how a man speaks. And the moral confusion of Isaiah 5:20 is reversed: "The fool will no more be called noble, nor the scoundrel said to be honorable." In this kingdom, things are called by their right names again. The fool's devices — scheming "to destroy the poor with lying words" — are exposed, "but he who is noble plans noble things, and on noble things he stands." Character becomes the currency of the realm.

**Complacent women and the coming trouble (32:9–14).** Before the glory, the warning: "Rise up, you women who are at ease, hear my voice; you complacent daughters, give ear to my speech." In a little more than a year, their vintage fails, the fields go untended, the joyful houses empty, and the palace is forsaken. Complacency — ease that assumes tomorrow is guaranteed — is Isaiah's target in every class of society. The city of mirth becomes caves and a haunt of wild donkeys "until" — and that word carries the next movement.

**Until the Spirit is poured out (32:15–20).** "Until the Spirit is poured upon us from on high, and the wilderness becomes a fruitful field, and the fruitful field is deemed a forest." Everything waits on that "until." Human reform cannot do it; invasion cannot do it; only the Spirit poured from on high turns wilderness into orchard. Then justice dwells in the wilderness and righteousness in the field — the King's character colonizing the land — and the harvest is peace: "My people will abide in a peaceful habitation, in secure dwellings, and in quiet resting places." Hail may flatten the forest and the city may be leveled, but "happy are you who sow beside all waters." The sowing continues through the storm, because the sower's security is not in the weather but in the King.

### Key verses

> "Behold, a king will reign in righteousness, and princes will rule in justice. Each will be like a hiding place from the wind, a shelter from the storm, like streams of water in a dry place, like the shade of a great rock in a weary land." — Isaiah 32:1–2 (ESV)

> "Until the Spirit is poured upon us from on high, and the wilderness becomes a fruitful field, and the fruitful field is deemed a forest. Then justice will dwell in the wilderness, and righteousness abide in the fruitful field. And the effect of righteousness will be peace, and the result of righteousness, quietness and trust forever." — Isaiah 32:15–17 (ESV)

### The bad news and the good news

The bad news of Isaiah 32 is that complacency is living on borrowed time — the vintage fails, the palace empties, and no human reign can make righteousness dwell in a wilderness. The good news is that a King has come who is Himself our hiding place, shelter, streams, and shade, and He pours out His Spirit from on high until wildernesses become orchards and the fruit of righteousness is peace, quietness, and trust forever. If your life is a weary land, there is a great Rock in it, and His shadow is big enough.

**A note from the Reformers.** Spurgeon preached the four shelter-images of verse 2 as four sermons for four kinds of trouble — wind, storm, drought, and heat — and one Christ sufficient for all of them. The King's reign, he said, is not first a matter of geography but of refuge: He reigns wherever a sinner hides in Him.

**Reflection question:** Which shelter do you need most today — hiding place from the wind, shelter from the storm, streams in a dry place, or shade in a weary land? What would hiding in the King look like, practically, this week?

---

## Isaiah 33 — The LORD Is Our King: Terror, Then the City at Peace

### Jesus in this chapter

Jesus is the King seen in His beauty in this chapter — "Your eyes will behold the king in his beauty; they will see a land that stretches afar" — and He is the whole government of His people in one verse: "For the LORD is our judge; the LORD is our lawgiver; the LORD is our king; he will save us." Judge, lawgiver, king, and Savior in a single Person — no separation of powers in heaven. He is also the stability of our times and the abundance of salvation: "he will be the stability of your times, abundance of salvation, wisdom, and knowledge; the fear of the LORD is Zion's treasure." And the chapter's lame taking the prey (33:23) reaches forward to the lame man leaping in Acts 3 and to Jesus' own summary of His ministry: "the lame walk" (Matthew 11:5).

### Commentary

**Woe to the destroyer (33:1–6).** The sixth woe falls on Assyria without naming her: "Ah, you destroyer, who yourself have not been destroyed, you traitor, whom none has betrayed! When you have ceased destroying, you will be destroyed." Treachery has an expiration date set by heaven. Against the destroyer rises the people's prayer — one of the great short prayers of the Bible: "O LORD, be gracious to us; we wait for you. Be our arm every morning, our salvation in the time of trouble." *Every morning* — grace asked for daily, like manna, because trouble does not keep office hours. At the thunder of God's voice the peoples flee and the nations scatter, and the spoil is gathered "as the caterpillar gathers." Then the stability verse (33:6): in a chapter about invasion, the treasure of Zion is not gold but the fear of the LORD. Nations stockpile weapons; Zion stockpiles reverence — and reverence turns out to be the stability of the times.

**The highway of holiness previewed (33:7–16).** The present looks bleak: ambassadors of peace weep bitterly, highways lie waste, the land mourns and languishes, Sharon is like a desert. God arises: "Now I will arise... Now I will lift myself up." The chaff and stubble conceive and give birth — human scheming produces combustible babies — and God's breath, "a fire that will consume you," turns on the peoples. Then the question that echoes Psalm 15 and Psalm 24: "Who among us can dwell with the consuming fire? Who among us can dwell with everlasting burnings?" The answer is a character portrait: "He who walks righteously and speaks uprightly, who despises the gain of oppressions, who shakes his hands, lest they hold a bribe, who stops his ears from hearing of bloodshed and shuts his eyes from looking on evil — he will dwell on the heights; his place of defense will be the fortresses of rocks; his bread will be given him; his water will be sure." Dwell with a consuming-fire God? Only the righteous can — which is why we need a righteousness not our own, and bread and water given, not earned.

**The King in His beauty (33:17–24).** Then the vision turns: eyes behold the King, the terrors are remembered like a bad dream — "Where is he who counted, where is he who weighed the tribute?" — the insolent enemy is gone, and Zion stands: "Look on Zion, the city of our appointed feasts! Your eyes will see Jerusalem, an untroubled habitation, an immovable tent, whose stakes will never be plucked up." On its rivers no warship rows; "the LORD in majesty will be for us a place of broad rivers and streams." The tackle hangs loose, the mast is unsteady — the ship of state looks unseaworthy — "then prey and spoil in abundance will be divided; even the lame will take the prey." And the last word of the chapter is the best: "And no inhabitant will say, 'I am sick'; the people who dwell there will be forgiven their iniquity." Sickness and sin dealt with together, forgiveness as the final fact about the city. Mark 2 in advance: the paralytic's deepest need, met first.

### Key verses

> "O LORD, be gracious to us; we wait for you. Be our arm every morning, our salvation in the time of trouble." — Isaiah 33:2 (ESV)

> "And he will be the stability of your times, abundance of salvation, wisdom, and knowledge; the fear of the LORD is Zion's treasure." — Isaiah 33:6 (ESV)

> "For the LORD is our judge; the LORD is our lawgiver; the LORD is our king; he will save us." — Isaiah 33:22 (ESV)

> "And no inhabitant will say, 'I am sick'; the people who dwell there will be forgiven their iniquity." — Isaiah 33:24 (ESV)

### The bad news and the good news

The bad news of Isaiah 33 is that destroyers and traitors have a set date with destruction, and that no one can dwell with the consuming fire on the strength of his own righteousness — our hands have held what they should have shaken off. The good news is that the LORD is our judge, lawgiver, king, and Savior in one Person: He is the stability of our times, our arm every morning, and in His city the lame take the prey and the forgiven never say "I am sick" again. In Christ, the consuming fire has become our King in His beauty.

**A note from the Reformers.** Calvin fastened on verse 22 as the charter of the church's comfort: because the same LORD is judge, lawgiver, and king, believers are not handed from one power to another — the One who made the law and judges by it is the very One who saves. All the offices meet in Christ, and they meet for us.

**Reflection question:** Do you need God most today as judge, lawgiver, king, or Savior — and what keeps you from letting Him be all four? What would "be our arm every morning" look like as a daily prayer?

---
## Isaiah 34 — The LORD Has a Sword: Judgment on Edom and All Nations

### Jesus in this chapter

Jesus is the Judge with the sword in this chapter — "the LORD has a sword; it is sated with blood" — and He is the One who will one day tread the winepress of the fierceness of God's wrath, His robe spattered, in imagery Revelation 19 borrows directly from Isaiah 34 and 63. Edom stands here for every nation and every heart that makes itself God's permanent enemy: "For the LORD has a day of vengeance, a year of recompense for the cause of Zion." The cause of Zion is defended by a Person. It matters that the Judge is Jesus — the same hands that were pierced hold the sword — because it means judgment is not the opposite of His love but the other side of His holiness, and no wrong against His people goes unanswered forever.

### Commentary

**The summons to all nations (34:1–4).** "Draw near, O nations, to hear, and give attention, O peoples! Let the earth hear, and all that fills it." The audience is universal because the indictment is: "For the LORD is enraged against all the nations, and furious against all their host; he has devoted them to destruction, has given them over for slaughter." Edom becomes the representative case — Israel's twin brother turned perpetual enemy — the way Babylon represented arrogant empire. The cosmic signs return: "All the host of heaven shall rot away, and the skies roll up like a scroll." Peter and John both inherit this sky (2 Peter 3:10; Revelation 6:14). The old creation itself is perishable; only the kingdom that cannot be shaken remains.

**The sword in Edom (34:5–15).** "For my sword has drunk its fill in the heavens; behold, it descends for judgment upon Edom, upon the people I have devoted to destruction." The sacrifice language is deliberate and terrible: "The LORD has a sacrifice in Bozrah, a great slaughter in the land of Edom" — wild oxen, bulls, and bullocks falling, the land soaked with blood, the soil made rich with fat. This is judgment described as an anti-feast: where chapter 25 set God's table for the nations, chapter 34 shows the table of wrath for the impenitent. Then the desolation inventory: thorns in the palaces, jackals and ostriches in the strongholds, the night bird (*lilith*, the screech owl of older translations) finding her rest. "Seek and read from the book of the LORD" — Isaiah invites verification: not one of these creatures shall be missing; God's mouth commanded it and His Spirit gathered them. Prophecy this specific is meant to be checked.

**It shall not be quenched (34:16–17).** "Its smoke shall go up forever; from generation to generation it shall lie waste; none shall pass through it forever and ever." Edom's land south of the Dead Sea is, to this day, a byword for desolation. God's lots are cast by His own hand, and the line He measures does not get redrawn by time. The chapter is short, severe, and meant to be read standing next to chapter 35 — because Isaiah never leaves judgment as the final landscape. The same "forever" that seals Edom's waste opens into the ransomed singing on a highway. Read them together, the way Isaiah bound them together, or you will misread both.

### Key verses

> "For the LORD is enraged against all the nations, and furious against all their host; he has devoted them to destruction, has given them over for slaughter." — Isaiah 34:2 (ESV)

> "For my sword has drunk its fill in the heavens; behold, it descends for judgment upon Edom, upon the people I have devoted to destruction." — Isaiah 34:5 (ESV)

> "Seek and read from the book of the LORD: Not one of these shall be missing; none shall be without her mate. For the mouth of the LORD has commanded, and his Spirit has gathered them." — Isaiah 34:16 (ESV)

### The bad news and the good news

The bad news of Isaiah 34 is that God's vengeance has a day and a year, His sword does not miss, and the Edom-heart — permanent, nursing enmity against God and His people — ends in smoke that rises forever. The good news is that this judgment is "for the cause of Zion": the Judge is not indifferent to what was done to His people, and the same book that seals Edom's waste opens, in the very next chapter, a highway home for the ransomed. Justice and mercy are not rivals in Isaiah. They are consecutive chapters.

**A note from the Reformers.** Calvin warned against reading chapters like this with either embarrassment or glee: the church neither softens God's vengeance nor savors it, but trembles at it — and flees to the mercy that the very next chapter holds out. The Reformers' rule was to preach the whole counsel, Edom and highway together.

**Reflection question:** Is there an "Edom" grudge in you — an enmity you have nursed so long it feels like identity? What would handing the cause over to God's day of vengeance free you to do instead?

---

## Isaiah 35 — The Highway of Holiness: The Ransomed Shall Return

### Jesus in this chapter

Jesus is the glory of this chapter — the glory of Lebanon given to the desert is the glory of the LORD Himself, and "your God will come... He will come and save you" is the promise Jesus was born to keep. When John the Baptist's disciples asked if Jesus was the Coming One, Jesus answered with this chapter: "The blind receive their sight and the lame walk, lepers are cleansed and the deaf hear" (Matthew 11:5) — Isaiah 35:5–6 performed in person. Jesus is also the Way of Holiness itself: "And a highway shall be there, and it shall be called the Way of Holiness" — and He would later say, "I am the way" (John 14:6). The ransomed who walk that highway singing, with everlasting joy on their heads, are His purchased people, coming home to Zion because He ransomed them.

### Commentary

**The desert blooms (35:1–2).** "The wilderness and the dry land shall be glad; the desert shall rejoice and blossom like the crocus; it shall blossom abundantly and rejoice with joy and singing." Isaiah sets this blooming directly after Edom's smoking waste, and the contrast is the message: God's judgment is not His hobby; creation's renewal is His goal. The glory of Lebanon, the majesty of Carmel and Sharon — the most fertile places known — are given to the desert. "They shall see the glory of the LORD, the majesty of our God." The blossoming is not landscaping; it is revelation. When God restores, the land itself becomes a sermon.

**Strengthen the weak hands (35:3–4).** Then a commission for the strong-hearted to carry to the faint-hearted: "Strengthen the weak hands, and make firm the feeble knees. Say to those who have an anxious heart, 'Be strong; fear not! Behold, your God will come with vengeance, with the recompense of God. He will come and save you.'" Hebrews 12:12 quotes the weak hands and feeble knees to tired Christians and tells them to lift them up. Notice the message to anxious hearts is not "try harder" but "Behold, your God will come" — courage is a matter of Who is coming, not of how we feel. Vengeance and salvation arrive in the same Person on the same day: He comes to repay His enemies and to rescue His anxious ones. If your heart is anxious today, this verse was written as a script for what other believers should say to you — and what you can say to yourself.

**Eyes opened, ears unstopped, lame leaping (35:5–7).** "Then the eyes of the blind shall be opened, and the ears of the deaf unstopped; then shall the lame man leap like a deer, and the tongue of the mute sing for joy." The mute singing — Isaiah loves that detail: the organ of silence becomes the organ of praise. "For waters break forth in the wilderness, and streams in the desert; the burning sand shall become a pool, and the thirsty ground springs of water." In the haunt of jackals — chapter 34's desolation fauna — grass and reeds and rushes grow. God's restoration does not avoid the jackal country. It irrigates it.

**The Way of Holiness (35:8–10).** "And a highway shall be there, and it shall be called the Way of Holiness; the unclean shall not pass over it. It shall belong to those who walk on the way; even if they are fools, they shall not go astray." Even fools cannot get lost on this road — the way is so plain, so well-kept by God, that the simple are safe on it. "No lion shall be there, nor shall any ravenous beast come up on it" — the dangers of the old road are banned from the new one. "And the ransomed of the LORD shall return and come to Zion with singing; everlasting joy shall be upon their heads; they shall obtain gladness and joy, and sorrow and sighing shall flee away." Ransomed — bought back, paid for. The Hebrew word is *padah*, the word for redeeming a slave or a firstborn with a price. The singers on the highway are not escapees; they are purchases. Someone paid. And their sorrow and sighing do not merely stop — they *flee*, chased off the road by joy.

### Key verses

> "Strengthen the weak hands, and make firm the feeble knees. Say to those who have an anxious heart, 'Be strong; fear not! Behold, your God will come with vengeance, with the recompense of God. He will come and save you.'" — Isaiah 35:3–4 (ESV)

> "Then the eyes of the blind shall be opened, and the ears of the deaf unstopped; then shall the lame man leap like a deer, and the tongue of the mute sing for joy." — Isaiah 35:5–6 (ESV)

> "And a highway shall be there, and it shall be called the Way of Holiness; the unclean shall not pass over it. It shall belong to those who walk on the way; even if they are fools, they shall not go astray." — Isaiah 35:8 (ESV)

> "And the ransomed of the LORD shall return and come to Zion with singing; everlasting joy shall be upon their heads; they shall obtain gladness and joy, and sorrow and sighing shall flee away." — Isaiah 35:10 (ESV)

### The bad news and the good news

The bad news of Isaiah 35 is that left in the desert, we are the blind, the deaf, the lame, and the mute — weak hands, feeble knees, anxious hearts — with jackals in the ruins and no water anywhere. The good news is that God comes — He Himself, not a substitute — opens what was shut, makes the lame leap and the mute sing, builds a highway so plain that fools cannot lose it, and ransoms a people who return singing while sorrow and sighing run away. In Jesus, the Coming One has come. The highway is open.

**A note from the Reformers.** Spurgeon called Isaiah 35 the chapter to give a discouraged believer on a Saturday night: God's message to anxious hearts is never a lecture on their anxiety but a look at their coming God — "Behold, your God will come... He will come and save you." The cure for fear is a Person arriving.

**Reflection question:** Which description fits you today — weak hands, feeble knees, anxious heart, or one of the shut senses? What does it mean for you that the message is not "fix yourself" but "your God will come"?

---

## Isaiah 36 — The Rabshakeh at the Wall: Whom Will You Trust?

### Jesus in this chapter

Jesus is the LORD whose honor is at stake in this chapter — the God the Rabshakeh blasphemes and Hezekiah trusts. The Assyrian field commander's whole speech is one question, asked with a sneer: "On what do you rest this trust of yours?" That question is the hinge of Isaiah 36–39 and, honestly, of every crisis in every believer's life. The chapter also sets up the blasphemy that God answers personally in chapter 37 — and the pattern is Christ's pattern: the enemy mocks, the king tears his clothes and goes into the house of the LORD, and the LORD fights. When the Sanhedrin and the soldiers mocked Jesus on the cross — "He trusts in God; let God deliver him now" (Matthew 27:43) — they were speaking the Rabshakeh's lines, and God's answer then was resurrection.

### Commentary

**The siege and the spokesman (36:1–3).** "In the fourteenth year of King Hezekiah, Sennacherib king of Assyria came up against all the fortified cities of Judah and took them." The year is 701 BC. Lachish has fallen; Jerusalem is next. The Rabshakeh — the king's chief spokesman — stands by the conduit of the upper pool, the very spot where Isaiah met Ahaz a generation earlier (Isaiah 7:3). Same location, same question, new king: will this son of David trust, where his father would not? Eliakim — the peg of chapter 22, now steward — comes out to meet him, with Shebna demoted to secretary beside him, exactly as Isaiah foretold.

**The speech: doubt the help, doubt the king, doubt the God (36:4–20).** The Rabshakeh's speech is a masterpiece of psychological warfare, delivered in Hebrew so the men on the wall can hear every word. First, Egypt: "Behold, you are trusting in Egypt, that broken reed of a staff, which will pierce the hand of any man who leans on it." Even Assyria's spokesman preaches Isaiah's doctrine of Egypt. Second, Hezekiah's reforms are twisted: hasn't Hezekiah removed the high places — hasn't he offended God himself? Third, the mocking wager: Assyria will give Judah two thousand horses if Judah can find riders for them. Fourth, the theological claim: "Is it without the LORD that I have come up against this land to destroy it? The LORD said to me, 'Go up against this land and destroy it.'" Even the enemy claims God's authorization. And finally the offer, in the hearing of the whole wall: make peace, surrender, and enjoy your own vine and fig tree — until deportation. "Do not let Hezekiah make you trust in the LORD by saying, 'The LORD will surely deliver us.'" And the closer, the roll call of dead gods: "Has any of the gods of the nations delivered his land out of the hand of the king of Assyria? Where are the gods of Hamath and Arpad?" Every crisis has a Rabshakeh — a voice, fluent in our own language, arguing that trust in God is the one option that has been tried and found wanting.

**Silence and torn clothes (36:21–22).** The people's answer is the wisest move in the chapter: "But they were silent and answered him not a word, for the king's command was, 'Do not answer him.'" Some speeches are not dignified with debate. And the officials return to Hezekiah "with torn clothes" and tell him the words. Torn clothes are the right first response to blasphemy — grief, not argument. Chapter 37 will show the second response: the house of the LORD, sackcloth, and Isaiah. When the mockery is loudest, the pattern is silence before men and speech before God.

### Key verses

> "And the Rabshakeh said to them, 'Say to Hezekiah, "Thus says the great king, the king of Assyria: On what do you rest this trust of yours? Do you think that mere words are strategy and power for war? In whom do you now trust, that you have rebelled against me?"'" — Isaiah 36:4–5 (ESV)

> "Do not let Hezekiah make you trust in the LORD by saying, 'The LORD will surely deliver us. This city will not be given into the hand of the king of Assyria.'" — Isaiah 36:18 (ESV)

> "But they were silent and answered him not a word, for the king's command was, 'Do not answer him.'" — Isaiah 36:21 (ESV)

### The bad news and the good news

The bad news of Isaiah 36 is that the enemy's voice is fluent, reasonable-sounding, and aimed precisely at our trust — it will quote our failures, mock our reforms, claim God's side, and offer a comfortable surrender, all in our own language. The good news is that the question "On what do you rest this trust of yours?" has an answer the Rabshakeh cannot refute, and the king who tears his clothes and goes to God's house will find that God defends His own name. You do not have to win the argument at the wall. You have to take the letter to the right address — which is chapter 37.

**A note from the Reformers.** Luther, who knew the devil's rhetoric personally, treated the Rabshakeh as a portrait of temptation itself: it always speaks our language, always argues from our circumstances, and always ends by forbidding us to trust in the LORD. His counsel matched Hezekiah's — do not debate it at the wall; carry it into the house of God.

**Reflection question:** What is your Rabshakeh saying right now — in your own language, with reasonable arguments? Will you answer it with silence before men and prayer before God, instead of surrender or debate?

---

## Isaiah 37 — The Letter Spread Before the LORD: 185,000 in One Night

### Jesus in this chapter

Jesus is the God who hears prayer in this chapter — "O LORD of hosts, God of Israel, enthroned above the cherubim, you are the God, you alone, of all the kingdoms of the earth" — and who answers blasphemy not with argument but with deliverance: "For I will defend this city to save it, for my own sake and for the sake of my servant David." The zeal of the LORD of hosts performs it. This is the God and Father of Jesus Christ, and the pattern of Hezekiah's prayer — spreading the enemy's letter before the LORD in His house — is the pattern Jesus teaches His own: bring the actual threat, name it plainly, ground your plea in God's glory, and leave the outcome on the altar. The angel who struck the Assyrian camp is the LORD's own agent; the risen Christ is the final answer to every Rabshakeh, God's "I have heard you" spoken in an empty tomb.

### Commentary

**Sackcloth and Isaiah (37:1–7).** Hezekiah tears his clothes, covers himself with sackcloth, and goes into the house of the LORD — grief moving in the right direction. He sends Eliakim and the senior priests, also in sackcloth, to Isaiah with a confession and a request: "This day is a day of distress, of rebuke, and of disgrace; children have come to the point of birth, and there is no strength to bring them forth." A nation in labor with no strength — that is honest prayer language. "It may be that the LORD your God will hear the words of the Rabshakeh... and will rebuke them. Therefore lift up your prayer for the remnant that is left." Isaiah's answer is swift: "Do not be afraid because of the words that you have heard... Behold, I will put a spirit in him, so that he shall hear a rumor and return to his own land. And I will make him fall by the sword in his own land." God answers the prayer before the crisis even peaks. Fear not — the outcome is already being arranged in Nineveh.

**The letter spread out (37:8–20).** Sennacherib, diverted by a rumor of Cushite advance, sends a letter — the mockery now in writing: "Do not let your God in whom you trust deceive you." Hezekiah's response is one of the great prayer scenes in Scripture: he goes up to the house of the LORD, "and spread it before the LORD." He does not summarize it, soften it, or answer it. He lays the actual document on the altar and prays over it. And the prayer is a model: it begins with who God is — enthroned above the cherubim, God alone of all kingdoms, maker of heaven and earth. It concedes the facts: "Truly, O LORD, the kings of Assyria have laid waste all the nations and their lands." Faith does not deny the casualty reports. Then the distinction: the gods of the nations were no gods, "but the work of men's hands, wood and stone" — that is why they could be destroyed. And the petition, grounded in glory: "So now, O LORD our God, save us from his hand, that all the kingdoms of the earth may know that you alone are the LORD." Hezekiah asks for deliverance as evangelism. Prayers that end in God's fame get heaven's attention.

**The oracle and the night of the angel (37:21–38).** Isaiah's answer comes back with a taunt song against Sennacherib — the virgin daughter of Zion despises him and shakes her head — and God's own cross-examination: "Whom have you mocked and reviled? Against whom have you raised your voice?... Against the Holy One of Israel!" Blasphemy is the charge; the hook in the nose and the bridle in the lips are the sentence — God will lead the proud king home by the way he came, like a beast. Then the sign and the promise: "For out of Jerusalem shall go a remnant, and out of Mount Zion a band of survivors. The zeal of the LORD of hosts will do this." And the fulfillment is one sentence of terrifying quiet: "And the angel of the LORD went out and struck down 185,000 in the camp of the Assyrians. And when people arose early in the morning, behold, these were all dead bodies." No battle. No casualties on Jerusalem's side. A city asleep behind its walls wakes up saved. Sennacherib goes home and is murdered by his own sons in the temple of his god — the god who could not save him from the sword Isaiah predicted. The God of Israel defends His city "for my own sake and for the sake of my servant David" — grace grounded in God's name and God's covenant, not in Jerusalem's worthiness.

### Key verses

> "And Hezekiah received the letter from the hand of the messengers, and read it; and Hezekiah went up to the house of the LORD, and spread it before the LORD." — Isaiah 37:14 (ESV)

> "O LORD of hosts, God of Israel, enthroned above the cherubim, you are the God, you alone, of all the kingdoms of the earth; you have made heaven and earth... So now, O LORD our God, save us from his hand, that all the kingdoms of the earth may know that you alone are the LORD." — Isaiah 37:16, 20 (ESV)

> "For I will defend this city to save it, for my own sake and for the sake of my servant David." — Isaiah 37:35 (ESV)

> "And the angel of the LORD went out and struck down 185,000 in the camp of the Assyrians. And when people arose early in the morning, behold, these were all dead bodies." — Isaiah 37:36 (ESV)

### The bad news and the good news

The bad news of Isaiah 37 is that some threats are real, written, and overwhelming — 185,000 soldiers do not care about our feelings, and our strength to bring forth deliverance is, honestly, none. The good news is that prayer is not a ritual for the strong but a spreading of the letter before the LORD by the weak — and the God enthroned above the cherubim defends His people for His own sake, in His own night, by His own angel. Jerusalem slept and woke up saved. The zeal of the LORD of hosts still does this.

**A note from the Reformers.** Spurgeon preached Hezekiah's spreading of the letter as the model of prayer for every believer with an unbearable document — a diagnosis, a debt, a threat: do not merely think about it; take it up to the house of the LORD, spread it out in full, and let God read what you cannot answer.

**Reflection question:** What "letter" are you carrying right now that you have read and re-read alone? What would it mean to spread that exact thing before the LORD this week — unsummarized, unsoftened — and leave it on the altar?

---

## Isaiah 38 — Fifteen Years Added: Hezekiah's Sickness and Song

### Jesus in this chapter

Jesus is the God who hears prayer and sees tears in this chapter — "I have heard your prayer; I have seen your tears. Behold, I will add fifteen years to your life" — and He is the one who turns the shadow backward on the steps as a sign. The God who can reverse a shadow can reverse a sentence. Hezekiah's recovery also sets up a greater picture: a king who was as good as dead, weeping at his turning to the wall, raised up to go to the house of the LORD on the third day — a small, human echo of the resurrection pattern that Jesus would fulfill in full. And Hezekiah's song line — "The living, the living, he thanks you, as I do this day" — is the charter of every testimony: the living thank Him, out loud, on purpose.

### Commentary

**The sentence and the tears (38:1–6).** "In those days Hezekiah became sick and was at the point of death. And Isaiah the prophet... came to him and said to him, 'Thus says the LORD: Set your house in order, for you shall die, you shall not recover.'" Prophets do not soften sentences; Isaiah delivers it flat.
