Hezekiah's response is immediate and unvarnished: "Then Hezekiah turned his face to the wall and prayed to the LORD" — a dying man's whole theology in one turn — pleading his walk of faithfulness and whole heart, and weeping bitterly. Before Isaiah has crossed the middle court on his way out, the word of the LORD turns him around: "Go and say to Hezekiah, Thus says the LORD, the God of David your father: I have heard your prayer; I have seen your tears. Behold, I will add fifteen years to your life. I will deliver you and this city out of the hand of the king of Assyria, and will defend this city." Prayer heard, tears seen, years added, city defended — four gifts in one answer. God is not embarrassed by a king's tears. He counts them as evidence and answers them as a Father.

**The sign of the backward shadow (38:7–8).** Hezekiah asks for a sign, and God gives him a choice of cosmic proportions: shall the shadow on the steps of Ahaz go forward ten steps or back ten steps? Hezekiah reasons that forward is the shadow's natural way — "it is an easy thing for the shadow to lengthen ten steps" — so let it go back. And it does: the sun's shadow retreats up the staircase. The God who made light on day one can bend its shadow on a sick king's request. A small sign with a large meaning: the God who can reverse a shadow can reverse a death sentence, and the fifteen years are His to give.

**The writing of Hezekiah (38:9–20).** Then we get the king's own psalm, written after his recovery — a rare thing, a testimony in the man's own words. He remembers the despair honestly: "I said, In the middle of my days I must depart; I am consigned to the gates of Sheol for the rest of my years." He remembers feeling cut off like a weaver's cloth, rolled up by God. He remembers chattering "like a swallow or a crane... like a dove" — inarticulate grief, moaning instead of praying. And he lands on the doctrine every sufferer needs: "Behold, it was for my welfare that I had great bitterness; but in love you have delivered my life from the pit of destruction, for you have cast all my sins behind your back." Behind God's back — where He puts the sins of His people, out of His own sight. "For Sheol does not thank you; death does not praise you... The living, the living, he thanks you, as I do this day; the father makes known to the children your faithfulness." And the practical note at the end is almost comic in its humility: the miraculous recovery is treated with "a cake of figs" laid on the boil. God heals with angels in chapter 37 and with fig cakes in chapter 38. Means are means; the Healer is the same.

### Key verses

> "Then Hezekiah turned his face to the wall and prayed to the LORD, saying, 'Please, O LORD, remember how I have walked before you in faithfulness and with a whole heart, and have done what is good in your sight.' And Hezekiah wept bitterly." — Isaiah 38:2–3 (ESV)

> "I have heard your prayer; I have seen your tears. Behold, I will add fifteen years to your life." — Isaiah 38:5 (ESV)

> "Behold, it was for my welfare that I had great bitterness; but in love you have delivered my life from the pit of destruction, for you have cast all my sins behind your back." — Isaiah 38:17 (ESV)

### The bad news and the good news

The bad news of Isaiah 38 is that even faithful kings get death sentences — sickness does not check our record before it visits, and the middle of our days can feel like the end of them. The good news is that God hears prayer and sees tears, adds years by His own free gift, casts sins behind His back, and turns bitterness into welfare. The God who reversed the shadow for Hezekiah raised His Son on the third day — and no sentence spoken over His people is final until He says so.

**A note from the Reformers.** Calvin lingered on "you have cast all my sins behind your back" as a picture of justification's completeness: God does not file our sins where He must keep seeing them; He puts them where He will not look — behind Him. That is why Hezekiah can sing. Forgiven people sing.

**Reflection question:** What sentence are you living under right now — medical, financial, relational — and have you turned your face to the wall with it? What would you say to God if you believed He counts tears as carefully as Hezekiah's?

---

## Isaiah 39 — Hezekiah Shows Everything: The Seed of Exile

### Jesus in this chapter

Jesus is present in this chapter by His absence from Hezekiah's conversation — and that is the lesson. The envoys from Babylon come, and Hezekiah shows them everything: treasure, armory, storehouses, "all that was found in his storehouses. There was nothing in his house or in all his realm that Hezekiah did not show them." Not once in the chapter does Hezekiah mention the God who healed him, defended his city, and turned the shadow backward. The King who should have been the sermon is missing from the tour. Against that silence stands Jesus, who never made that mistake — who told the healed demoniac, "Go home to your friends and tell them how much the Lord has done for you" (Mark 5:19). Chapter 39 is the hinge of the whole book: pride after mercy plants the seed of exile, and it is why chapters 40–66 must speak of a Servant greater than Hezekiah, who would show the nations not his treasures but his wounds.

### Commentary

**The flattering embassy (39:1–2).** "At that time Merodach-baladan the son of Baladan, king of Babylon, sent envoys with letters and a present to Hezekiah, for he heard that he had been sick and had recovered." Babylon is still a rising rebel against Assyria, fishing for allies — and flattery is its bait: a get-well letter and a gift. "And Hezekiah welcomed them gladly, and showed them his treasure house, the silver, the gold, the spices, the precious oil, his whole armory, all that was found in his storehouses." Gladly. The king who wept so well in chapter 38 boasts so easily in chapter 39. Gratitude has a short shelf life when flattery arrives; a man can be on his face before God in one chapter and on a guided tour of his own glory in the next. The envoys came, ostensibly, about his sickness — the perfect opening to speak of the God who adds fifteen years. Hezekiah showed them the silver instead.

**The prophet's questions (39:3–4).** Isaiah's interrogation is gentle and total: "What did these men say? And from where did they come to you?... What have they seen in your house?" Hezekiah answers with the whole indictment in his own mouth: "They have seen all that is in my house. There is nothing in my storehouses that I did not show them." All. Nothing withheld. The verb "show" tolls through the chapter like a bell. What a man shows off tells you what he trusts; Hezekiah, delivered by grace, displayed his assets. Isaiah does not rage. He simply asks questions until the king convicts himself — the prophet's method with proud men in every age.

**The sentence and the shrug (39:5–8).** "Hear the word of the LORD of hosts: Behold, the days are coming, when all that is in your house, and that which your fathers have stored up till this day, shall be carried to Babylon. Nothing shall be left... And some of your own sons, who will come from you, whom you will father, shall be taken away, and they shall be eunuchs in the palace of the king of Babylon." Daniel and his friends are in that sentence. The treasures Hezekiah displayed will make the trip he previewed. And Hezekiah's response is the saddest relief in the Old Testament: "The word of the LORD that you have spoken is good." For he thought, "There will be peace and security in my days." In my days. The judgment is real, the sons are his, the exile is certain — and the king is comforted that it will not happen on his watch. Isaiah records it without comment, and the silence is the comment. This is where the first movement of the book ends: not with Assyria at the gates, but with pride in the palace and exile in the future — and it is precisely here, with everything Hezekiah showed about to be carried away, that chapter 40 opens its mouth: "Comfort, comfort my people."

### Key verses

> "And Hezekiah welcomed them gladly, and showed them his treasure house, the silver, the gold, the spices, the precious oil, his whole armory, all that was found in his storehouses. There was nothing in his house or in all his realm that Hezekiah did not show them." — Isaiah 39:2 (ESV)

> "Behold, the days are coming, when all that is in your house, and that which your fathers have stored up till this day, shall be carried to Babylon. Nothing shall be left, says the LORD." — Isaiah 39:6 (ESV)

> "Then Hezekiah said to Isaiah, 'The word of the LORD that you have spoken is good.' For he thought, 'There will be peace and security in my days.'" — Isaiah 39:8 (ESV)

### The bad news and the good news

The bad news of Isaiah 39 is that a man can survive the siege and the sickness and still lose the testimony — flattered into displaying his treasures instead of declaring his God, and comforted by a judgment that falls on his children instead of himself. The good news is that God's book does not end with Hezekiah's shrug: the exile this chapter plants is the very exile chapters 40–66 will answer with comfort, a Servant, and a homecoming. God's mercy outruns His people's worst chapters. The hinge swings toward grace.

**A note from the Reformers.** Calvin called Hezekiah's "in my days" the most selfish sentence a godly man ever spoke — proof that even true believers, left to themselves, will trade their children's future for their own quiet. It stands in the book as a warning that grace received must become grace declared, or it curdles into pride within a single chapter.

**Reflection question:** When God has delivered you — healed, rescued, provided — what do you show people: your treasures or your God? And is there a place where "at least it won't happen in my days" has replaced concern for those coming after you?

---
## Isaiah 40 — Comfort, Comfort My People: The Voice, the Creator, and Wings Like Eagles

### Jesus in this chapter

This chapter is the front door of the gospel in the Old Testament, and Jesus stands in every doorway of it. He is the Lord whose way the voice prepares — John the Baptist takes Isaiah 40:3 as his own job description (Matthew 3:3), and the glory revealed is Christ's. He is the Word of God that stands forever while the grass withers — the Word made flesh. He is the Shepherd of verse 11, who "will tend his flock like a shepherd; he will gather the lambs in his arms" — Jesus' own picture of Himself (John 10:11). And He is the coming God of verse 10, who comes with might and brings His reward with Him — the same Jesus who says in Revelation 22:12, "Behold, I am coming soon, bringing my recompense with me." Isaiah 40 is where the book turns from indictment to embrace, and the embrace has a name.

### Commentary

**The commission of comfort (40:1–2).** "Comfort, comfort my people, says your God. Speak tenderly to Jerusalem, and cry to her that her warfare is ended, that her iniquity is pardoned, that she has received from the LORD's hand double for all her sins." Doubled words for a doubled need: comfort, comfort. The Hebrew verb *nachamu* means to console deeply, to help someone breathe again. "Speak tenderly" is literally "speak to the heart" — the language of a suitor and of Hosea's God wooing His wife back in the wilderness. The message is threefold: warfare ended, iniquity pardoned, penalty paid in full — "double" not as double punishment but as a receipt stamped paid beyond measure, the way a debt note was folded double and nailed up when discharged. Isaiah 40 opens where every sermon to broken people should open: the war is over, the sin is pardoned, and God says so first.

**The voice and the highway (40:3–5).** "A voice cries: 'In the wilderness prepare the way of the LORD; make straight in the desert a highway for our God.'" Every valley lifted, every mountain made low, the uneven ground leveled — royal road-building for a King coming home with His exiles. "And the glory of the LORD shall be revealed, and all flesh shall see it together, for the mouth of the LORD has spoken." All flesh, together — the audience of this revelation is the whole human race. John the Baptist stood in the actual wilderness, in the actual Jordan valley, and said: that's me, that's the voice, and the King is right behind me. When the King came, the glory revealed turned out to be a Man who could be touched, crucified, and raised — and all flesh has been seeing it together ever since.

**The word that stands forever (40:6–8).** A second voice cries, "All flesh is grass" — human glory like the flower of the field, beautiful and brief, withered by the breath of the LORD. Peter quotes this to Christians scattered and suffering: "All flesh is like grass... but the word of the Lord remains forever. And this word is the good news that was preached to you" (1 Peter 1:24–25). Empires are grass — Assyria was, Babylon would be, and every power since. The word is not grass. The exile generation needed to know that the promise outlives the empire that mocks it. So do we.

**Behold your God (40:9–11).** Then the herald climbs the mountain: "Go on up to a high mountain, O Zion, herald of good news... say to the cities of Judah, 'Behold your God!'" Good news is not "behold your improvement" or "behold your circumstances." It is a Person, pointed at. "Behold, the Lord GOD comes with might, and his arm rules for him; behold, his reward is with him, and his recompense before him." And then, without transition, the might becomes tenderness: "He will tend his flock like a shepherd; he will gather the lambs in his arms; he will carry them in his bosom, and gently lead those that are with young." The arm that rules the nations is the arm that carries lambs. The same verse holds omnipotence and gentleness without strain, because in God they were never two things. If you are a lamb — weak, young in faith, recently rescued — your place is not at the back of the flock. It is in His arms, against His heart.

**The incomparable Creator (40:12–26).** The chapter's center is a hymn to God's unlikeness: Who has measured the waters in the hollow of His hand, marked off the heavens with a span, weighed the mountains in scales? To whom will you liken God? Not to an idol — the idol section is almost comedy: a craftsman casts it, a goldsmith plates it, a poor man picks a wood that will not rot, and the result must be propped up so it cannot move. The Creator sits above the circle of the earth, and its inhabitants are like grasshoppers; He stretches out the heavens like a curtain and spreads them like a tent; He brings princes to nothing and makes the rulers of the earth as emptiness — scarcely planted before He blows on them and they wither. "To whom then will you compare me, that I should be like him? says the Holy One. Lift up your eyes on high and see: who created these?" The stars are His muster roll; He calls them all by name, and "by the greatness of his might... not one is missing." The God who never loses a star has not lost you.

**They shall mount up with wings (40:27–31).** The chapter ends by answering the exile's complaint by name: "Why do you say, O Jacob, and speak, O Israel, 'My way is hidden from the LORD, and my right is disregarded by my God'?" Hidden and disregarded — the two fears of every suffering believer. The answer is not an explanation of the exile but a revelation of the Creator: "Have you not known? Have you not heard? The LORD is the everlasting God, the Creator of the ends of the earth. He does not faint or grow weary; his understanding is unsearchable. He gives power to the faint, and to him who has no might he increases strength." Even youths — the demographic of stamina — faint and fall exhausted. "But they who wait for the LORD shall renew their strength; they shall mount up with wings like eagles; they shall run and not be weary; they shall walk and not faint." The Hebrew word for wait, *qavah*, means to hope with tension, like a cord stretched tight — waiting as an act of strength, not passivity. And notice the order reverses itself at the end: fly, run, walk. The Christian life is mostly the walking — daily, ordinary, unglamorous faithfulness — and the promise is that even the walking will not make us faint, because the strength is renewed, exchanged: our weakness traded for His strength, every day, at the rate of waiting.

### Key verses

> "Comfort, comfort my people, says your God. Speak tenderly to Jerusalem, and cry to her that her warfare is ended, that her iniquity is pardoned, that she has received from the LORD's hand double for all her sins." — Isaiah 40:1–2 (ESV)

> "A voice cries: 'In the wilderness prepare the way of the LORD; make straight in the desert a highway for our God. Every valley shall be lifted up, and every mountain and hill be made low; the uneven ground shall become level, and the rough places a plain. And the glory of the LORD shall be revealed, and all flesh shall see it together, for the mouth of the LORD has spoken.'" — Isaiah 40:3–5 (ESV)

> "He will tend his flock like a shepherd; he will gather the lambs in his arms; he will carry them in his bosom, and gently lead those that are with young." — Isaiah 40:11 (ESV)

> "But they who wait for the LORD shall renew their strength; they shall mount up with wings like eagles; they shall run and not be weary; they shall walk and not faint." — Isaiah 40:31 (ESV)

### The bad news and the good news

The bad news of Isaiah 40 is that all flesh is grass — our glory, our strength, and our empires wither at a breath — and that left to ourselves we conclude our way is hidden from God and our cause disregarded. The good news is that the everlasting Creator does not faint, does not forget a single star or a single lamb, has pardoned His people's iniquity, and renews the strength of everyone who waits for Him. The Shepherd's arm rules the nations and carries the lambs — the same arm, the same day.

**A note from the Reformers.** Spurgeon called Isaiah 40 the chapter where fainting Christians should live, and he pointed out that the promise is not that God will remove the walking but that He will renew the walker — strength exchanged at the rate of waiting, renewed like a currency swapped at the border of heaven.

**Reflection question:** Where do you most need to hear "Comfort, comfort" — and which promise of this chapter will you wait on deliberately: the pardoned iniquity, the Shepherd's arms, or the renewed strength?

---

## Isaiah 41 — Fear Not, I Am With You: The God Who Calls the Nations and Holds His Servant

### Jesus in this chapter

Jesus is the Servant who suddenly steps forward at this chapter's end: "But you, Israel, my servant, Jacob, whom I have chosen, the offspring of Abraham, my friend." Israel bears the title first, but Israel the servant-nation kept failing at the job, and the title narrows through these chapters until it rests on one faithful Israelite — Jesus, the true Servant, chosen, upheld, and unafraid. He is also the Redeemer named in verse 14 — "your Redeemer is the Holy One of Israel" — and the voice of the chapter's great promise, spoken to trembling people: "Fear not, for I am with you; be not dismayed, for I am your God; I will strengthen you, I will help you, I will uphold you with my righteous right hand." Jesus speaks that promise in His own person to His disciples: "I am with you always, to the end of the age" (Matthew 28:20).

### Commentary

**The courtroom of the nations (41:1–7).** God summons the coastlands to court: "Let us together draw near for judgment." He has stirred up "one from the east" — Cyrus of Persia, still unnamed, whose victories are already being credited to God's righteousness: "Who stirred up one from the east whom victory meets at every step?... Who has performed and done this, calling the generations from the beginning? I, the LORD, the first, and with the last; I am he." History's newest superpower is, before he is even born, a footnote in God's sentence. The coastlands see and tremble; and their response is a tragedy in miniature — they help each other, say "Take courage!" to a neighbor, and rush to make more idols: the craftsman encourages the goldsmith, the smoother encourages the hammerer, and they nail the god down "so that it cannot be moved." Fear drives men to their workshops. God drives fear away with His presence. Two responses to a shaking world, side by side.

**Fear not, I am with you (41:8–20).** Then God turns from the nations to His trembling servant, and the temperature changes completely: "But you, Israel, my servant, Jacob, whom I have chosen, the offspring of Abraham, my friend; you whom I took from the ends of the earth, and called from its farthest corners, saying to you, 'You are my servant, I have chosen you and not cast you off'; fear not, for I am with you." Chosen, taken, called, not cast off — the verbs are all God's. Then the promise itself, five "I wills" stacked like stones: I will strengthen you, I will help you, I will uphold you with my righteous right hand. The enemies who rage will be as nothing; "you shall seek those who contend with you, but you shall not find them." And then the tenderness reaches its peak with an insult turned into endearment: "Fear not, you worm Jacob, you men of Israel! I am the one who helps you, declares the LORD; your Redeemer is the Holy One of Israel." A worm with a Redeemer — that is the gospel's arithmetic. And the worm becomes a threshing sledge, sharp and new, threshing mountains — God's weakest instruments doing His largest work. The poor and needy seeking water find God opening rivers on bare heights and fountains in valleys, planting cedars in the desert — so that men "may see and know... that the hand of the LORD has done this, the Holy One of Israel has created it."

**The idols' empty résumé (41:21–29).** God then invites the idols to present their case: "Tell us what is to come hereafter... tell us the former things... do good, or do harm, that we may be dismayed and terrified." Silence. The verdict: "Behold, you are nothing, and your work is less than nothing; an abomination is he who chooses you." Idols cannot predict, cannot act, cannot even do harm on cue. Against their silence God sets His own track record: "I am the first to say to Zion, 'Behold, here they are!' and I give to Jerusalem a herald of good news." The chapter ends with God stirring up the one from the north — Cyrus again — while the idols' counselors are exposed as wind and emptiness. The God who tells the future is the God who holds His servant. Both facts are in the same chapter because both are needed by frightened people.

### Key verses

> "Fear not, for I am with you; be not dismayed, for I am your God; I will strengthen you, I will help you, I will uphold you with my righteous right hand." — Isaiah 41:10 (ESV)

> "For I, the LORD your God, hold your right hand; it is I who say to you, 'Fear not, I am the one who helps you.'" — Isaiah 41:13 (ESV)

> "Fear not, you worm Jacob, you men of Israel! I am the one who helps you, declares the LORD; your Redeemer is the Holy One of Israel." — Isaiah 41:14 (ESV)

> "Behold, you are nothing, and your work is less than nothing; an abomination is he who chooses you." — Isaiah 41:24 (ESV)

### The bad news and the good news

The bad news of Isaiah 41 is that our self-made gods are nothing and less than nothing — they cannot tell the future, cannot help, and must themselves be nailed down so they do not fall — and that we, by nature, are worm Jacob, unable to thresh anything. The good news is that the living God chooses, takes, calls, and does not cast off; He holds our right hand like a father crossing a street with a child, stacks five "I wills" against our fears, and names Himself our Redeemer. Fear not is not a suggestion in this chapter. It is a conclusion drawn from Who is with us.

**A note from the Reformers.** Luther called Isaiah 41:10 a verse to put under the pillow of every dying Christian — the promises are not conditional on our grip but on God's: He holds our right hand, we do not hold His. Spurgeon said the five "I wills" are five cords of one rope, and a drowning man needs only one of them to hold.

**Reflection question:** Which of the five "I wills" in verse 10 do you most need today — strengthen, help, or uphold — and what fear has been answering God's "fear not" with arguments?

---

## Isaiah 42 — The First Servant Song: The Bruised Reed He Will Not Break

### Jesus in this chapter

This is the first of the Servant Songs, and Matthew tells us plainly whose song it is. After Jesus withdrew from the Pharisees' plot and healed everyone who followed, Matthew writes: "This was to fulfill what was spoken by the prophet Isaiah: 'Behold, my servant whom I have chosen, my beloved with whom my soul is well pleased. I will put my Spirit upon him, and he will proclaim justice to the Gentiles. He will not quarrel or cry aloud, nor will anyone hear his voice in the streets; a bruised reed he will not break, and a smoldering wick he will not quench, until he brings justice to victory; and in his name the Gentiles will hope'" (Matthew 12:17–21). That is Isaiah 42:1–4, applied by the Holy Spirit to Jesus without apology. The Servant upheld by God, delighted in by God, anointed with the Spirit, gentle with the bruised, a covenant for the people and a light for the nations — that is Jesus Christ, and there is no one else it could be.

### Commentary

**Behold my Servant (42:1–4).** "Behold my servant, whom I uphold, my chosen, in whom my soul delights; I have put my Spirit upon him; he will bring forth justice to the nations." Three things God says about Him before He does anything: upheld, chosen, delighted in. At Jesus' baptism heaven opened and the voice used this song's words — "You are my beloved Son; with you I am well pleased" — and the Spirit descended like a dove. Then the manner of His work: "He will not cry aloud or lift up his voice, or make it heard in the street." The Servant does not campaign. He does not shout down His opponents or build His kingdom on noise. And then the two sentences that hurting people have held onto for two thousand years: "A bruised reed he will not break, and a faintly burning wick he will not quench." A bruised reed — a stalk already bent and cracked, useless for music or support — He does not snap off and discard. A smoldering wick — a lamp down to its last ember, more smoke than flame — He does not snuff out. He tends it back to flame. "He will faithfully bring forth justice. He will not grow faint or be discouraged till he has established justice in the earth; and the coastlands wait for his law." The gentle Servant is not a fragile Servant. The One who will not break a reed will not be broken Himself until justice stands on the earth.

**The covenant and the light (42:5–9).** God the Creator — who stretched out the heavens, gave breath to the people on the earth and spirit to those who walk in it — speaks the Servant's commission: "I am the LORD; I have called you in righteousness; I will take you by the hand and keep you; I will give you as a covenant for the people, a light for the nations, to open the eyes that are blind, to bring out the prisoners from the dungeon, from the prison those who sit in darkness." The Servant does not merely announce a covenant; He *is* the covenant — the promise made personal, embodied, touchable. Simeon held Him in the temple and sang Isaiah's words back: "a light for revelation to the Gentiles, and for glory to your people Israel" (Luke 2:32). Prisoners out of dungeons, blind eyes opened — Jesus read His own job description out of Isaiah in Nazareth and then went and did every item on it. "Behold, the former things have come to pass, and new things I now declare; before they spring forth I tell you of them." The God who predicts is the God who performs.

**The song and the blind servants (42:10–25).** The song breaks into praise: "Sing to the LORD a new song, his praise from the end of the earth" — sailors, coastlands, deserts, villages all summoned. God goes out like a man of war, routs His enemies, and then speaks one of the tenderest lines in the book: "For a long time I have held my peace; I have kept still and restrained myself; now I will cry out like a woman in labor." God's long silence breaks in birth-pangs — something new is being born. He will lead the blind "in a way that they do not know... I will turn the darkness before them into light, the rough places into level ground." But the chapter ends with the paradox that explains the exile: the servant-nation itself is blind. "Who is blind but my servant, or deaf as my messenger whom I send?" Israel, called to be the light, could not see. "He sees many things, but does not observe them; his ears are open, but he does not hear." The plundered, trapped people in verse 22 are what happens when the servant refuses to serve. And that failure is exactly why the Servant of verses 1–9 had to be one Man, not a nation. The nation failed the job description; the Son fulfilled it.

### Key verses

> "Behold my servant, whom I uphold, my chosen, in whom my soul delights; I have put my Spirit upon him; he will bring forth justice to the nations." — Isaiah 42:1 (ESV)

> "A bruised reed he will not break, and a faintly burning wick he will not quench; he will faithfully bring forth justice." — Isaiah 42:3 (ESV)

> "I am the LORD; I have called you in righteousness; I will take you by the hand and keep you; I will give you as a covenant for the people, a light for the nations, to open the eyes that are blind, to bring out the prisoners from the dungeon, from the prison those who sit in darkness." — Isaiah 42:6–7 (ESV)

> "Behold, the former things have come to pass, and new things I now declare; before they spring forth I tell you of them." — Isaiah 42:9 (ESV)

### The bad news and the good news

The bad news of Isaiah 42 is that the servant-nation went blind — seeing many things and observing none — and that by ourselves we are exactly what the song describes: bruised reeds and smoldering wicks, one squeeze or one gust from done. The good news is that God sent a Servant who does not break bruised reeds or quench smoldering wicks, who is Himself the covenant and the light, who opens blind eyes and empties dungeons — and who will not grow faint until justice stands on the earth. If you are barely burning, you are not disqualified. You are His specialty.

**A note from the Reformers.** Spurgeon preached "the bruised reed and the smoldering wick" to discouraged sinners more than once, insisting that Christ's gentleness to the weakest is not a lesser part of His glory but the heart of it — He came not for the strong stalks but for the cracked ones, and His tenderness is as certain as His power.

**Reflection question:** Are you a bruised reed or a smoldering wick today — bent, cracked, barely alight? What would it mean to stop hiding that from the Servant and let Him tend you instead of finishing you?

---

## Isaiah 43 — Fear Not, I Have Redeemed You: Called by Name Through Fire and Water

### Jesus in this chapter

Jesus is the Redeemer who speaks this whole chapter — "I, I am the LORD, and besides me there is no savior" — and the only God who was ever pierced for the people He redeems. He is the One who calls His own by name ("I have called you by name, you are mine"), the promise Jesus takes up as the Good Shepherd who "calls his own sheep by name" (John 10:3). He is the blotted-out transgression of verse 25 — "I, I am he who blots out your transgressions for my own sake, and I will not remember your sins" — the forgiveness Jesus would purchase and pronounce. And He is the new thing springing forth in the wilderness, the rivers in the desert: the exodus God does a second time, greater than the first, in the gospel.

### Commentary

**Called by name (43:1–7).** "But now thus says the LORD, he who created you, O Jacob, he who formed you, O Israel: 'Fear not, for I have redeemed you; I have called you by name, you are mine.'" Created, formed, redeemed, named, owned — five claims of God on His people before a single promise is made. The Hebrew word for redeemed, *ga'al*, is the kinsman-redeemer word — the family member who buys back what was lost, at his own cost, because it belongs to the family. "When you pass through the waters, I will be with you; and through the rivers, they shall not overwhelm you; when you walk through fire you shall not be burned, and the flame shall not consume you." Note the realism: *when*, not *if* — waters and fire are on the itinerary of God's people. The promise is not exemption but presence, and not just presence but outcome: not overwhelmed, not burned, not consumed. Shadrach, Meshach, and Abednego would walk this verse literally in Babylon and come out without the smell of fire. "For I am the LORD your God, the Holy One of Israel, your Savior. I give Egypt as your ransom, Cush and Seba in exchange for you. Because you are precious in my eyes, and honored, and I love you, I give men in return for you, peoples in exchange for your life." God ransoms nations for His people in history — and in the gospel He gives not nations but His own Son as the ransom. "You are precious in my eyes... and I love you" — let that land. Then the gathering: from east, west, north, and south He brings His sons and daughters, "everyone who is called by my name, whom I created for my glory." God's glory and our gathering are the same event.

**Witnesses and the only Savior (43:8–13).** The courtroom reopens, but now Israel is not the defendant — Israel is the witness: "You are my witnesses, declares the LORD, and my servant whom I have chosen, that you may know and believe me and understand that I am he. Before me no god was formed, nor shall there be any after me. I, I am the LORD, and besides me there is no savior." The doubling "I, I" is God's signature of emphasis. The idols' witnesses are blind and know nothing; God's witnesses have a job — to know, believe, and understand, and then to say so. "Also henceforth I am he; there is none who can deliver from my hand; I work, and who can turn it back?" A salvation no one can reverse, worked by a hand no one can pry open. That is the security of everyone in Christ.

**The new exodus and the blotted record (43:14–28).** God announces He will do for Babylon what He did to Egypt — send the proud city fleeing in its ships — and then He says the most surprising thing in the chapter: "Remember not the former things, nor consider the things of old. Behold, I am doing a new thing; now it springs forth, do you not perceive it? I will make a way in the wilderness and rivers in the desert." The God of the exodus tells His people not to live in the exodus — because He is doing something bigger. Wild beasts will honor Him; the chosen people are formed "that they might declare my praise." But then the indictment returns, and it is heartbreaking: "Yet you did not call upon me, O Jacob; but you have been weary of me, O Israel!" No sacrifices, no offerings, no incense — instead, "you have burdened me with your sins; you have wearied me with your iniquities." And against that record — unanswerable, unpayable — verse 25 stands like a sunrise: "I, I am he who blots out your transgressions for my own sake, and I will not remember your sins." For My own sake. Not because Israel earned amnesia from God, but because God's glory is bound up in forgiving. The courtroom of verse 26 — "Put me in remembrance; let us argue together" — ends not with Israel proving innocence ("your first father sinned") but with God proving mercy. That is the whole gospel: we bring the record; He brings the blotter.

### Key verses

> "Fear not, for I have redeemed you; I have called you by name, you are mine. When you pass through the waters, I will be with you; and through the rivers, they shall not overwhelm you; when you walk through fire you shall not be burned, and the flame shall not consume you." — Isaiah 43:1–2 (ESV)

> "Because you are precious in my eyes, and honored, and I love you, I give men in return for you, peoples in exchange for your life." — Isaiah 43:4 (ESV)

> "I, I am the LORD, and besides me there is no savior." — Isaiah 43:11 (ESV)

> "Behold, I am doing a new thing; now it springs forth, do you not perceive it? I will make a way in the wilderness and rivers in the desert." — Isaiah 43:19 (ESV)

> "I, I am he who blots out your transgressions for my own sake, and I will not remember your sins." — Isaiah 43:25 (ESV)

### The bad news and the good news

The bad news of Isaiah 43 is that God's own people wearied Him with their iniquities, burdened Him with their sins, and stopped calling on Him — and that waters and fire are certainties on the road, not possibilities. The good news is that the God we wearied blots out our transgressions for His own sake and refuses to remember them; He calls us by name, prices us as precious, gives a ransom for us, and walks with us through every water and flame so that we are neither overwhelmed nor consumed. Besides Him there is no savior — and beside us, in the fire, there is no one but Him.

**A note from the Reformers.** Calvin called Isaiah 43:25 the fountainhead of the doctrine of free justification: God blots out transgressions "for my own sake," which means the cause of forgiveness is in God, not in the sinner — and therefore the worst record in the room can be blotted as completely as the best.

**Reflection question:** Which do you need to hear most today — "I have called you by name, you are mine," or "I will not remember your sins"? Why that one — and will you let it be true?

---

## Isaiah 44 — I Will Pour Water on the Thirsty Land: The Maker, the Idol, and the Return

### Jesus in this chapter

Jesus is the King and Redeemer of this chapter — "Thus says the LORD, the King of Israel and his Redeemer, the LORD of hosts: 'I am the first and I am the last; besides me there is no god'" — words Jesus claims for Himself verbatim in Revelation: "I am the first and the last, and the living one" (Revelation 1:17–18). He is the One who pours the Spirit like water on thirsty land (44:3), the outpouring He promises in John 7:37–39. He is the Rock of verse 8 — "Is there a God besides me? There is no Rock; I know not any" — and Paul says the Rock was Christ. And He is the blotted-out record again, deepened: "I have blotted out your transgressions like a cloud and your sins like mist; return to me, for I have redeemed you" (44:22). The cloud of sin, dissolved by the sun of redemption — and the only thing asked is return.

### Commentary

**Water on thirsty land (44:1–8).** "But now hear, O Jacob my servant, Israel whom I have chosen!" The chosen servant who failed is still chosen, still addressed, still helped: "Fear not, O Jacob my servant... For I will pour water on the thirsty land, and streams on the dry ground; I will pour my Spirit upon your offspring, and my blessing on your descendants. They shall spring up among the grass like willows by flowing streams." The Hebrew word for Spirit is *ruach* — breath, wind, Spirit — and the image is irrigation: God's Spirit as water on cracked ground, producing a generation that springs up and claims Him publicly: "This one will say, 'I am the LORD's,' another will call on the name of Jacob, and another will write on his hand, 'The LORD's,' and name himself by the name of Israel." A tattoo of belonging, written by the owner's own hand. Then the challenge that silences every rival: "Who is like me? Let him proclaim it... Fear not, nor be afraid; have I not told you from of old and declared it? And you are my witnesses!" Witnesses again — God's people exist to testify that He told it beforehand and did it on time.

**The folly of the idol workshop (44:9–20).** Then Isaiah writes the most devastating satire in the Bible. The craftsmen are "nothing"; their images profit nothing; the smith grows hungry and faint over his own work — the god-maker needs lunch more than his god does. The carpenter stretches a line, marks it with a pencil, shapes it with planes — and then the centerpiece, verses 14–17, which should be read slowly and allowed to do its work: a man plants a cedar, the rain nourishes it, he cuts it down. Part he burns to warm himself and bake bread. Part he cooks his meat over. And from the rest he makes a god, his idol, falls down before it, and prays: "Deliver me, for you are my god!" The same log: half dinner-fire, half deity. Isaiah's verdict is not anger but pity sharpened to a point: "They know not, nor do they discern, for he has shut their eyes, so that they cannot see, and their hearts, so that they cannot understand. No one considers, nor is there knowledge or discernment to say, 'Half of it I burned in the fire; I also baked bread on its coals; I roasted meat and have eaten. And shall I make the rest of it an abomination? Shall I fall down before a block of wood?'" A block of wood. "He feeds on ashes; a deluded heart has led him astray, and he cannot deliver his soul or say, 'Is there not a lie in my right hand?'" Feeding on ashes — that is Isaiah's name for every substitute god: the addiction, the approval, the money, the grudge. It promises a meal and delivers ash, and the tragedy is not that men hold a lie in their right hand but that they cannot bring themselves to ask the question about it.

**Return to Me, for I have redeemed you (44:21–28).** The satire gives way to song: "Remember these things, O Jacob... I have blotted out your transgressions like a cloud and your sins like mist; return to me, for I have redeemed you. Sing, O heavens, for the LORD has done it; shout, O depths of the earth; break forth into singing, you mountains, O forest, and every tree in it! For the LORD has redeemed Jacob, and will be glorified in Israel." The order is everything: redemption first, return second. God does not say "return to me and I will redeem you." He says "I have redeemed you — therefore return." And the chapter closes with God naming His shepherd-instrument for the rebuilding: Cyrus, "my shepherd," who shall fulfill all God's purpose, saying of Jerusalem, "She shall be built," and of the temple, "Your foundation shall be laid." The Persian king is God's hired hand for Israel's homecoming — and a signpost to the true Shepherd-King who would rebuild a greater temple.

### Key verses

> "For I will pour water on the thirsty land, and streams on the dry ground; I will pour my Spirit upon your offspring, and my blessing on your descendants." — Isaiah 44:3 (ESV)

> "Thus says the LORD, the King of Israel and his Redeemer, the LORD of hosts: 'I am the first and I am the last; besides me there is no god.'" — Isaiah 44:6 (ESV)

> "He feeds on ashes; a deluded heart has led him astray, and he cannot deliver his soul or say, 'Is there not a lie in my right hand?'" — Isaiah 44:20 (ESV)

> "I have blotted out your transgressions like a cloud and your sins like mist; return to me, for I have redeemed you." — Isaiah 44:22 (ESV)

### The bad news and the good news

The bad news of Isaiah 44 is that the human heart is an idol factory that feeds on ashes — we bow to blocks of wood we ourselves carved, warmed ourselves by the other half, and cannot even ask whether there is a lie in our right hand. The good news is that besides the LORD there is no Rock, and this Rock has blotted out our transgressions like a cloud burned off by morning sun: "Return to me, for I have redeemed you." The redemption is already done. The returning is all that's left.

**A note from the Reformers.** Calvin preached the idol workshop of Isaiah 44 as the anatomy of all false religion, and he added the sentence that became famous in his Institutes: the human heart is "a perpetual forge of idols." His cure was Isaiah's cure — not better craftsmanship, but a blotted record and a returning people.

**Reflection question:** What is the "block of wood" in your life — the thing made from the same material as your ordinary comforts that you have quietly started bowing to? Will you ask the question Isaiah says the idolater cannot ask: "Is there not a lie in my right hand?"

---

## Isaiah 45 — Cyrus, the Hidden God, and Salvation to the Ends of the Earth

### Jesus in this chapter

Jesus is the LORD who speaks through this whole chapter — the God who anoints a pagan king, hides Himself in darkness and in a manger's obscurity, and then throws the doors open to the world: "Turn to me and be saved, all the ends of the earth! For I am God, and there is no other." Paul takes this chapter's oath and puts it at the climax of the Christ-hymn: "By myself I have sworn; from my mouth has gone out in righteousness a word that shall not return: 'To me every knee shall bow, every tongue shall swear allegiance'" — and then Philippians 2:10–11 says every knee bows "at the name of Jesus." The universal summons of Isaiah 45:22 is the gospel's open door, and the knee that bows to the LORD bows, in the New Testament's own reading, to Jesus Christ the Lord.

### Commentary

**Cyrus, God's anointed (45:1–8).** "Thus says the LORD to his anointed, to Cyrus, whose right hand I have grasped, to subdue nations before him and to loose the belts of kings, to open doors before him that gates may not be closed." *Anointed* — the word is *mashiach*, messiah. The only pagan in the Bible to receive the title is a Persian king who does not know God (45:4–5: "I call you by your name... though you do not know me"). God names Cyrus in prophecy roughly a century and a half before his birth, grasps his right hand, levels mountains of bronze and iron before him — all "for the sake of my servant Jacob, and Israel my chosen." The mightiest man alive is a tool in a story about God's people. And the purpose is evangelistic even for Cyrus: "that people may know, from the rising of the sun and from the west, that there is none besides me; I am the LORD, and there is no other. I form light and create darkness; I make well-being and create calamity; I, the LORD, do all these things." No dualism, no rival power, no corner of reality outside His hand. Then heaven is summoned to rain: "Shower, O heavens, from above, and let the clouds rain down righteousness; let the earth open, that salvation and righteousness may bear fruit; let the earth cause them both to sprout." Salvation pictured as weather and harvest — God's doing from sky to soil.

**The clay and the Potter (45:9–13).** "Woe to him who strives with him who formed him, a pot among earthen pots! Does the clay say to him who forms it, 'What are you making?' or 'Your work has no handles'?" Paul quotes this in Romans 9 for the same purpose: the creature's cross-examination of the Creator is answered by the relationship itself — as absurd as a newborn indicting his parents for his existence. God's response is not a theodicy lecture but a reminder of His track record: He made the earth, created man on it, stretched out the heavens, and marshaled their host — and He has raised up Cyrus "in righteousness... and he shall build my city and set my exiles free, not for price or reward." The God who answers the clay's complaint is the God already rebuilding the city.

**The God who hides Himself — and saves the world (45:14–25).** Egypt's wealth, Cush's merchandise, Sabean giants come over in chains to Israel's God, confessing: "God is with you only, and there is no other, no god besides him." Then Isaiah's honest aside: "Truly, you are a God who hides himself, O God of Israel, the Savior." God's hiddenness is not a problem invented by modern doubters; Isaiah confesses it in the middle of a prophecy of world conversion. The idol-makers go in confusion; "but Israel is saved by the LORD with everlasting salvation; you shall not be put to shame or confounded to all eternity." Then creation itself is summoned as character witness: the God who did not create the earth "a chaos" (*tohu* — the waste of Genesis 1:2) but formed it to be inhabited "did not speak in secret... I the LORD speak the truth; I declare what is right." And then the open door, the widest invitation in the Old Testament: "Turn to me and be saved, all the ends of the earth! For I am God, and there is no other." Turn — the whole requirement. Ends of the earth — the whole invitation list. "By myself I have sworn... 'To me every knee shall bow, every tongue shall swear allegiance.'" The chapter ends with the seed of Abraham justified and glorying — in the LORD, not in themselves.

### Key verses

> "I am the LORD, and there is no other. I form light and create darkness; I make well-being and create calamity; I, the LORD, do all these things." — Isaiah 45:6–7 (ESV)

> "Woe to him who strives with him who formed him, a pot among earthen pots! Does the clay say to him who forms it, 'What are you making?' or 'Your work has no handles'?" — Isaiah 45:9 (ESV)

> "Truly, you are a God who hides himself, O God of Israel, the Savior." — Isaiah 45:15 (ESV)

> "Turn to me and be saved, all the ends of the earth! For I am God, and there is no other." — Isaiah 45:22 (ESV)

> "By myself I have sworn; from my mouth has gone out in righteousness a word that shall not return: 'To me every knee shall bow, every tongue shall swear allegiance.'" — Isaiah 45:23 (ESV)

### The bad news and the good news

The bad news of Isaiah 45 is that we are clay that cross-examines the Potter, served by a God who often hides Himself, in a world where idols' makers go in confusion — and none of us can save ourselves from any of it. The good news is that the hidden God has spoken in the open: "Turn to me and be saved, all the ends of the earth!" — and the knee that will one day bow willingly or unwillingly to the LORD is invited today to bow gladly to Jesus, and be saved by everlasting salvation, never to be put to shame.

**A note from the Reformers.** Spurgeon was converted as a teenager in a snowstorm, in a tiny chapel, by a substitute preacher who had almost nothing to say except Isaiah 45:22 — "Look unto me, and be ye saved, all the ends of the earth" — pointed at the boy and said, "Young man, look to Jesus Christ." Spurgeon looked, and lived, and preached that verse for fifty years as proof that the whole gospel can fit in a look.

**Reflection question:** Spurgeon was saved by doing the one thing this verse asks — looking. What have you been doing instead of turning: striving like clay, waiting for God to stop hiding, or negotiating terms? What would a simple turn look like today?

---

## Isaiah 46 — Carried from the Womb: The Gods Who Must Be Carried, and the God Who Carries

### Jesus in this chapter

Jesus is the carrying God of this chapter, set against the carried gods. Babylon's Bel and Nebo are loaded onto beasts and carts, burdens for weary animals; Israel's God reverses the picture entirely: "Listen to me, O house of Jacob... who have been borne by me from before your birth, carried from the womb; even to your old age I am he, and to gray hairs I will carry you. I have made, and I will bear; I will carry and will save." That is the God of Jesus Christ — the Shepherd who carries the lamb (Isaiah 40:11), the Father who carries His people from womb to gray hairs. Jesus is also the righteousness drawing near at the chapter's end: "I bring near my righteousness; it is not far off, and my salvation will not delay" — the salvation that arrived in person, on time, in Bethlehem and at the cross.

### Commentary

**Bel bows down, Nebo stoops (46:1–2).** The chapter opens with a parade in reverse: Babylon's great gods, Bel and Nebo, are not processing in triumph but being evacuated — their images loaded "as burdens on weary beasts," packed onto carts like furniture ahead of an invasion. "They stoop; they bow down together; they cannot save the burden, but themselves go into captivity." The gods cannot even save themselves, let alone their worshipers. Isaiah's satire has a surgical point: a god who must be carried is a burden; the true God is a Carrier. Every religion of man's making eventually becomes a load its maker must haul — more rules, more fear, more maintenance. The gospel announces a God who hauls us.

**Carried from the womb to gray hairs (46:3–4).** Then the tenderness, and it is aimed at the elderly and the worn-out: "Listen to me, O house of Jacob, all the remnant of the house of Israel, who have been borne by me from before your birth, carried from the womb; even to your old age I am he, and to gray hairs I will carry you. I have made, and I will bear; I will carry and will save." Five verbs of God's carrying against the remnant's zero. The Hebrew word for carried here is the language of a parent with a child in arms. God's commitment is not to the young and strong only; it is cradle-to-gray-hairs coverage, and it does not lapse. If you are old, tired, or feel like a burden everyone else has set down — this verse is God's direct answer: *I have made, and I will bear.*

**The goldsmith's god versus the God who declares the end (46:5–13).** Men lavish gold from their bags, hire a goldsmith, make a god, fall down and worship it — then "they lift it to their shoulders, they carry it, they set it in its place, and it stands there; it cannot move from its place. If one cries to it, it does not answer or save him from his trouble." Against that immobile object, God sets His own credential: "Remember this and stand firm... remember the former things of old; for I am God, and there is no other; I am God, and there is none like me, declaring the end from the beginning and from ancient times things not yet done, saying, 'My counsel shall stand, and I will accomplish all my purpose.'" Prophecy is God's signature: He declares the end from the beginning because He is standing at both. He calls a bird of prey from the east — Cyrus again — "the man of my counsel from a far country. I have spoken, and I will bring it to pass; I have purposed, and I will do it." Then the summons to the stubborn-hearted, "who are far from righteousness": "Listen to me... I bring near my righteousness; it is not far off, and my salvation will not delay; I will put salvation in Zion, for Israel my glory." Stubborn hearts are not told righteousness is far. They are told it is near — brought near by God Himself. Paul's "the righteousness of God" in Romans is this promise arriving by name.

### Key verses

> "Bel bows down; Nebo stoops; their idols are on beasts and livestock; these things you carry are borne as burdens on weary beasts. They stoop; they bow down together; they cannot save the burden, but themselves go into captivity." — Isaiah 46:1–2 (ESV)

> "Listen to me, O house of Jacob, all the remnant of the house of Israel, who have been borne by me from before your birth, carried from the womb; even to your old age I am he, and to gray hairs I will carry you. I have made, and I will bear; I will carry and will save." — Isaiah 46:3–4 (ESV)

> "I am God, and there is no other; I am God, and there is none like me, declaring the end from the beginning and from ancient times things not yet done, saying, 'My counsel shall stand, and I will accomplish all my purpose.'" — Isaiah 46:9–10 (ESV)

> "I bring near my righteousness; it is not far off, and my salvation will not delay; I will put salvation in Zion, for Israel my glory." — Isaiah 46:13 (ESV)

### The bad news and the good news

The bad news of Isaiah 46 is that every man-made god becomes a burden its maker must carry — silent under crying, immobile under trouble — and that stubborn hearts sit far from righteousness by their own choice. The good news is that the true God carries: from the womb, to old age, to gray hairs, without lapse and without complaint — and He has brought His righteousness near and put His salvation in Zion on schedule. You do not have to carry God. In Christ, He carries you.

**A note from the Reformers.** Calvin said this chapter teaches the difference between religion and the gospel in a single image: religion is man carrying his god; the gospel is God carrying man. Every system that leaves the sinner hauling the weight has Bel and Nebo in its baggage, whatever name is painted on the cart.

**Reflection question:** What are you carrying right now that feels like a god on a cart — a burden of religion, guilt, or self-salvation? What would it mean to be carried instead, gray hairs and all?

---

## Isaiah 47 — The Virgin Daughter of Babylon Sits in the Dust

### Jesus in this chapter

Jesus is the Redeemer who speaks the sentence in this chapter — "Our Redeemer — the LORD of hosts is his name — is the Holy One of Israel" (47:4) — the one line of hope dropped into the middle of Babylon's humiliation, and it is spoken by the exiles watching their oppressor fall. Christ is also the standard by which Babylon is judged: she showed no mercy — "you did not remember the outcome of them or give thought to their future" — and the Judge who weighs her is the same Lord who said that judgment is without mercy to one who has shown no mercy (James 2:13). Babylon trusted in her wisdom, her sorceries, and her "I am, and there is no one besides me" — the exact blasphemy of self-deification that Jesus exposes and ends. The Redeemer's presence in verse 4 keeps the chapter from being mere revenge: God's people are not gloating; they are naming the One who acted.

### Commentary

**Down from the throne (47:1–4).** "Come down and sit in the dust, O virgin daughter of Babylon; sit on the ground without a throne." The pampered queen is put to slave's work — taking off the veil, grinding at the mill, shame uncovered. The reasons are stated as an indictment of cruelty: "I was angry with my people; I profaned my heritage; I gave them into your hand; you showed them no mercy; on the aged you made your yoke exceedingly heavy." God used Babylon as His rod, but Babylon swung the rod with relish, and the rod is judged for the relish. Instruments are accountable for their attitude. And in the middle of it, the exiles' confession rises like a song line in a courtroom: "Our Redeemer — the LORD of hosts is his name — is the Holy One of Israel." Their hope is not Babylon's fall but their Redeemer's name.

**I am, and there is no one besides me (47:5–11).** Babylon's creed is quoted verbatim: "I shall be mistress forever... I am, and there is no one besides me; I shall not sit as a widow or know the loss of children." *I am* — she has stolen God's own name (compare Isaiah 45:6, "I am the LORD, and there is no other") and applied it to herself. That is the final form of pride: not merely ignoring God but occupying His sentence. The answer: widowhood and loss of children "shall come upon you in full measure... in a moment, in one day." Her defenses are catalogued and dismissed — sorceries, enchantments, astrologers — and her wisdom itself is named as her seducer: "Your wisdom and your knowledge led you astray." Disaster will come "suddenly, of which you are not aware" — the one future her horoscopes never printed.

**Stand fast in your enchantments (47:12–15).** God's sarcasm closes the chapter: "Stand fast in your enchantments and your many sorceries, with which you have labored from your youth; perhaps you may be able to succeed; perhaps you may inspire terror. You are wearied with your many counsels; let them stand forth and save you, those who divide the heavens, who gaze at the stars, who at the new moons make known what shall come upon you." They cannot. "Behold, they are like stubble; the fire consumes them. They cannot deliver themselves from the power of the flame." The counselors burn first. And the final image is desolation's loneliest picture: her merchants and companions from her youth "wander each in his own direction; there is no one to save you." A life, or an empire, built on "I am" ends with no one in the room. The Redeemer of verse 4 saves His people precisely from that room.

### Key verses

> "Our Redeemer — the LORD of hosts is his name — is the Holy One of Israel." — Isaiah 47:4 (ESV)

> "You said, 'I shall be mistress forever,' so that you did not lay these things to heart or remember their outcome... And you said in your heart, 'I am, and there is no one besides me; I shall not sit as a widow or know the loss of children.'" — Isaiah 47:7, 10 (ESV)

> "But evil shall come upon you, which you will not know how to charm away; disaster shall fall upon you, for which you will not be able to atone; and ruin shall come upon you suddenly, of which you are not aware." — Isaiah 47:11 (ESV)

### The bad news and the good news

The bad news of Isaiah 47 is that the "I am" of self-sufficiency — in an empire, or in a man who needs nothing and no one — ends in dust, widowhood, and a room with no one to save. The good news is that God's people have a Redeemer with a name: the LORD of hosts, the Holy One of Israel, who does not leave His exiles under the mistress forever. Babylon's throne empties. The Redeemer's does not.

**A note from the Reformers.** Luther saw in Babylon's "I am, and there is no one besides me" the exact speech of the unregenerate heart — every sinner enthrones himself with God's name on his own lips. The gospel dethrones that "I am" not by crushing the sinner but by revealing the true I AM, who redeems instead of merely ruling.

**Reflection question:** Where does Babylon's creed — "I am, and there is no one besides me; I will not sit as a widow" — echo in your own self-talk? What would naming the Redeemer, out loud, do to that voice?

---

## Isaiah 48 — For My Name's Sake: The Furnace of Affliction and the Way Out of Babylon

### Jesus in this chapter

Jesus is the Speaker who steps forward in this chapter's most startling verse: "And now the Lord GOD has sent me, and his Spirit" (48:16) — the LORD speaking of being sent by the LORD, with the Spirit — a window into the triune God that only makes full sense in Christ, the Sent One who says, "Come near to me, hear this: from the beginning I have not spoken in secret; from the time it came to be I have been there." He is also the Teacher who profits His people: "I am the LORD your God, who teaches you to profit, who leads you in the way you should go" — the same voice that says in Matthew 11, "learn from me." And He is the Redeemer of Israel (48:17) whose peace-offer stands over the whole chapter: "Oh that you had paid attention to my commandments! Then your peace would have been like a river."

### Commentary

**Stubborn, refined, and spared for God's name (48:1–11).** The chapter addresses people who "swear by the name of the LORD... but not in truth or right" — religious identity without religious reality. God rehearses His predictions: He declared former things long ago and did them suddenly, "lest you should say, 'My idol did them.'" He knows His people's diagnosis by name: "Because I know that you are obstinate, and your neck is an iron sinew and your forehead brass." And then the mercy that scandalizes our bookkeeping: "For my name's sake I defer my anger; for the sake of my praise I restrain it for you, that I may not cut you off. Behold, I have refined you, but not as silver; I have tried you in the furnace of affliction. For my own sake, for my own sake, I do it, for how should my name be profaned? My glory I will not give to another." Twice: for my own sake. Israel is spared not because she was refined into silver — the furnace produced no such metal — but because God's name is staked on her survival. That is the most secure ground a sinner can stand on: not the quality of our refining, but the jealousy of God for His own glory. The furnace of affliction is real, chosen by God ("I have tried you"), and purposeful — but the reason we come out of it at all is His name, not our silver.

**The Sent One speaks (48:12–16).** "Listen to me, O Jacob, and Israel, whom I called: I am he; I am the first, and I am the last. My hand laid the foundation of the earth, and my right hand spread out the heavens." The Creator speaks — and then, in verse 16, the Speaker distinguishes Himself from the Sender in a way that has made readers lean in for two thousand years: "And now the Lord GOD has sent me, and his Spirit." The church's doctors, from the early fathers to Calvin, have seen the Trinity in seed here: the LORD who created all things, sent by the Lord GOD, accompanied by His Spirit. That is the Messiah's own voiceprint — the One who would say, "As the Father has sent me, even so I am sending you," and who was Himself sent, with the Spirit descending and remaining on Him. Isaiah 48:16 is one of the Old Testament's quiet doors into the deepest room of the faith.

**Peace like a river — and the exit from Babylon (48:17–22).** "Thus says the LORD, your Redeemer, the Holy One of Israel: 'I am the LORD your God, who teaches you to profit, who leads you in the way you should go. Oh that you had paid attention to my commandments! Then your peace would have been like a river, and your righteousness like the waves of the sea; your offspring would have been like the sand...'" It is a lament, not a threat — God's "Oh that you had" is the sound of a Father over a road not taken. The Hebrew word for peace is *shalom* — wholeness, everything in its place — pictured as a river: constant, flowing, supplied from beyond itself. Then the command of the hour: "Go out from Babylon, flee from Chaldea, declare this with a shout of joy, proclaim it... say, 'The LORD has redeemed his servant Jacob!'" The exodus is to be announced like news, with shouting. They thirsted not in the deserts; He made water flow from the rock — the old exodus mercy in the new exodus journey. And then the book's recurring boundary line, the last verse, which will close the next movement too: "There is no peace, says the LORD, for the wicked." The river of peace is real, wide, and offered — and it does not flow through Babylon. You have to leave to drink.

### Key verses

> "For my name's sake I defer my anger; for the sake of my praise I restrain it for you, that I may not cut you off. Behold, I have refined you, but not as silver; I have tried you in the furnace of affliction." — Isaiah 48:9–10 (ESV)

> "Draw near to me, hear this: from the beginning I have not spoken in secret, from the time it came to be I have been there. And now the Lord GOD has sent me, and his Spirit." — Isaiah 48:16 (ESV)

> "Thus says the LORD, your Redeemer, the Holy One of Israel: 'I am the LORD your God, who teaches you to profit, who leads you in the way you should go. Oh that you had paid attention to my commandments! Then your peace would have been like a river, and your righteousness like the waves of the sea.'" — Isaiah 48:17–18 (ESV)

> "There is no peace, says the LORD, for the wicked." — Isaiah 48:22 (ESV)

### The bad news and the good news

The bad news of Isaiah 48 is that we are iron-necked and brass-browed, refined in the furnace without becoming silver, and that there is no peace — none — for the wicked who stay in Babylon. The good news is that God spares for His own name's sake, which is firmer ground than our performance; that the Sent One has come, teaching us to profit and leading us in the way; and that the river of peace still flows for everyone who goes out from Babylon at His word. His name is the reason. His peace is the offer. His road is open.

**A note from the Reformers.** Calvin called "for my own sake, for my own sake" the sinner's only anchor: if our standing depended on the silver the furnace found in us, no man would stand; because it depends on God's zeal for His own name and glory, no believer can fall. That is *soli Deo gloria* doing pastoral work.

**Reflection question:** Are you trusting the quality of your refining — how much better you've become — or the name of the God who spares for His own sake? And what is your Babylon that you still need to "go out from"?

---
## Isaiah 49 — The Second Servant Song: Engraved on the Palms of His Hands

### Jesus in this chapter

This is the second Servant Song, and the Servant speaks in His own voice — and the voice is unmistakably Jesus. Called from the womb, named from His mother's body, His mouth made like a sharp sword, hidden in the shadow of God's hand like an arrow in a quiver: that is the incarnate Son, prepared in obscurity at Nazareth for the day of revealing. His complaint — "I have labored in vain; I have spent my strength for nothing and vanity" — is the cry of the preacher of Galilee rejected by His own towns, and of Gethsemane's sorrow; and the Father's answer expands His mission beyond anything Israel imagined: "It is too light a thing that you should be my servant to raise up the tribes of Jacob... I will make you as a light for the nations, that my salvation may reach to the end of the earth." Simeon sang this verse over the infant Jesus (Luke 2:32), and Paul and Barnabas quoted it as their marching orders to the Gentiles (Acts 13:47). And the chapter's most tender promise — "I have engraved you on the palms of my hands" — finds its literal fulfillment in nail-pierced hands held up in an upper room: "See my hands" (John 20:27).

### Commentary

**The Servant's call (49:1–4).** "Listen to me, O coastlands, and give attention, you peoples from afar. The LORD called me from the womb, from the body of my mother he named my name." The audience is the whole world because the mission is. "He made my mouth like a sharp sword; in the shadow of his hand he hid me; he made me a polished arrow; in his quiver he hid me away." A sword-mouth: Jesus' words cut — they divided crowds, exposed hearts, and still do (Hebrews 4:12 calls the word of God itself the sword). A hidden arrow: thirty years of obscurity were not wasted years; they were quiver years, the arrow being polished in the dark. "And he said to me, 'You are my servant, Israel, in whom I will be glorified.'" The Servant bears Israel's name because He is Israel reduced to one faithful Man — everything the nation was called to be, He is. Then the honesty: "But I said, 'I have labored in vain; I have spent my strength for nothing and vanity; yet surely my right is with the LORD, and my recompense with my God.'" The Servant knows the feeling of fruitless labor — every pastor, parent, and witness who has poured out years and seen little should know they are in the Servant's own company. And His resolve is the pattern: He does not deny the vanity he feels; He relocates His recompense. "Yet surely" — my case is with God, whatever the visible results say.

**Too light a thing (49:5–13).** The Father's answer to the Servant's complaint is not comfort but enlargement: the mission gets bigger. "It is too light a thing that you should be my servant to raise up the tribes of Jacob and to bring back the preserved of Israel; I will make you as a light for the nations, that my salvation may reach to the end of the earth." Too light — restoring Israel alone would be beneath the Servant's worth. Kings will rise and princes prostrate themselves "because of the LORD, who is faithful, the Holy One of Israel, who has chosen you." Then the promises to the prisoners, Isaiah's signature population: "Saying to the prisoners, 'Come out,' to those who are in darkness, 'Appear.'" They shall feed along the ways; neither hunger nor thirst nor scorching wind shall strike them, "for he who has pity on them will lead them, and by springs of water will guide them." Mountains become a road; highways are raised up; people come from far — from the north, the west, and the land of Syene — and heaven and earth are summoned to sing "for the LORD has comforted his people and will have compassion on his afflicted."

**Can a woman forget? (49:14–21).** Then Zion speaks, and her line is the most human in the book: "But Zion said, 'The LORD has forsaken me; my Lord has forgotten me.'" Forsaken and forgotten — the two-word creed of every sufferer who judges God's love by God's timing. The answer is one of the highest verses in Scripture: "'Can a woman forget her nursing child, that she should have no compassion on the son of her womb? Even these may forget, yet I will not forget you. Behold, I have engraved you on the palms of my hands; your walls are continually before me.'" Even these may forget — God concedes the extreme case, a mother forgetting the child at her breast, and then outbids it: *yet I will not forget you.* And the proof is engraving — not written in pencil, not noted in a book that can be lost, but cut into palms. The Hebrew word *chaqaq* means to carve, to inscribe in stone or metal. God's people are carved into God's hands. When Jesus rose and showed His hands to Thomas, the engraving had become wounds — our names, carried in pierced palms. Zion's children then come home in a rush that overwhelms her grief: the desolate places too narrow for the crowd, the children of her bereavement saying, "The place is too crowded for me; make room for me to dwell." Loss is answered with overflow.

**The prey taken from the mighty (49:22–26).** The chapter closes with nations carrying Zion's sons and daughters home, kings as foster fathers and queens as nursing mothers, bowing with faces to the earth — the world's powers reduced to childcare for God's people. "Then you will know that I am the LORD; those who wait for me shall not be put to shame." And the last word answers the sufferer's deepest legal question — can the prey be taken from the mighty, can captives of a tyrant be rescued? "Even the captives of the mighty shall be taken, and the prey of the tyrant be rescued, for I will contend with those who contend with you, and I will save your children." God becomes the litigant: *I* will contend. The addict's tyrant, the sinner's captor, the strong man of Mark 3 — God Himself takes the case, and He does not lose custody battles.

### Key verses

> "And he said to me, 'You are my servant, Israel, in whom I will be glorified.' But I said, 'I have labored in vain; I have spent my strength for nothing and vanity; yet surely my right is with the LORD, and my recompense with my God.'" — Isaiah 49:3–4 (ESV)

> "It is too light a thing that you should be my servant to raise up the tribes of Jacob and to bring back the preserved of Israel; I will make you as a light for the nations, that my salvation may reach to the end of the earth." — Isaiah 49:6 (ESV)

> "Can a woman forget her nursing child, that she should have no compassion on the son of her womb? Even these may forget, yet I will not forget you. Behold, I have engraved you on the palms of my hands; your walls are continually before me." — Isaiah 49:15–16 (ESV)

> "Even the captives of the mighty shall be taken, and the prey of the tyrant be rescued, for I will contend with those who contend with you, and I will save your children." — Isaiah 49:25 (ESV)

### The bad news and the good news

The bad news of Isaiah 49 is that even the Servant's labor can look vain, and God's people can feel — sincerely, achingly — forsaken and forgotten, while tyrants hold their captives in what looks like unbreakable custody. The good news is that feelings are not the record: we are engraved on the palms of God's hands, the Servant's mission has been enlarged to the ends of the earth, and God Himself contends with those who contend with us until the prey is taken from the mighty. Forgotten is the one thing you cannot be, with your name in those palms.

**A note from the Reformers.** Spurgeon preached the engraved palms as the answer to every believer who fears being overlooked in the crowd of God's concerns: your name is not in a book God might misplace; it is cut into hands He cannot open without seeing. And the hands, Spurgeon added, turned out to be pierced ones — the engraving and the atonement are the same marks.

**Reflection question:** Where are you saying "the LORD has forsaken me, my Lord has forgotten me" — and what would change if you answered that feeling with the engraving instead: your name, cut into His palms, continually before Him?

---

## Isaiah 50 — The Third Servant Song: The Taught Tongue and the Unflinching Face

### Jesus in this chapter

The third Servant Song is the Servant's own testimony, and every line of it is Jesus. "The Lord GOD has given me the tongue of those who are taught, that I may know how to sustain with a word him who is weary" — that is the ministry of Jesus in one sentence, the word that sustained lepers, widows, tax collectors, and failing disciples. "I gave my back to those who strike, and my cheeks to those who pull out the beard; I did not hide my face from mocking and spitting" — that is the passion narrative, written seven centuries early: the scourging, the mocking, the spitting of Matthew 26–27. "I have set my face like a flint" — Luke uses the very image for Jesus' resolve toward Jerusalem (Luke 9:51). And the courtroom challenge — "Who will contend with me? Let us stand up together. Who is my adversary? Let him come near to me" — is the confidence Paul echoes in Romans 8: "Who shall bring any charge against God's elect? It is God who justifies. Who is to condemn?"

### Commentary

**Where is your mother's certificate of divorce? (50:1–3).** The chapter opens with God's cross-examination of the exiles' despair: "Where is your mother's certificate of divorce, with which I sent her away? Or which of my creditors is it to whom I have sold you?" The exiles assumed God had divorced Israel or sold her children to pay His debts. Both are denied: there is no certificate, no creditor. "Behold, for your iniquities you were sold, and for your transgressions your mother was sent away." The exile was not God's bankruptcy; it was sin's consequence. And the deeper mystery: "Why, when I came, was there no man? Why, when I called, was there no one to answer?" God came calling and found an empty house — the tragedy is not His absence but our non-response. His arm is not shortened that it cannot save, nor His ear dull. The God who dries up the sea and clothes the heavens with blackness has not lost strength; His people have lost responsiveness. That distinction matters for every dry season: the arm is not short. Check the ear, check the answer, before you check the arm.

**The Servant's morning school (50:4–6).** Then the Servant speaks, and His first credential is not power but teachability: "The Lord GOD has given me the tongue of those who are taught, that I may know how to sustain with a word him who is weary. Morning by morning he awakens; he awakens my ear to hear as those who are taught." The Hebrew word for the taught is *limmudim* — disciples, learners. The Son of God, in His humanity, learned — morning by morning, ear awakened, in the school of the Father's word. That is why His words sustain the weary as no one else's can: He speaks as One who has listened. And the obedient ear leads directly to the obedient back: "The Lord GOD has opened my ear, and I was not rebellious; I did not turn backward. I gave my back to those who strike, and my cheeks to those who pull out the beard; I did not hide my face from mocking and spitting." In the ancient world, pulling the beard was contempt made physical; spitting was the signature of disgrace. The Servant does not merely endure these things — He *gives* His back. Voluntary. The sufferings of Christ were not an ambush that overtook Him; they were an obedience He walked into with His ear open and His face forward.

**The flint face and the near Vindicator (50:7–9).** "But the Lord GOD helps me; therefore I have not been disgraced; therefore I have set my face like a flint, and I know that I shall not be put to shame.
