He who vindicates me is near. Who will contend with me? Let us stand up together. Who is my adversary? Let him come near to me. Behold, the Lord GOD helps me; who will declare me guilty?" Flint is rock that strikes sparks — a face set like flint does not waver, flinch, or negotiate. And the ground of the resolve is not temperament but proximity: the Vindicator is *near*. Jesus set His face to go to Jerusalem (Luke 9:51) on exactly this confidence — the Father who would vindicate Him was near, and the resurrection was the vindication delivered on schedule (Romans 1:4). "All of them will wear out like a garment; the moth will eat them up" — the adversaries are perishables. The case is already decided by the calendar.

**Walk by His fire or by yours (50:10–11).** The chapter ends by dividing its hearers. To the one "who fears the LORD and obeys the voice of his servant," walking in darkness with no light of his own: "Let him trust in the name of the LORD and rely on his God." Darkness is not disqualification; it is the address where trusting happens. And to the self-sufficient: "Behold, all you who kindle a fire, who equip yourselves with burning torches! Walk by the light of your fire, and by the torches that you have kindled! This you have from my hand: you shall lie down in torment." Man-made light ends in a bed of torment. God's darkness, trusted in, ends in vindication. Those are the two options Isaiah leaves on the table — His fire or yours — and every person in recovery from self-reliance knows exactly what the torches cost.

### Key verses

> "Is my hand shortened, that it cannot redeem? Or have I no power to deliver?" — Isaiah 50:2 (ESV)

> "The Lord GOD has given me the tongue of those who are taught, that I may know how to sustain with a word him who is weary. Morning by morning he awakens; he awakens my ear to hear as those who are taught." — Isaiah 50:4 (ESV)

> "I gave my back to those who strike, and my cheeks to those who pull out the beard; I did not hide my face from mocking and spitting. But the Lord GOD helps me; therefore I have not been disgraced; therefore I have set my face like a flint, and I know that I shall not be put to shame." — Isaiah 50:6–7 (ESV)

> "Who among you fears the LORD and obeys the voice of his servant? Let him who walks in darkness and has no light trust in the name of the LORD and rely on his God." — Isaiah 50:10 (ESV)

### The bad news and the good news

The bad news of Isaiah 50 is that our exile is our own iniquity's doing, that God came calling and found no one answering, and that the torches we kindle for ourselves end in torment. The good news is that God's arm is not shortened, and His Servant — taught morning by morning, obedient unto a scourged back and a spat-upon face, vindicated by the God who is near — has sustained weary sinners with His word ever since. If you are walking in darkness with no light of your own, you are not outside the promise. You are its exact addressee: trust in the name of the LORD.

**A note from the Reformers.** Calvin saw in the Servant's awakened ear the pattern of all true ministry and all true discipleship: God opens ears before He loosens tongues, and the man who will not be taught morning by morning will have nothing with which to sustain the weary by afternoon.

**Reflection question:** Are you walking by God's darkness-trusting way, or by torches you kindled yourself? And what would a "morning by morning" awakened ear — a daily, teachable fifteen minutes with God — change about the words you have for weary people?

---

## Isaiah 51 — Listen to Me: The Rock You Were Hewn From, and the Cup Passed Over

### Jesus in this chapter

Jesus is the arm of the LORD in this chapter — the arm the exiles pray to see awake: "Awake, awake, put on strength, O arm of the LORD; awake, as in days of old, the generations of long ago. Was it not you who cut Rahab in pieces, who pierced the dragon?" John will tell us the arm of the LORD is revealed in Jesus — and that many, seeing Him, did not believe (John 12:38). He is also the comforter who answers His own people's prayer: "I, I am he who comforts you" (51:12) — the same God who says in Christ, "I will not leave you as orphans." And He is the one who drank the cup of wrath to its dregs so that His people would never drink it again: the cup passes from Jerusalem's trembling hand (51:22) to His hand in Gethsemane — "Let this cup pass from me" — where He drank what Isaiah says God took away from us.

### Commentary

**Look to the rock (51:1–8).** Three times the chapter opens with "Listen to me" — addressed first to "you who pursue righteousness, you who seek the LORD." Their first lesson is backward: "Look to the rock from which you were hewn, and to the quarry from which you were dug. Look to Abraham your father and to Sarah who bore you; for he was but one when I called him, that I might bless him and multiply him." Small beginnings are God's signature move: one old man, one barren woman, and a nation from them. A remnant that feels too small should always be sent back to the quarry. Then the comfort: "For the LORD comforts Zion; he comforts all her waste places and makes her wilderness like Eden, her desert like the garden of the LORD; joy and gladness will be found in her, thanksgiving and the voice of song." Eden again — God's endgame is always a garden restored. His righteousness draws near, His salvation has gone out, His arms judge the peoples — and against the heavens that will vanish like smoke and the earth that will wear out like a garment stands the permanent thing: "but my salvation will be forever, and my righteousness will never be dismayed." Then the word to the persecuted remnant, tender and defiant at once: "Listen to me, you who know righteousness, the people in whose heart is my law; fear not the reproach of man, nor be dismayed at their revilings. For the moth will eat them up like a garment... but my righteousness will be forever." Moths eat the revilers' garments; God's righteousness outlasts the moths.

**Awake, arm of the LORD (51:9–16).** The people pray the boldest prayer in the book — telling God to wake up: "Awake, awake, put on strength, O arm of the LORD." It is not unbelief; it is faith quoting history back to God: You cut Rahab — Egypt, the sea-monster — in pieces; You dried up the sea for the ransomed to pass over. "And the ransomed of the LORD shall return and come to Zion with singing" — Isaiah 35's highway, prayed for as memory and prophecy at once. God's answer corrects the prayer's premise with comfort, not rebuke: "I, I am he who comforts you; who are you that you are afraid of man who dies, of the son of man who is made like grass, and have forgotten the LORD, your Maker?" Fear of man is a memory problem: we remember the oppressor's size and forget our Maker's. The prisoners will be set free and not die in the pit; their bread will not be lacking; and God puts His words in His people's mouth and covers them in the shadow of His hand — the same hand-shadow that hid the polished arrow of chapter 49.

**The cup passes over (51:17–23).** Then Jerusalem is addressed as a drunk staggering from the cup of God's wrath: "You have drunk from the hand of the LORD the cup of his wrath, the bowl, the cup of staggering, you have drunk and drained it." Her devastation is complete — no one to guide her among all the sons she has borne; desolation, destruction, famine, and sword, with no comforter. And then the turn, one of the great gospel turns in the Old Testament: "Thus says your Lord, the LORD your God who pleads the cause of his people: 'Behold, I have taken from your hand the cup of staggering; the bowl of my wrath you shall drink no more. And I will put it into the hand of your tormentors, who have said to you, "Bow down, that we may pass over"; and you have made your back like the ground and like the street for them to pass over.'" God pleads the cause of the people He Himself judged — prosecutor turned advocate — and the cup changes hands. Every Christian knows where that cup finally landed: in Gethsemane's prayer and Golgotha's cry. The cup we shall drink no more is the cup He drained.

### Key verses

> "Look to the rock from which you were hewn, and to the quarry from which you were dug. Look to Abraham your father and to Sarah who bore you; for he was but one when I called him, that I might bless him and multiply him." — Isaiah 51:1–2 (ESV)

> "I, I am he who comforts you; who are you that you are afraid of man who dies, of the son of man who is made like grass, and have forgotten the LORD, your Maker, who stretched out the heavens and laid the foundations of the earth?" — Isaiah 51:12–13 (ESV)

> "Thus says your Lord, the LORD your God who pleads the cause of his people: 'Behold, I have taken from your hand the cup of staggering; the bowl of my wrath you shall drink no more.'" — Isaiah 51:22 (ESV)

### The bad news and the good news

The bad news of Isaiah 51 is that Jerusalem drank the cup of wrath to the dregs for her sins — and that fear of dying men still makes us forget our Maker, leaving us staggering under reproaches God never asked us to carry. The good news is that God pleads the cause of His own judged people, takes the cup out of their trembling hand forever, and puts it into the hand of their tormentors — a transfer completed when Jesus drank the cup in the garden and on the cross. The bowl of wrath, for everyone in Him, is empty. You shall drink no more.

**A note from the Reformers.** Luther loved the quarry sermon of verses 1–2: when the church feels small, God never tells her to look at her size but at her source — one Abraham, one Sarah, one call. Numbers were never the ground of hope; the God who multiplies the one is.

**Reflection question:** Which cup are you still trying to drink — guilt, shame, the reproach of men — that God says He has taken from your hand? And what "quarry" memory do you need to look back to, to remember what God does with small beginnings?

---

## Isaiah 52 — Awake, Awake, Zion: Beautiful Feet and the Servant Exalted

### Jesus in this chapter

This chapter runs from the most beautiful feet in the Bible to the most marred face. The feet belong to the messenger who publishes peace — "How beautiful upon the mountains are the feet of him who brings good news" — and Paul applies it to every gospel preacher sent with the news of Jesus (Romans 10:15). But the chapter's last three verses turn to the Servant Himself, in the opening of the fourth and greatest Servant Song (52:13–53:12): "Behold, my servant shall act wisely; he shall be high and lifted up, and shall be exalted." High and lifted up — the words of Isaiah 6:1, where the Lord sat on His throne — now applied to the Servant who will be marred beyond recognition. Kings shut their mouths because of Him. What they had never been told, they see. The exaltation of Jesus runs *through* the marring, not around it — and this chapter is the doorway into the holy of holies of Isaiah.

### Commentary

**Awake, awake — put on your strength (52:1–6).** For the third time God calls for waking: "Awake, awake, put on your strength, O Zion; put on your beautiful garments, O Jerusalem." The unclean and uncircumcised will never enter her again — the slave girl shakes off the dust, sits up from the ground, and looses the bonds from her neck. "You were sold for nothing, and you shall be redeemed without money" — redeemed, but not with cash; the price will be of another kind entirely, and chapter 53 names it. The reason for the redemption is God's name: His people were taken away for nothing, their rulers howl, "and my name is continually, all day long, despised among the nations" — the line Paul quotes in Romans 2:24. God's response is not embarrassment but promise: "Therefore my people shall know my name. Therefore in that day they shall know that it is I who speak; here I am." *Here I am* — the God who seems absent announces His presence in advance.

**Beautiful feet and the shout of the watchmen (52:7–10).** "How beautiful upon the mountains are the feet of him who brings good news, who publishes peace, who brings good news of happiness, who publishes salvation, who says to Zion, 'Your God reigns.'" In a world of runners bringing casualty lists, the gospel-runner's feet are beautiful because of the cargo: peace, happiness, salvation, and the four-word headline, *Your God reigns*. The Hebrew word for good news is *besorah* — the Septuagint's *euangelion*, the New Testament's "gospel." Isaiah invented the word's career. Then the watchmen lift their voices together — they sing because they see "eye to eye" the return of the LORD to Zion: God coming home with His people, in plain sight. "The LORD has bared his holy arm before the eyes of all the nations, and all the ends of the earth shall see the salvation of our God." The bared arm is the rolled-up sleeve of a worker — God getting His arm into the job personally. That arm, John and Luke both testify, is Jesus (John 12:38; Luke 3:6 quotes the promise over His forerunner).

**Depart, depart — and the Servant lifted up (52:11–15).** The exodus call sounds again: "Depart, depart, go out from there; touch no unclean thing; go out from the midst of her; purify yourselves, you who bear the vessels of the LORD." Paul quotes it to the Corinthian church about separation from idolatry (2 Corinthians 6:17). But this exodus is unhurried: "For you shall not go out in haste, and you shall not go in flight, for the LORD will go before you, and the God of Israel will be your rear guard." The first exodus fled by night; this one walks out behind and before God Himself. Then, without warning, the camera leaves the procession and fixes on one figure: "Behold, my servant shall act wisely; he shall be high and lifted up, and shall be exalted. As many were astonished at you — his appearance was so marred, beyond human semblance, and his form beyond that of the children of mankind — so shall he sprinkle many nations. Kings shall shut their mouths because of him, for that which has not been told them they see, and that which they have not heard they understand." Marred beyond human semblance — beaten past recognition — and yet the nations are sprinkled by Him, the priestly word for cleansing blood applied to Gentiles. The astonishment runs in both directions: horror at His suffering, and speechless wonder at His exaltation. Chapter 53 now explains the marring. Take off your shoes.

### Key verses

> "How beautiful upon the mountains are the feet of him who brings good news, who publishes peace, who brings good news of happiness, who publishes salvation, who says to Zion, 'Your God reigns.'" — Isaiah 52:7 (ESV)

> "The LORD has bared his holy arm before the eyes of all the nations, and all the ends of the earth shall see the salvation of our God." — Isaiah 52:10 (ESV)

> "Behold, my servant shall act wisely; he shall be high and lifted up, and shall be exalted. As many were astonished at you — his appearance was so marred, beyond human semblance, and his form beyond that of the children of mankind — so shall he sprinkle many nations." — Isaiah 52:13–14 (ESV)

### The bad news and the good news

The bad news of Isaiah 52 is that God's name is despised among the nations because of His people's failures, and that the Servant who would fix it would be marred beyond human semblance doing so — our sin wrote that damage on His face. The good news is that your God reigns: the beautiful feet have run, the holy arm is bared, the exiles walk out unhurried with God before and behind them, and the marred Servant sprinkles many nations clean. The exaltation is as certain as the marring was terrible.

**A note from the Reformers.** Calvin said the church should never grow tired of the phrase "Your God reigns," because it is the sum of all preaching comfort: not "your circumstances improve" or "your enemies retreat," but a reigning God, present tense, announced by runners whose feet are beautiful because their news is.

**Reflection question:** Whose "beautiful feet" brought the good news to you — and to whom are your feet supposed to be running now? What does "Your God reigns" need to mean over one specific fear this week?

---

## Isaiah 53 — The Suffering Servant: Pierced for Our Transgressions

### Jesus in this chapter

There is no "in this chapter" needed here the way other chapters need it, because this chapter is Jesus — the most complete portrait of His suffering and its meaning ever written, seven hundred years before the cross. Every verse is handled below, because every verse deserves it. The Ethiopian eunuch was reading this chapter when he asked Philip, "About whom, I ask you, does the prophet say this, about himself or about someone else?" — and Philip, "beginning with this Scripture, told him the good news about Jesus" (Acts 8:34–35). The church has answered the eunuch's question the same way ever since: not about Isaiah, and not about Israel as a nation — about Jesus of Nazareth, the Lamb of God. If you read only one chapter of this commentary with full attention, read this one. It is the Bible in miniature, and it is the reason The Hope Connection exists.

### Commentary

**The report nobody believed (53:1).** "Who has believed what he has heard from us? And to whom has the arm of the LORD been revealed?" The song opens with unbelief — ours. The message of a crucified Savior has always sounded impossible, and Isaiah predicts the shrug in advance. John quotes this verse over Jesus' rejected ministry (John 12:38), and Paul quotes it over Israel's unbelief (Romans 10:16). Do not miss the pairing: believing the report and seeing the arm of the LORD are the same event. Where the report is believed, the arm is revealed; where the arm seems hidden, the report is usually being refused.

**The shoot with no beauty (53:2).** "For he grew up before him like a young plant, and like a root out of dry ground; he had no form or majesty that we should look at him, and no beauty that we should desire him." A root out of dry ground — life where life should not be, in the parched soil of Israel's failure and a peasant girl's obscurity. And the devastating aesthetic verdict: nothing about His appearance drew us. No form, no majesty, no beauty — by the world's measurements, the most forgettable-looking Savior imaginable. God deliberately sent salvation in a package the eye would pass over, so that faith, not taste, would be the door. If you have ever felt overlooked, unremarkable, easy to pass by — your Savior was all of that first, on purpose.

**Despised and rejected (53:3).** "He was despised and rejected by men, a man of sorrows and acquainted with grief; and as one from whom men hide their faces he was despised, and we esteemed him not." *A man of sorrows* — the Hebrew is plural and intensive: sorrows were His native country. *Acquainted with grief* — grief was not a visitor to Jesus; it was a longtime acquaintance. And the response of men: they hid their faces. We could not look at Him. "We esteemed him not" — we priced Him at zero. Every person who has been despised, written off, or looked away from should know: the Savior was treated exactly that way, by the very people He came to save, and He stayed.

**Surely He has borne our griefs (53:4).** "Surely he has borne our griefs and carried our sorrows; yet we esteemed him stricken, smitten by God, and afflicted." Matthew quotes this verse over Jesus' healing ministry in Capernaum — the sick and demon-oppressed brought to Him at sundown, and "he healed all who were sick. This was to fulfill what was spoken by the prophet Isaiah: 'He took our illnesses and bore our diseases'" (Matthew 8:16–17). Before the cross, in the healings, the bearing had already begun. And notice the tragic misreading: "we esteemed him stricken, smitten by God" — the onlookers at the cross thought His suffering proved God's rejection of Him. It proved the opposite. He was smitten — but with our sentence, not His.

**Pierced, crushed, chastised, wounded — for us (53:5).** This is the center of the chapter, the center of Isaiah, and many would say the center of the Bible: "But he was pierced for our transgressions; he was crushed for our iniquities; upon him was the chastisement that brought us peace, and with his wounds we are healed." Four blows, four causes. Pierced — the nails and the spear. Crushed — the weight of Gethsemane and Golgotha. Chastised — punished, as a guilty man is punished. Wounded — striped, scourged. And every blow lands *for* something of ours: our transgressions, our iniquities. The exchange is total and one-directional: His wounds, our peace; His stripes, our healing. The Hebrew word for peace is *shalom* — the chastisement that brought us *shalom* fell on Him. Peter, who watched it happen, preaches this verse verbatim to Christians: "He himself bore our sins in his body on the tree... By his wounds you have been healed" (1 Peter 2:24). This is substitution, stated without embarrassment and without rival. Someone had to be pierced. It was going to be us. It was Him.

**All we like sheep (53:6).** "All we like sheep have gone astray; we have turned — every one — to his own way; and the LORD has laid on him the iniquity of us all." The most democratic verse in the Bible: *all we*. Sheep do not rebel dramatically; they drift, nibble by nibble, each to his own way — the sin of self-direction more than of spectacle. Every one to his own way: that is the human story in six words. And against the universal straying stands the universal transfer: the LORD — God Himself, the offended party — *laid on Him* the iniquity of us all. The Hebrew verb means to cause to fall upon, to strike with a blow. God did not wait for us to find our way back to the fold and then negotiate. He gathered the whole straying flock's guilt and laid it, deliberately, on the Shepherd.

**The silent Lamb (53:7).** "He was oppressed, and he was afflicted, yet he opened not his mouth; like a lamb that is led to the slaughter, and like a sheep that before its shearers is silent, so he opened not his mouth." This is the verse the Ethiopian was reading. Jesus before the Sanhedrin: silent. Before Pilate: silent, so that the governor marveled greatly. Before Herod: not a word. The silence was not weakness or resignation; it was willingness — the Lamb was not dragged; He was led, and He went. A sheep does not know what the shearers intend; Jesus knew exactly what the slaughter meant, and opened not His mouth anyway. Our defense attorney at the cross was silence, because any word He spoke in His own defense would have left us undefended.

**Cut off, and buried with the rich (53:8–9).** "By oppression and judgment he was taken away; and as for his generation, who considered that he was cut off out of the land of the living, stricken for the transgression of my people? And they made his grave with the wicked and with a rich man in his death, although he had done no violence, and there was no deceit in his mouth." The trial was oppression dressed as judgment; His contemporaries barely registered that He was dying *for the transgression of my people*. And the burial detail — wicked in intention, rich in fact — was fulfilled to the letter when Joseph of Arimathea, a rich man, laid Him in his own new tomb (Matthew 27:57–60). No violence done, no deceit spoken: Peter quotes this too (1 Peter 2:22), holding up the innocent sufferer as both sacrifice and example. The grave with the wicked failed to keep Him classified with them; the rich man's tomb became the doorway of the resurrection.

**It was the will of the LORD to crush him (53:10).** The hardest and most necessary verse in the chapter: "Yet it was the will of the LORD to crush him; he has put him to grief; when his soul makes an offering for sin, he shall see his offspring; he shall prolong his days; the will of the LORD shall prosper in his hand." The cross was not an accident that God salvaged; it was the will of the LORD — planned, purposed, and (Acts 2:23) delivered up according to "the definite plan and foreknowledge of God." The Father was not a spectator at Calvary; He was the author of the offering. And the crushed One does not stay crushed: He *sees His offspring* — a family born from His death; He *prolongs His days* — resurrection, stated in the middle of the death-song. His soul makes an offering for sin — the Hebrew is *asham*, the guilt offering of Leviticus — and the offering works: the will of the LORD prospers in the dead-and-risen Servant's hand.

**He shall see and be satisfied (53:11).** "Out of the anguish of his soul he shall see and be satisfied; by his knowledge shall the righteous one, my servant, make many to be accounted righteous, and he shall bear their iniquities." The anguish produces sight and satisfaction — Jesus, "who for the joy that was set before him endured the cross" (Hebrews 12:2), now sees the harvest and calls it worth it. And the mechanism of salvation is stated in courtroom language: the righteous One makes *many* to be *accounted righteous* — justified, reckoned righteous — *because* He bears their iniquities. This is the doctrine of justification by faith in seed form, the verse Romans is built on. He bears; we are accounted. He is righteous; we are made righteous in the accounting of God. Not by our knowledge of Him first, but "by his knowledge" — His knowing, His doing, His bearing.

**Therefore I will divide him a portion with the many (53:12).** "Therefore I will divide him a portion with the many, and he shall divide the spoil with the strong, because he poured out his soul to death and was numbered with the transgressors; yet he bore the sin of many, and makes intercession for the transgressors." The song ends in a victory parade: the crushed Servant divides spoil like a conquering king. Why? *Because* He poured out His soul to death — the exaltation is earned by the emptying. He was numbered with the transgressors — crucified between two thieves, as Mark notes this very verse fulfilled (Mark 15:28; Luke 22:37 quotes Jesus applying it to Himself). And the last activity named is present and ongoing: He *makes intercession* for the transgressors. From the cross — "Father, forgive them" — to the right hand of God — "he always lives to make intercession for them" (Hebrews 7:25) — the Servant's work for transgressors did not end at His death. If you are a transgressor, you have, right now, an Intercessor who bore your sin and divides His spoil with you. That is Isaiah 53. That is the gospel. That is why we are Christians.

### Key verses

> "He was despised and rejected by men, a man of sorrows and acquainted with grief; and as one from whom men hide their faces he was despised, and we esteemed him not." — Isaiah 53:3 (ESV)

> "But he was pierced for our transgressions; he was crushed for our iniquities; upon him was the chastisement that brought us peace, and with his wounds we are healed." — Isaiah 53:5 (ESV)

> "All we like sheep have gone astray; we have turned — every one — to his own way; and the LORD has laid on him the iniquity of us all." — Isaiah 53:6 (ESV)

> "Out of the anguish of his soul he shall see and be satisfied; by his knowledge shall the righteous one, my servant, make many to be accounted righteous, and he shall bear their iniquities." — Isaiah 53:11 (ESV)

> "Therefore I will divide him a portion with the many, and he shall divide the spoil with the strong, because he poured out his soul to death and was numbered with the transgressors; yet he bore the sin of many, and makes intercession for the transgressors." — Isaiah 53:12 (ESV)

### The bad news and the good news

The bad news of Isaiah 53 is total: all we like sheep have gone astray, every one to his own way, and the iniquity was real enough to require piercing, crushing, and a soul poured out to death. There is no verse in this chapter that lets us minimize what our sin cost. The good news is the same chapter: the cost was paid — by Him. Pierced for our transgressions, He makes many accounted righteous; buried with the rich, He rose to divide the spoil; numbered with transgressors, He intercedes for transgressors still. If you bring Him nothing but your iniquity, you bring the one thing He came to carry.

**A note from the Reformers.** Luther said every Christian ought to know Isaiah 53 by heart, and he called the chapter the clearest gospel in the Old Testament — in his lectures he treated verse 5 as the sum of the faith: Christ's wounds, our peace; nothing of ours added, nothing of His withheld. Spurgeon, preaching the chapter, told his hearers that if they could only take one chapter of the Bible to a desert island of the soul, this was the one — because a man can live on Isaiah 53 forever.

**Reflection question:** Isaiah 53 leaves no spectator seats — you are either among the sheep who strayed and were carried home by His bearing, or still carrying your own iniquity yourself. Which is it, honestly — and if you have never let Him bear it, what is stopping you from handing it over today?

---

## Isaiah 54 — Sing, O Barren One: The Wife Restored and the Covenant of Peace

### Jesus in this chapter

Jesus is the Husband of this chapter — "For your Maker is your husband, the LORD of hosts is his name; and the Holy One of Israel is your Redeemer, the God of the whole earth he is called" — and the covenant He swears here is the new covenant in His blood: "my covenant of peace shall not be removed, says the LORD, who has compassion on you." He is the afflicted, storm-tossed wife's Redeemer who comes back for her after the "brief moment" of forsaking. He is the foundation of sapphires and the walls of precious stones — the city He builds is the bride of Revelation 21, whose builder and maker is God. And He is the heritage of the servants at the chapter's end: "No weapon that is fashioned against you shall succeed... This is the heritage of the servants of the LORD and their vindication from me, declares the LORD" — the vindication (*tsidqah*, righteousness) that comes from God, not from ourselves, which is exactly the righteousness Christ gives His servants.

### Commentary

**Sing, O barren one (54:1–3).** The chapter after the cross opens with an impossible command: "Sing, O barren one, who did not bear; break forth into singing and cry aloud, you who have not been in labor!" Barrenness singing — because the Servant's death has reversed the arithmetic: "For the children of the desolate one will be more than the children of her who is married." Paul quotes this verse in Galatians 4:27 for the church — the children born by promise outnumbering the children born by nature. The tent must be enlarged, the curtains lengthened, the cords stretched, the stakes strengthened — growth preparation commanded before the growth arrives. "For you will spread abroad to the right and to the left, and your offspring will possess the nations and will people the desolate cities." The desolate cities repopulated: that is what the Servant's family does to ruined places.

**The wife brought back (54:4–8).** Then the most tender marriage language in the prophets: "Fear not, for you will not be ashamed; be not confounded, for you will not be disgraced; for you will forget the shame of your youth and the reproach of your widowhood you will remember no more. For your Maker is your husband." The wife was called "like a wife deserted and grieved in spirit, like a wife of youth when she is cast off" — and the Husband's explanation of the exile is reframed as a lover's brief absence: "For a brief moment I deserted you, but with great compassion I will gather you. In overflowing anger for a moment I hid my face from you, but with everlasting love I will have compassion on you, says the LORD, your Redeemer." A brief moment versus everlasting love — the proportions of God's anger and God's love are stated in units of time, and they are not close. The Hebrew word for that love is *hesed* — covenant love, pledged love, the love that is bound by promise and not by mood. Our Redeemer does not love us at our best and tolerate us at our worst. He has sworn the proportion: momentary anger, everlasting hesed.

**The Noah oath (54:9–10).** God reaches back to His oldest recorded oath: "This is like the days of Noah to me: as I swore that the waters of Noah should no more go over the earth, so I have sworn that I will not be angry with you, and will not rebuke you." And then the verse that has held up collapsing believers for centuries: "For the mountains may depart and the hills be removed, but my steadfast love shall not depart from you, and my covenant of peace shall not be removed, says the LORD, who has compassion on you." Mountains are the Bible's symbol for the most permanent things in the landscape — and God says even if *they* relocate, His love does not. The covenant of peace is more stable than geology. If your faith is resting on anything less permanent than mountains, move it to this verse.

**The city of stones and the heritage of servants (54:11–17).** To the "afflicted one, storm-tossed and not comforted," God becomes a builder: foundations of sapphires, pinnacles of agate, gates of carbuncles, walls of precious stones — the blueprint Revelation 21 builds from. "All your children shall be taught by the LORD, and great shall be the peace of your children" — Jesus quotes this in John 6:45 to explain why people come to Him: they are taught by God. "In righteousness you shall be established" — the city's mortar is righteousness, given righteousness. And the closing charter: tyranny far away, terror kept at a distance, every weapon fashioned against God's servants failing, every tongue that rises against them in judgment condemned by the servants themselves. "This is the heritage of the servants of the LORD and their vindication from me." Heritage is not wages. It is what children receive because of whose they are.

### Key verses

> "For your Maker is your husband, the LORD of hosts is his name; and the Holy One of Israel is your Redeemer, the God of the whole earth he is called." — Isaiah 54:5 (ESV)

> "For a brief moment I deserted you, but with great compassion I will gather you. In overflowing anger for a moment I hid my face from you, but with everlasting love I will have compassion on you, says the LORD, your Redeemer." — Isaiah 54:7–8 (ESV)

> "For the mountains may depart and the hills be removed, but my steadfast love shall not depart from you, and my covenant of peace shall not be removed, says the LORD, who has compassion on you." — Isaiah 54:10 (ESV)

> "No weapon that is fashioned against you shall succeed, and you shall refute every tongue that rises against you in judgment. This is the heritage of the servants of the LORD and their vindication from me, declares the LORD." — Isaiah 54:17 (ESV)

### The bad news and the good news

The bad news of Isaiah 54 is that the wife was deserted for real sins — shame of youth, reproach of widowhood, a moment of overflowing anger that was entirely deserved — and that weapons really are fashioned against God's servants. The good news is that the Husband is the Maker, the moment of anger is brief, the love is everlasting, the covenant of peace is more durable than mountains, and the weapons fail by charter. In Christ, the deserted wife is gathered, taught, built of sapphires, and given a heritage she did not earn. Sing, barren one. The arithmetic has changed.

**A note from the Reformers.** Calvin treated Isaiah 54:10 as a complete answer to the trembling conscience: when a believer's feelings announce that God's love has departed, the verse replies with geology — sooner the mountains. Assurance, for the Reformers, was never a mood to manufacture but an oath to believe.

**Reflection question:** Do you measure God's love by the "brief moment" — the hard season, the hidden face — or by the everlasting hesed He has sworn? What mountain in your life have you been treating as more permanent than His covenant of peace?

---

## Isaiah 55 — Come, Buy Without Money: The Free Invitation and the Word That Does Not Return Empty

### Jesus in this chapter

Jesus is the host of this chapter's market and the substance of its feast. "Come, everyone who thirsts, come to the waters; and he who has no money, come, buy and eat!" — Jesus stands in the temple at the feast and cries the same invitation in His own voice: "If anyone thirsts, let him come to me and drink" (John 7:37). He is the David of the everlasting covenant — "I will make with you an everlasting covenant, my steadfast, sure love for David" — the Son of David to whom nations that did not know Him come running. He is the Word that goes out of God's mouth and does not return empty: the Word made flesh, who accomplished exactly what He was sent to do and came back with the harvest. And He is the pardon offered to the wicked who forsake their way: "let the wicked forsake his way... let him return to the LORD, that he may have compassion on him... for he will abundantly pardon." Abundantly pardon — in Christ, that is not poetry. It is policy.

### Commentary

**The free market (55:1–5).** "Come, everyone who thirsts, come to the waters; and he who has no money, come, buy and eat! Come, buy wine and milk without money and without price." Buy without money — the gospel's economics in one paradox: everything costs nothing because Someone else paid everything. Then the question that exposes every false feast: "Why do you spend your money for that which is not bread, and your labor for that which does not satisfy?" Money spent, labor spent, and still hungry — that is the balance sheet of every substitute satisfaction. "Listen diligently to me, and eat what is good, and delight yourselves in rich food. Incline your ear, and come to me; hear, that your soul may live; and I will make with you an everlasting covenant, my steadfast, sure love for David." Hearing is eating in this chapter — the soul lives by inclining an ear. The covenant offered is the Davidic covenant, the promise that put a Son on an eternal throne, now opened to everyone who comes thirsty. "Behold, you shall call a nation that you do not know, and a nation that did not know you shall run to you, because of the LORD your God" — the magnet is not Israel's attractiveness but the Holy One's glory on her. Missions are built into the invitation from the start.

**Seek Him while He may be found (55:6–9).** "Seek the LORD while he may be found; call upon him while he is near; let the wicked forsake his way, and the unrighteous man his thoughts; let him return to the LORD, that he may have compassion on him, and to our God, for he will abundantly pardon." The Hebrew for abundantly is *harbeh* — He will multiply pardon, pardon in heaps. The condition is not achievement but direction: forsake the way, forsake the thoughts, return. And then God explains why His pardon can be so much larger than our expectation: "For my thoughts are not your thoughts, neither are your ways my ways, declares the LORD. For as the heavens are higher than the earth, so are my ways higher than your ways and my thoughts than your thoughts." This verse is usually quoted about God's mysterious providence; in context it is about His *mercy* being higher than ours. We pardon carefully, in measured doses, keeping accounts. His thoughts of pardon are heavens higher. The distance between our forgiveness and His is the distance between earth and sky.

**The word that accomplishes (55:10–13).** "For as the rain and the snow come down from heaven and do not return there but water the earth, making it bring forth and sprout, giving seed to the sower and bread to the eater, so shall my word be that goes out from my mouth; it shall not return to me empty, but it shall accomplish that which I purpose, and shall succeed in the thing for which I sent it." God's word is weather, not commentary — it does things. Every sermon, every Scripture opened, every verse spoken over a hospital bed participates in that guarantee: not empty, not failed, not wasted. The chapter ends in a world remade: "For you shall go out in joy and be led forth in peace; the mountains and the hills before you shall break forth into singing, and all the trees of the field shall clap their hands. Instead of the thorn shall come up the cypress; instead of the brier shall come up the myrtle; and it shall make a name for the LORD, an everlasting sign that shall not be cut off." Thorns were the curse's signature (Genesis 3:18); cypress and myrtle replacing them means the curse itself is being unplanted. Creation applauds the homecoming of the pardoned.

### Key verses

> "Come, everyone who thirsts, come to the waters; and he who has no money, come, buy and eat! Come, buy wine and milk without money and without price. Why do you spend your money for that which is not bread, and your labor for that which does not satisfy?" — Isaiah 55:1–2 (ESV)

> "Seek the LORD while he may be found; call upon him while he is near; let the wicked forsake his way, and the unrighteous man his thoughts; let him return to the LORD, that he may have compassion on him, and to our God, for he will abundantly pardon." — Isaiah 55:6–7 (ESV)

> "So shall my word be that goes out from my mouth; it shall not return to me empty, but it shall accomplish that which I purpose, and shall succeed in the thing for which I sent it." — Isaiah 55:11 (ESV)

### The bad news and the good news

The bad news of Isaiah 55 is that we have spent our money on what is not bread and our labor on what does not satisfy — and that the seeking has a window: "while he may be found." The good news is that the feast is free, the pardon is abundant beyond the heavens' height, and the Word that invites us cannot return to God empty — it will accomplish His purpose in everyone who hears and comes. You do not need money for this market. You need thirst.

**A note from the Reformers.** Spurgeon preached Isaiah 55:1 as the charter of free grace and loved the absurdity of it: a market where the only currency accepted is having none. The man with empty pockets and an honest thirst, he said, is the richest customer in God's market — because the price was paid at a cross, not at the till.

**Reflection question:** What have you been spending money, labor, and years on that is "not bread"? What would it mean to bring your thirst — and your empty pockets — to God's market today, and simply come?

---
## Isaiah 56 — The House of Prayer for All Peoples: Foreigners and Eunuchs Welcome

### Jesus in this chapter

Jesus claims this chapter with His own mouth. Standing in the temple court with a whip of cords, He quotes it: "Is it not written, 'My house shall be called a house of prayer for all the nations'?" (Mark 11:17; Isaiah 56:7). The house Jesus cleansed was being run as a marketplace in the very court — the Court of the Gentiles — that this chapter reserves for the foreigners. Jesus is also the gatherer of verse 8: "The Lord GOD, who gathers the outcasts of Israel, declares, 'I will gather yet others to him besides those already gathered.'" And He is the hope of the eunuch, who was barred from the assembly under the old order (Deuteronomy 23:1) but is promised here "a monument and a name better than sons and daughters... an everlasting name that shall not be cut off" — the promise the Ethiopian eunuch of Acts 8, reading Isaiah on his way home from Jerusalem, was about to receive in baptism. The first Gentile convert of the church age was a eunuch holding this book. That is not a coincidence.

### Commentary

**Justice, Sabbath, and the welcome (56:1–8).** The book's third movement opens with ethics before ecstasy: "Keep justice, and do righteousness, for soon my salvation will come, and my righteousness be revealed. Blessed is the man who does this... who keeps the Sabbath, not profaning it, and keeps his hand from doing any evil." Salvation is coming soon, and the appropriate preparation is not speculation but justice and rest. Then the door swings wide to the two classes of people most sure they were shut out. The foreigner must not say, "The LORD will surely separate me from his people" — God answers with covenant membership, Sabbath joy, and a place in the house: "these I will bring to my holy mountain, and make them joyful in my house of prayer; their burnt offerings and their sacrifices will be accepted on my altar." And the eunuch must not say, "Behold, I am a dry tree" — the man with no descendants, no future, no name to carry on — "to the eunuchs who keep my Sabbaths... I will give in my house and within my walls a monument and a name better than sons and daughters; I will give them an everlasting name that shall not be cut off." The Hebrew word for monument is *yad* — a hand, a memorial stone. Better than sons and daughters: a name from God's own hand, everlasting, in God's own house. Nobody who comes to God in Christ is a dry tree. Nobody's story ends with what they cannot produce.

**The blind watchmen (56:9–12).** Then the chapter snaps to the leaders who were supposed to guard this welcome: "All you beasts of the field, come to devour — all you beasts in the forest. His watchmen are blind; they are all without knowledge; they are all silent dogs; they cannot bark; dreaming, lying down, loving to slumber." Watchdogs that cannot bark — the failure is not malice but sleep. "The dogs have a mighty appetite; they never have enough. But they are shepherds who have no understanding; they have all turned to their own way, each to his own gain, one and all." And their motto, quoted with contempt, is Isaiah 22's creed in a new key: "Come, let me get wine; let us fill ourselves with strong drink; and tomorrow will be like this day, great beyond measure." Shepherds drunk on today, promising tomorrow will be the same. Against them stands the open house of verses 1–8: God gathering outcasts while His watchmen sleep. The church in every age is one of these two pictures. Choose the gathering.

### Key verses

> "Thus says the LORD: 'Keep justice, and do righteousness, for soon my salvation will come, and my righteousness be revealed.'" — Isaiah 56:1 (ESV)

> "And the foreigners who join themselves to the LORD, to minister to him, to love the name of the LORD, and to be his servants... these I will bring to my holy mountain, and make them joyful in my house of prayer; their burnt offerings and their sacrifices will be accepted on my altar; for my house shall be called a house of prayer for all peoples." — Isaiah 56:6–7 (ESV)

> "The Lord GOD, who gathers the outcasts of Israel, declares, 'I will gather yet others to him besides those already gathered.'" — Isaiah 56:8 (ESV)

### The bad news and the good news

The bad news of Isaiah 56 is that God's own watchmen can be blind, silent, and drunk while the beasts circle the flock — and that foreigners and eunuchs, the outcasts, have often been told by God's people that the door is closed to them. The good news is that the Lord GOD gathers the outcasts Himself, gives the dry tree an everlasting name better than sons and daughters, and calls His house a house of prayer for *all* peoples. In Jesus, the gathering has begun and the door stands open. If you have ever been told you do not belong, hear the Gatherer's own word over the watchmen's sleep: "I will gather yet others."

**A note from the Reformers.** Calvin noted that this chapter demolishes every attempt to make God's house smaller than God's mercy: the foreigner and the eunuch are not tolerated at the margins but brought to the mountain, made joyful, and given accepted sacrifices. The Reformation's recovery of the priesthood of all believers is Isaiah 56 applied to the whole congregation.

**Reflection question:** Have you ever felt like the foreigner or the dry tree — sure the door was for other people? And are you, anywhere, a sleeping watchman while God is gathering outcasts you have not noticed?

---

## Isaiah 57 — The High and Holy One Dwells with the Contrite

### Jesus in this chapter

Jesus is the high and lifted up One of this chapter — "For thus says the One who is high and lifted up, who inhabits eternity, whose name is Holy" — the same throne-language as Isaiah 6, now bent low in mercy: "I dwell in the high and holy place, and also with him who is of a contrite and lowly spirit, to revive the spirit of the lowly, and to revive the heart of the contrite." That is the incarnation in one sentence: the God of eternity dwelling, at the same time, in heaven and in the crushed human heart. Jesus is also the peace of the chapter's end — "Peace, peace, to the far and to the near, says the LORD, and I will heal him" — the promise Paul preaches in Ephesians 2:17 of Christ Himself: "And he came and preached peace to you who were far off and peace to those who were near." And He is the healer of the backslider: "I have seen his ways, but I will heal him; I will lead him and restore comfort to him and his mourners."

### Commentary

**The righteous perish, and no one lays it to heart (57:1–2).** The chapter opens with a quiet tragedy: "The righteous man perishes, and no one lays it to heart; devout men are taken away, while no one understands. For the righteous man is taken away from calamity; he enters into peace; they rest in their beds who walk in their uprightness." Sometimes God's mercy to His people looks like loss to everyone else: the righteous are taken *from* calamity, gathered before the storm, and their death is an entrance into peace — not a defeat. In a generation rushing toward judgment, the godly dying early are not the unlucky ones. Their beds are rest. It is a verse to remember at the graves of believers who died before the hard years came.

**Sons of the sorceress (57:3–13).** Then the indictment of the idolatrous generation, and it is unsparing: children of transgression who burn with lust among the oaks, slaughter children in the valleys, pour drink offerings to smooth stones — "Among the smooth stones of the valley is your portion" — and set their memorial behind doors and posts, making covenants with the gods of the nations and the bed of Molech. Even weariness does not stop them: "You were wearied with the length of your way, but you did not say, 'It is hopeless'; you found new life for your strength, and so you were not faint." Sin renews its own energy like a drug. And God's question cuts through all of it: "Whom did you dread and fear, so that you lied... Did you not fear me? But I have been silent and hidden from sight" — His silence misread as absence, His patience misread as permission. The verdict on the whole collection of idols is one line: "When you cry out, let your collection of idols deliver you! The wind will carry them all off, a breath will take them away. But he who takes refuge in me shall possess the land and shall inherit my holy mountain." A breath disperses the pantheon; the refuge-taker inherits the mountain.

**The highway for the contrite (57:14–21).** Then the voice of chapter 40 returns: "Build up, build up, prepare the way, remove every obstruction from my people's way." And the promise that stands among the highest in all Scripture: "For thus says the One who is high and lifted up, who inhabits eternity, whose name is Holy: 'I dwell in the high and holy place, and also with him who is of a contrite and lowly spirit, to revive the spirit of the lowly, and to revive the heart of the contrite.'" Two addresses for God: eternity, and the crushed heart. The Hebrew word for contrite is *dakka* — pulverized, ground to dust. God does not say He visits the contrite, or approves of them from the throne. He *dwells* with them. "For I will not contend forever, nor will I always be angry" — His anger has a term limit because His Spirit-made creatures could not survive it otherwise. And the healing word lands on the very backslider just indicted: "I have seen his ways, but I will heal him; I will lead him and restore comfort to him and his mourners, creating the fruit of the lips. Peace, peace, to the far and to the near, says the LORD, and I will heal him." Seen his ways — all of them — *but I will heal him*. That "but" is the gospel in a conjunction. The chapter ends, as chapters 48 did, with the boundary: "But the wicked are like the tossing sea; for it cannot be quiet... 'There is no peace,' says my God, 'for the wicked.'" The sea cannot be quiet; the contrite cannot be un-dwelt-with. Two impossibilities, and we choose which one describes us.

### Key verses

> "For thus says the One who is high and lifted up, who inhabits eternity, whose name is Holy: 'I dwell in the high and holy place, and also with him who is of a contrite and lowly spirit, to revive the spirit of the lowly, and to revive the heart of the contrite.'" — Isaiah 57:15 (ESV)

> "I have seen his ways, but I will heal him; I will lead him and restore comfort to him and his mourners, creating the fruit of the lips. Peace, peace, to the far and to the near, says the LORD, and I will heal him." — Isaiah 57:18–19 (ESV)

> "But the wicked are like the tossing sea; for it cannot be quiet, and its waters toss up mire and dirt. 'There is no peace,' says my God, 'for the wicked.'" — Isaiah 57:20–21 (ESV)

### The bad news and the good news

The bad news of Isaiah 57 is that sin renews its own strength like a drug, that God's silence gets misread as permission, and that the wicked are a sea that cannot be quiet — no peace, by nature, ever. The good news is that the God who inhabits eternity also dwells with the pulverized: He has seen our ways — all of them — and His sentence is "but I will heal him." Peace, peace, to the far and to the near, is preached by Christ Himself, and the contrite heart is the one address in the universe where the high and holy One has promised to live.

**A note from the Reformers.** Luther called Isaiah 57:15 the verse that taught him where God lives: not in the confident, the decorated, or the self-assured, but in the crushed — and he said a man who wants to find God should stop looking up in speculation and start looking down into his own broken heart, where God has promised His address.

**Reflection question:** Are you trying to get God to dwell with your improved self, or will you let Him dwell where He says He lives — with the contrite and lowly spirit? What needs to be pulverized, honestly, today?

---

## Isaiah 58 — The Fast God Chooses: Loosing Bonds and Sharing Bread

### Jesus in this chapter

Jesus is the standard of this chapter's true fast — and its fulfillment. When the Pharisees measured Him by fasting customs, He answered as the Bridegroom (Matthew 9:14–15), and His own ministry was Isaiah 58 enacted: loosing the bonds of the demon-bound, feeding the hungry multitudes, housing Himself with outcasts, and calling the heavy-laden to His yoke instead of theirs. He is also the rebuilder promised at the chapter's end — "you shall be called the repairer of the breach, the restorer of streets to dwell in" — the One who repairs the breach between God and man and between man and man. And He is the Lord of the Sabbath this chapter guards, who called Himself Lord of the Sabbath and made the day a delight by filling it with mercy (Matthew 12:7–8).

### Commentary

**Cry aloud: the fast that is a fraud (58:1–5).** God tells Isaiah to preach at full volume: "Cry aloud; do not hold back; lift up your voice like a trumpet; declare to my people their transgression." The people in question are not pagans — they "seek me daily and delight to know my ways, as if they were a nation that did righteousness." They fast, they humble themselves, they bow their heads like reeds and spread sackcloth and ashes — and they ask, bewildered, "Why have we fasted, and you see it not?" God's answer names the fraud precisely: "Behold, in the day of your fast you seek your own pleasure, and oppress all your workers. Behold, you fast only to quarrel and to fight and to hit with a wicked fist." A fast that coexists with exploitation and violence is theater. "Is such the fast that I choose, a day for a person to humble himself? Is it to bow down his head like a reed, and to spread sackcloth and ashes under him? Will you call this a fast, and a day acceptable to the LORD?" Religious performance measured by posture, while the workers are oppressed and the fists are flying — God calls it unacceptable in the form of a question that answers itself.

**The fast God chooses (58:6–12).** Then the positive definition, one of the great ethical passages of the Old Testament: "Is not this the fast that I choose: to loose the bonds of wickedness, to undo the straps of the yoke, to let the oppressed go free, and to break every yoke? Is it not to share your bread with the hungry and bring the homeless poor into your house; when you see the naked, to cover him, and not to hide yourself from your own flesh?" The Hebrew word for yoke is *motah* — the bar that binds a beast or a slave. God's chosen fast is not primarily about food refused but about burdens removed: bonds loosed, yokes broken, bread shared, homeless housed, naked clothed, family not hidden from. And the promises attached are luminous: "Then shall your light break forth like the dawn, and your healing shall spring up speedily; your righteousness shall go before you; the glory of the LORD shall be your rear guard. Then you shall call, and the LORD will answer; you shall cry, and he will say, 'Here I am.'" *Here I am* — the answer God gives to people who answer others that way. "If you pour yourself out for the hungry and satisfy the desire of the afflicted, then shall your light rise in the darkness and your gloom be as the noonday." Poured-out people become lighted people. "And the LORD will guide you continually and satisfy your desire in scorched places and make your bones strong; and you shall be like a watered garden, like a spring of water, whose waters do not fail." And the ruins get rebuilt: "Your ancient ruins shall be rebuilt; you shall raise up the foundations of many generations; you shall be called the repairer of the breach, the restorer of streets to dwell in." A ministry to the broken, in Isaiah's vision, ends with streets people can live on again.

**The Sabbath delight (58:13–14).** The chapter closes by guarding the day of rest from the same fraud: "If you turn back your foot from the Sabbath, from doing your pleasure on my holy day, and call the Sabbath a delight and the holy day of the LORD honorable... then you shall take delight in the LORD, and I will make you ride on the heights of the earth." Delight is the test-word twice over. God is not honored by a Sabbath of gritted teeth any more than by a fast of flying fists; He is honored when His day — and His ways — become our delight, "for the mouth of the LORD has spoken."

### Key verses

> "Is not this the fast that I choose: to loose the bonds of wickedness, to undo the straps of the yoke, to let the oppressed go free, and to break every yoke? Is it not to share your bread with the hungry and bring the homeless poor into your house; when you see the naked, to cover him, and not to hide yourself from your own flesh?" — Isaiah 58:6–7 (ESV)

> "Then you shall call, and the LORD will answer; you shall cry, and he will say, 'Here I am.'" — Isaiah 58:9 (ESV)

> "And the LORD will guide you continually and satisfy your desire in scorched places and make your bones strong; and you shall be like a watered garden, like a spring of water, whose waters do not fail." — Isaiah 58:11 (ESV)

> "You shall be called the repairer of the breach, the restorer of streets to dwell in." — Isaiah 58:12 (ESV)

### The bad news and the good news

The bad news of Isaiah 58 is that God refuses our religious performances when they coexist with oppression — a fast with flying fists is an unacceptable day, whatever the sackcloth looks like. The good news is that the life God chooses is better than the one He refuses: loose bonds, share bread, house the homeless, and your light breaks like dawn, your gloom becomes noonday, and when you cry, God says, "Here I am." In Christ, the Repairer of the breach has come — and He makes repairers out of everyone He repairs.

**A note from the Reformers.** Calvin insisted that Isaiah 58 is God's own commentary on all outward worship: ceremonies without mercy are not merely incomplete but offensive, because God reads our treatment of the hungry as the truest transcript of our prayers. The Reformers' diaconal reforms — organized care for the poor in Geneva — were this chapter with a city budget.

**Reflection question:** Where is your religion currently posture without practice — a fast, a habit, a service — while a yoke near you stays unbroken? Whose bread, house, or covering is God asking from you this week?

---

## Isaiah 59 — His Arm Is Not Shortened: Sin's Confession and the Redeemer Who Comes to Zion

### Jesus in this chapter

Jesus is the Redeemer who comes to Zion at this chapter's climax — "And a Redeemer will come to Zion, to those in Jacob who turn from transgression, declares the LORD" — the verse Paul quotes at the climax of Romans 11 for Israel's future salvation (Romans 11:26). He is also the Warrior of verses 16–17, who saw there was no man, no intercessor, and so His own arm brought salvation: "He put on righteousness as a breastplate, and a helmet of salvation on his head; he put on garments of vengeance for clothing, and wrapped himself in zeal as a cloak." Paul takes that armor and hands it to the church in Ephesians 6 — because the armor was Christ's first. And He is the answer to the chapter's opening problem: our iniquities have separated us from God — so God came Himself, in His own arm, across the separation.

### Commentary

**Not His arm, not His ear — our sins (59:1–8).** The chapter opens by correcting the exile's theology: "Behold, the LORD's hand is not shortened, that it cannot save, or his ear dull, that it cannot hear. But your iniquities have made a separation between you and your God, and your sins have hidden his face from you so that he does not hear." Isaiah 50:2 asked the same question; now it gets the full answer. The problem is not God's capacity but man's barrier — and Isaiah itemizes the barrier without flinching: hands defiled with blood, fingers with iniquity, lips speaking lies, tongues muttering perverseness; no one enters suit justly; webs of spiders and eggs of adders — works that cannot cover and food that kills; feet that run to evil and shed innocent blood; thoughts of iniquity, desolation and destruction in their highways. "The way of peace they do not know, and there is no justice in their paths; they have made their roads crooked; no one who treads on them knows peace." Paul stitches verses 7–8 into the great indictment of Romans 3:15–17 — Isaiah's description of Judah becomes the Spirit's description of the whole human race. That is the point of the chapter's first half: this is not their problem. It is ours.

**The confession (59:9–15).** Then the pronouns change, and the prophet climbs inside the guilt: "*We* look for light, and behold, darkness... We grope for the wall like the blind... we stumble at noon as in the twilight, among those in full vigor we are like dead men. We all growl like bears; we moan and moan like doves." This is true confession — not "they" but "we," not excuses but inventory: "For our transgressions are multiplied before you, and our sins testify against us... transgressing, and denying the LORD, and turning back from following our God." And the public consequence: "Justice is turned back, and righteousness stands far away; for truth has stumbled in the public squares, and uprightness cannot enter. Truth is lacking, and he who departs from evil makes himself a prey." When truth stumbles in the square, the honest man becomes the target. Isaiah has seen our century too.

**His own arm brought salvation (59:16–21).** "The LORD saw it, and it displeased him that there was no justice. He saw that there was no man, and wondered that there was no one to intercede; then his own arm brought him salvation, and his righteousness upheld him." No man, no intercessor — so God became both. That is the incarnation's logic stated in advance: the arm of the LORD, which is a Person (Isaiah 53:1), does the interceding and the saving that no human could do. He arms for battle — breastplate of righteousness, helmet of salvation, garments of vengeance, cloak of zeal — and repays wrath to His adversaries and repayment to His enemies, until "they shall fear the name of the LORD from the west, and his glory from the rising of the sun; for he will come like a rushing stream, which the wind of the LORD drives." Then the promise the whole chapter was building to: "And a Redeemer will come to Zion, to those in Jacob who turn from transgression, declares the LORD." To those who *turn* — the Redeemer comes to repentant people, not perfect ones. And the covenant that seals it is about the Word and the Spirit staying in the family: "'And as for me, this is my covenant with them,' says the LORD: 'My Spirit that is upon you, and my words that I have put in your mouth, shall not depart out of your mouth, or out of the mouth of your offspring, or out of the mouth of your children's offspring,' says the LORD, 'from this time forth and forevermore.'" Grandchildren with the word in their mouths — that is God's stated ambition for our families.

### Key verses

> "Behold, the LORD's hand is not shortened, that it cannot save, or his ear dull, that it cannot hear. But your iniquities have made a separation between you and your God, and your sins have hidden his face from you so that he does not hear." — Isaiah 59:1–2 (ESV)

> "He saw that there was no man, and wondered that there was no one to intercede; then his own arm brought him salvation, and his righteousness upheld him." — Isaiah 59:16 (ESV)

> "And a Redeemer will come to Zion, to those in Jacob who turn from transgression, declares the LORD." — Isaiah 59:20 (ESV)

> "My Spirit that is upon you, and my words that I have put in your mouth, shall not depart out of your mouth, or out of the mouth of your offspring, or out of the mouth of your children's offspring, says the LORD, from this time forth and forevermore." — Isaiah 59:21 (ESV)

### The bad news and the good news

The bad news of Isaiah 59 is that the separation is real and it is ours — iniquities have hidden God's face, truth has stumbled in the public square, and there was no man, no intercessor, anywhere in the human race qualified to fix it. The good news is that God fixed it Himself: His own arm brought salvation, His Redeemer has come to Zion for everyone who turns from transgression, and His covenant puts His Spirit and His words in our mouths and our grandchildren's mouths forever. The arm was never short. It was reaching.

**A note from the Reformers.** Luther called Isaiah 59:16 the seed of the whole gospel: when God wondered that there was no intercessor, He did not lower the standard or recruit a better man — He stretched out His own arm. Christ is that arm. Spurgeon added that the armor of Ephesians 6 fits Christians because it first fit Christ — we fight in equipment He already won in.

**Reflection question:** Where have you been blaming God's short arm or dull ear for a separation your own sin built? What would turning — the one qualification the Redeemer asks — look like today?

---

## Isaiah 60 — Arise, Shine: The Glory of Zion and the Nations Streaming In

### Jesus in this chapter

Jesus is the light of this chapter — "Arise, shine, for your light has come, and the glory of the LORD has risen upon you" — and He names Himself with its words: "I am the light of the world" (John 8:12). The darkness covers the earth and thick darkness the peoples, "but the LORD will arise upon you, and his glory will be seen upon you. And nations shall come to your light, and kings to the brightness of your rising." That is the magi on the road, the Greeks seeking Jesus, and the whole mission of the church in one picture. The chapter's end belongs to Him completely: "Your sun shall no more go down... for the LORD will be your everlasting light" — the promise Revelation 21–22 builds the new Jerusalem from, where "the city has no need of sun or moon to shine on it, for the glory of God gives it light, and its lamp is the Lamb." Jesus is Zion's everlasting light, and the violence-free, sorrow-finished city of Isaiah 60 is His hometown, still under construction and already inhabited by everyone in Him.

### Commentary

**Arise, shine (60:1–3).** After chapter 59's confession, the command is resurrection language: "Arise, shine, for your light has come." Zion is not told to generate light but to get up into the light that has arrived — the glory of the LORD risen upon her like a sunrise she did not schedule. The contrast is total: "For behold, darkness shall cover the earth, and thick darkness the peoples; but the LORD will arise upon you, and his glory will be seen upon you." Thick darkness on the peoples; glory visible on God's people — not glory they produced, but glory they reflect because He has risen on them. "And nations shall come to your light, and kings to the brightness of your rising." The church's evangelism in Isaiah is mostly this: shine, and the nations navigate by it.

**Sons from afar, wealth of nations (60:4–14).** The homecoming is described in waves: sons coming from afar, daughters carried on the hip; the abundance of the sea turned to Zion; camels of Midian and Ephah covering the land, all from Sheba, bringing gold and frankincense — the caravan Matthew saw arrive at a house in Bethlehem — "and they shall bring good news, the praises of the LORD." Ships of Tarshish bring sons from far away, "their silver and their gold with them, for the name of the LORD your God." Foreigners build Zion's walls; their kings minister to her; "the nation and kingdom that will not serve you shall perish." The trees of Lebanon — cypress, plane, pine — come to beautify the sanctuary, and the promise lands on a sentence every returning exile needed: "The sons of those who afflicted you shall come bending low to you, and all who despised you shall bow down at your feet; they shall call you the City of the LORD, the Zion of the Holy One of Israel." And God's own summary of the exchange: "Whereas you have been forsaken and hated, with no one passing through, I will make you majestic forever, a joy from age to age." Forsaken and hated to majestic forever — the whole book's arc in one verse.

**Violence no more heard (60:15–22).** "You shall suck the milk of nations" — nourished by the world that starved her — "and know that I, the LORD, am your Savior and your Redeemer, the Mighty One of Jacob." Then the upgrades: bronze replaced by gold, iron by silver, wood by bronze, stones by iron — the city's very materials glorified — "I will make your overseers peace and your taskmasters righteousness." Government itself converted: Peace and Righteousness hold the offices. "Violence shall no more be heard in your land, devastation or destruction within your borders; you shall call your walls Salvation, and your gates Praise." Walls named Salvation, gates named Praise — the architecture preaches. "Your sun shall no more go down, nor your moon withdraw itself; for the LORD will be your everlasting light, and your days of mourning shall be ended. Your people shall all be righteous; they shall possess the land forever, the branch of my planting, the work of my hands, that I might be glorified." *All* righteous — not by their record but by His planting. And the multiplication promise for the small: "The least one shall become a clan, and the smallest one a mighty nation; I am the LORD; in its time I will hasten it." In its time — and *I will hasten it*. God's timing and God's speed in the same sentence: He is never late, and when the time comes, He is suddenly fast.

### Key verses

> "Arise, shine, for your light has come, and the glory of the LORD has risen upon you. For behold, darkness shall cover the earth, and thick darkness the peoples; but the LORD will arise upon you, and his glory will be seen upon you. And nations shall come to your light, and kings to the brightness of your rising." — Isaiah 60:1–3 (ESV)

> "Violence shall no more be heard in your land, devastation or destruction within your borders; you shall call your walls Salvation, and your gates Praise." — Isaiah 60:18 (ESV)

> "Your sun shall no more go down, nor your moon withdraw itself; for the LORD will be your everlasting light, and your days of mourning shall be ended." — Isaiah 60:20 (ESV)

> "The least one shall become a clan, and the smallest one a mighty nation; I am the LORD; in its time I will hasten it." — Isaiah 60:22 (ESV)

### The bad news and the good news

The bad news of Isaiah 60 is that thick darkness covers the peoples — and that Zion left to herself is forsaken, hated, and passed by, with mourning that does not end. The good news is that her light has come: the LORD has risen upon her, nations stream to the brightness, violence is evicted, mourning is ended, the least become clans, and the everlasting light is a Person who never sets. In Christ, the sunrise has already happened. Arise and shine is not a pep talk; it is a weather report.

**A note from the Reformers.** Calvin read Isaiah 60 as the church's charter for every dark age: her light is not her own, her walls are Salvation, and her growth is God's hastening — which frees her from both despair at her smallness and pride at her size. The least one becomes a clan by promise, not by program.

**Reflection question:** Where are you still sitting in the dark as if the light had not come? And who in your life is navigating toward whatever light they can see in you — are you shining, or covering it?

---

## Isaiah 61 — The Spirit of the Lord Is Upon Me: The Year of the LORD's Favor

### Jesus in this chapter

This is the chapter Jesus chose for His first sermon. In the synagogue at Nazareth, He stood, unrolled the scroll of Isaiah, and read: "The Spirit of the Lord is upon me, because he has anointed me to proclaim good news to the poor. He has sent me to proclaim liberty to the captives and recovering of sight to the blind, to set at liberty those who are oppressed, to proclaim the year of the LORD's favor." Then He rolled up the scroll, sat down, and said, "Today this Scripture has been fulfilled in your hearing" (Luke 4:18–21). Notice where He stopped reading: mid-sentence, before "and the day of vengeance of our God" — because His first coming was the year of favor; the day of vengeance waits for His return. The Anointed One of Isaiah 61 is Jesus, describing His own ministry in His own hometown, and the ministry He describes — poor, captives, blind, oppressed — is the exact population The Hope Connection exists to serve. This chapter is not just about Jesus. Read rightly, it is His mission statement, and by His Spirit it becomes ours.

### Commentary

**The Anointed's commission (61:1–3).** "The Spirit of the Lord GOD is upon me, because the LORD has anointed me to bring good news to the poor; he has sent me to bind up the brokenhearted, to proclaim liberty to the captives, and the opening of the prison to those who are bound; to proclaim the year of the LORD's favor, and the day of vengeance of our God; to comfort all who mourn." Every verb is a ministry: bring, bind up, proclaim, open, comfort. The brokenhearted are not scolded but *bound up* — the Hebrew is the word for bandaging a wound. The year of the LORD's favor is Jubilee language (Leviticus 25) — the year debts were cancelled, slaves freed, and land returned — now announced as the character of an entire age, the age of the Anointed. And the exchange for mourners in Zion is the chapter's famous triple trade: "to grant to those who mourn in Zion — to give them a beautiful headdress instead of ashes, the oil of gladness instead of mourning, the garment of praise instead of a faint spirit." Ashes for a headdress, mourning for oil, faintness for praise — God's exchange counter, where nothing brought is refused and everything given is better. The result: "that they may be called oaks of righteousness, the planting of the LORD, that he may be glorified." Mourners become oaks — slow, deep, storm-proof — not by their effort but by His planting.

**Ruins rebuilt, strangers serving (61:4–7).** "They shall build up the ancient ruins; they shall raise up the former devastations; they shall repair the ruined cities, the devastations of many generations." The comforted become the rebuilders — the sequence of every true recovery: ashes exchanged, then ruins rebuilt, by the same people who once sat in them. Strangers shepherd their flocks; foreigners farm their land; "but you shall be called the priests of the LORD; they shall speak of you as the ministers of our God." A nation of priests — the calling Exodus 19 announced and 1 Peter 2:9 hands to the church. And the shame-arithmetic is reversed: "Instead of your shame there shall be a double portion; instead of dishonor they shall rejoice in their lot; therefore in their land they shall possess a double portion; they shall have everlasting joy." Double for shame — the same mathematics as Isaiah 40:2, grace paying out at twice the rate of disgrace.

**The robe of righteousness (61:8–11).** "For I the LORD love justice; I hate robbery and wrong" — God's character guarantees the chapter. Then the Anointed (or Zion in His voice) sings the response: "I will greatly rejoice in the LORD; my soul shall exult in my God, for he has clothed me with the garments of salvation; he has covered me with the robe of righteousness, as a bridegroom decks himself like a priest with a beautiful headdress, and as a bride adorns herself with her jewels." Clothing again — salvation as a garment put *on* us, righteousness as a robe we are *covered* with, not a suit we sewed. The Hebrew verb for covered is the one used for a priest being robed for office. And the growth is as certain as agriculture: "For as the earth brings forth its sprouts, and as a garden causes what is sown in it to sprout up, so the Lord GOD will cause righteousness and praise to sprout up before all the nations." God farms righteousness the way gardens grow — surely, seasonally, irresistibly.

### Key verses

> "The Spirit of the Lord GOD is upon me, because the LORD has anointed me to bring good news to the poor; he has sent me to bind up the brokenhearted, to proclaim liberty to the captives, and the opening of the prison to those who are bound; to proclaim the year of the LORD's favor." — Isaiah 61:1–2 (ESV)

> "To grant to those who mourn in Zion — to give them a beautiful headdress instead of ashes, the oil of gladness instead of mourning, the garment of praise instead of a faint spirit; that they may be called oaks of righteousness, the planting of the LORD, that he may be glorified." — Isaiah 61:3 (ESV)

> "I will greatly rejoice in the LORD; my soul shall exult in my God, for he has clothed me with the garments of salvation; he has covered me with the robe of righteousness, as a bridegroom decks himself like a priest with a beautiful headdress, and as a bride adorns herself with her jewels." — Isaiah 61:10 (ESV)

### The bad news and the good news

The bad news of Isaiah 61 is that we come to this chapter as the poor, the brokenhearted, the captives, the bound, and the mourning — covered in ashes with faint spirits — and the day of vengeance of our God is real and still on the calendar.
