The good news is that the Anointed has come in the year of favor: He binds the brokenhearted, opens the prison, trades ashes for a headdress and mourning for oil, clothes us in a robe of righteousness we did not sew, and plants us as oaks. Today — Jesus' own word in Nazareth — this Scripture is fulfilled in the hearing of everyone who will listen.

**A note from the Reformers.** Spurgeon preached Isaiah 61:3's exchange — beauty for ashes — as the whole Christian life in a single transaction, and he told mourners that God never takes the ashes without giving the headdress in the same motion. The exchange counter of grace, he said, has never once been closed, and no mourner has ever been turned away from it empty-handed.

**Reflection question:** Which item do you most need to bring to the exchange today — ashes, mourning, or a faint spirit? And having received from the Anointed, whose ruins are you now called to help rebuild?

---

## Isaiah 62 — For Zion's Sake I Will Not Keep Silent: The New Name and the Wedding

### Jesus in this chapter

Jesus is the Bridegroom of this chapter — the coming One for whom the watchmen are posted and the gates kept open. Zion's new name is "Hephzibah" — "My delight is in her" — and her land "Beulah" — "Married" — "for the LORD delights in you, and your land shall be married. For as a young man marries a young woman, so shall your sons marry you, and as the bridegroom rejoices over the bride, so shall your God rejoice over you." That rejoicing God is Jesus at the wedding feast of the Lamb (Revelation 19:7–9), and His delight in His people is the engine of the whole chapter. He is also the One whose coming the highway is built for: "Behold, your salvation comes; behold, his reward is with him, and his recompense before him" — the words of Isaiah 40:10 again, and of Revelation 22:12 on Jesus' own lips.

### Commentary

**The intercessor who will not rest (62:1–7).** God Himself speaks the chapter's first vow: "For Zion's sake I will not keep silent, and for Jerusalem's sake I will not be quiet, until her righteousness goes forth as brightness, and her salvation as a burning torch." The nations will see her righteousness and kings her glory, "and you shall be called by a new name that the mouth of the LORD will give. You shall be a crown of beauty in the hand of the LORD, and a royal diadem in the hand of your God." A crown held in God's hand — Zion is not merely protected; she is displayed, the way a king holds up his treasure. Then the watchmen are posted, and their job description is one of the great texts on prayer in the Bible: "You who put the LORD in remembrance, take no rest, and give him no rest until he establishes Jerusalem and makes it a praise in the earth." Give Him no rest — prayer as holy persistence, reminding God of His own promises until He performs them. Jesus taught the same shameless persistence in the parable of the friend at midnight (Luke 11) and the widow before the judge (Luke 18). The watchmen of Isaiah 62 are those parables in uniform.

**No more given to your enemies (62:8–9).** God swears by His right hand and His mighty arm: the grain will no longer feed Zion's enemies, nor foreigners drink her wine. "But those who garner it shall eat it and praise the LORD, and those who gather it shall drink it in the courts of my sanctuary." Labor enjoyed by the laborers, harvest celebrated in the sanctuary — the curse of futility (Deuteronomy 28) reversed at the table. What God's people sow in tears in this age, they eat in praise in His courts.

**Prepare the way — again (62:10–12).** The book's highway imagery returns for the homecoming: "Go through, go through the gates; prepare the way for the people; build up, build up the highway; clear it of stones; lift up a signal over the peoples." Stones cleared, signal raised — the road home is a community project. "Behold, the LORD has proclaimed to the end of the earth: Say to the daughter of Zion, 'Behold, your salvation comes; behold, his reward is with him, and his recompense before him.' And they shall be called The Holy People, The Redeemed of the LORD; and you shall be called Sought Out, A City Not Forsaken." Sought Out — the final name answers chapter 49's fear ("The LORD has forsaken me") and chapter 54's desertion with a permanent title. The city that thought she was forsaken is renamed by heaven: Sought Out, Not Forsaken. Names in Isaiah are destinies, and this is the last one Zion gets. It is a good one to end on.

### Key verses

> "For Zion's sake I will not keep silent, and for Jerusalem's sake I will not be quiet, until her righteousness goes forth as brightness, and her salvation as a burning torch." — Isaiah 62:1 (ESV)

> "You shall no more be termed Forsaken, and your land shall no more be termed Desolate, but you shall be called My Delight Is in Her, and your land Married; for the LORD delights in you, and your land shall be married." — Isaiah 62:4 (ESV)

> "I have set watchmen on your walls, O Jerusalem; they will never be silent day or night. You who put the LORD in remembrance, take no rest, and give him no rest until he establishes Jerusalem and makes it a praise in the earth." — Isaiah 62:6–7 (ESV)

> "And they shall be called The Holy People, The Redeemed of the LORD; and you shall be called Sought Out, A City Not Forsaken." — Isaiah 62:12 (ESV)

### The bad news and the good news

The bad news of Isaiah 62 is that Zion's old names were earned — Forsaken and Desolate described a real history of sin and exile — and that her grain fed her enemies while her watchmen's work went undone. The good news is that God has renamed her by His own mouth: My Delight Is in Her, Married, Sought Out, A City Not Forsaken — and He has posted watchmen who give Him no rest until the renaming is visible to all nations. In Christ, the Bridegroom rejoices over His bride, and no one He has sought out gets re-forsaken.

**A note from the Reformers.** Calvin saw in the watchmen who "give him no rest" the charter of the praying church: God delights to be reminded of His promises, not because He forgets them, but because He has appointed prayer as the means by which His people share in His purposes. Importunity is not unbelief; it is obedience to this chapter.

**Reflection question:** What old name — Forsaken, Desolate, or one of your own — are you still answering to, after God has renamed you? And what promise of His are you being called to "give Him no rest" about?

---

## Isaiah 63 — The Winepress and the Prayer: Who Is This Who Comes from Edom?

### Jesus in this chapter

Jesus is the figure in the crimson garments: "Who is this who comes from Edom, in crimsoned garments from Bozrah, he who is splendid in his apparel, marching in the greatness of his strength? 'It is I, speaking in righteousness, mighty to save.'" Revelation 19 dresses the returning Christ in this chapter's wardrobe — a robe dipped in blood, treading "the winepress of the fury of the wrath of God the Almighty." He trod the winepress alone: "I have trodden the winepress alone, and from the peoples no one was with me." Alone is the operative word — in judgment as in atonement, the work is His without assistance. And the chapter's second half is a prayer that remembers the exodus and grieves over a hard-hearted people, ending with the cry, "We have become like those over whom you have never ruled" — the prayer Jesus' own coming answers, when the heavens are finally rent open (as chapter 64 begs) and the Mighty-to-Save arrives in person.

### Commentary

**The winepress trodden alone (63:1–6).** The watchman's question opens the scene: who is this, coming from Edom — from the direction of chapter 34's judgment — with garments stained crimson? The answer is a self-introduction: "It is I, speaking in righteousness, mighty to save." Mighty to save — the whole gospel in three words, spoken by a figure whose clothes are soaked from the winepress of wrath. "I have trodden the winepress alone, and from the peoples no one was with me; I trod them in my anger and trampled them in my wrath; their lifeblood spattered on my garments." The loneliness is emphasized twice: no one was with Him. In the day of vengeance, as in the night of the cross, the Savior works unassisted — no co-redeemers, no helpers, no committee. "For the day of vengeance was in my heart, and my year of redemption had come. I looked, but there was no one to help; I was appalled, but there was no one to uphold; so my own arm brought me salvation, and my wrath upheld me." Isaiah 59:16 again — His own arm. The same arm that saves His people executes the vengeance that protects them; the two are one work, and both are His alone.

**Remembering the days of old (63:7–14).** Then the prophet turns to prayer, and the prayer begins with memory: "I will recount the steadfast love of the LORD, the praises of the LORD, according to all that the LORD has granted us." *Hesed* recounted — the discipline of listing God's covenant loves before listing our complaints. He remembers the exodus as family history: "For he said, 'Surely they are my people, children who will not deal falsely.' And he became their Savior. In all their affliction he was afflicted, and the angel of his presence saved them. In his love and in his pity he redeemed them; he lifted them up and carried them all the days of old." *In all their affliction he was afflicted* — God's sympathy is not metaphor; He suffers with His suffering people, a truth that reaches its depth when God in the flesh weeps at a grave and bleeds on a cross. "But they rebelled and grieved his Holy Spirit" — the phrase Paul borrows in Ephesians 4:30 — "therefore he turned to be their enemy, and himself fought against them." Then the remembering continues anyway: the days of Moses, the Shepherd of the flock, the Spirit put in their midst, the sea divided, the people led "by the right hand of Moses with his glorious arm" — to make for Himself an everlasting name. Memory is the prayer's fuel: what God did at the sea is the argument for what He can do in the exile.

**You are our Father (63:15–19).** The prayer ends by pleading relationship against appearances: "Look down from heaven and see, from your holy and beautiful habitation. Where are your zeal and your might?" The plea is not denial but appeal — God's own zeal, invoked against God's own restraint. "But you are our Father, though Abraham does not know us, and Israel does not acknowledge us; you, O LORD, are our Father, our Redeemer from of old is your name." When even the patriarchs would not recognize the degenerate descendants, God is claimed as Father and Redeemer anyway — the relationship rests on His name, not on their resemblance to their ancestors. And the hardest question in the book is prayed honestly: "O LORD, why do you make us wander from your ways and harden our heart, so that we fear you not? Return for the sake of your servants." The prayer does not solve the mystery of hardening; it does something better — it throws the mystery back on God's mercy and begs Him to return. That is where chapter 64 begins: still praying, still pleading, with the heavens overhead waiting to be torn.

### Key verses

> "Who is this who comes from Edom, in crimsoned garments from Bozrah, he who is splendid in his apparel, marching in the greatness of his strength? 'It is I, speaking in righteousness, mighty to save.'" — Isaiah 63:1 (ESV)

> "I looked, but there was no one to help; I was appalled, but there was no one to uphold; so my own arm brought me salvation, and my wrath upheld me." — Isaiah 63:5 (ESV)

> "In all their affliction he was afflicted, and the angel of his presence saved them. In his love and in his pity he redeemed them; he lifted them up and carried them all the days of old." — Isaiah 63:9 (ESV)

> "But you are our Father, though Abraham does not know us, and Israel does not acknowledge us; you, O LORD, are our Father, our Redeemer from of old is your name." — Isaiah 63:16 (ESV)

### The bad news and the good news

The bad news of Isaiah 63 is that God's people can rebel until they grieve His Spirit and find Him fighting against them — and that when the day of vengeance comes, no human helper stands anywhere in sight. The good news is that the Savior works alone and needs no help: mighty to save, His own arm brings salvation, He is afflicted in all our affliction, and He answers to the name Father and Redeemer from of old even when Abraham would not know us. The winepress He trod alone means the salvation He gives is complete alone too.

**A note from the Reformers.** Calvin dwelt on "in all their affliction he was afflicted" as one of the Old Testament's warmest windows into God's heart: the Lord does not redeem from a distance but enters the affliction of His people — a sympathy the incarnation would make literal, when God was afflicted in our flesh and still is touched with the feeling of our infirmities (Hebrews 4:15).

**Reflection question:** When you pray, do you recount the steadfast love first — the exodus list — or start with the complaint? What is on your "days of old" list, and what would remembering it do to your present prayer?

---

## Isaiah 64 — Oh That You Would Rend the Heavens: The Potter and the Clay

### Jesus in this chapter

Jesus is the answer to this chapter's prayer — the heaven-rending God who came down. "Oh that you would rend the heavens and come down, that the mountains might quake at your presence." At Jesus' baptism, Mark tells us He saw "the heavens being torn open" (Mark 1:10) — the Greek verb *schizō* is Isaiah 64's prayer answered in a single word. The mountains did not quake that day; something better happened: the Spirit descended like a dove and the Father spoke. Jesus is also the reason the chapter's confession can end in hope: "But now, O LORD, you are our Father; we are the clay, and you are our potter; we are all the work of your hand." The Potter did not discard the marred clay; in Christ He remade it. And the chapter's famous line — "From of old no one has heard or perceived by the ear, no eye has seen a God besides you, who acts for those who wait for him" — is quoted by Paul of the gospel itself (1 Corinthians 2:9): the things God prepared for those who love Him turned out to be a crucified and risen Christ, beyond anything eye had seen.

### Commentary

**Rend the heavens (64:1–4).** The prayer of chapter 63 continues and intensifies into one of the boldest requests in the Bible: tear the sky and come down. "As when fire kindles brushwood and the fire causes water to boil — to make your name known to your adversaries, and that the nations might tremble at your presence!" Isaiah is asking for a theophany like Sinai — the God who "came down on Mount Sinai" and did "awesome things that we did not look for" — because the situation, a ruined sanctuary and a hard-hearted people, is beyond human repair. Some situations do not need improvement; they need intervention. The confession under the request is honest: God's past descents were always more than His people expected — "awesome things that we did not look for" — and His specialty is acting "for those who wait for him." Waiting again: the book's refrain. The God who rends heavens rewards waiters, not fixers.

**All our righteous deeds are a polluted garment (64:5–7).** Then the confession descends to the root: "We have all become like one who is unclean, and all our righteous deeds are like a polluted garment. We all fade like a leaf, and our iniquities, like the wind, take us away." Notice what is confessed: not just sins, but *righteous deeds* — the best things, the religious things, are polluted garments in the presence of a holy God. That is the deepest cut in the chapter and the doorway of the gospel: when even our righteousness is rags, we stop bringing our record and start pleading God's relationship. "There is no one who calls upon your name, who rouses himself to take hold of you; for you have hidden your face from us, and have made us melt in the hand of our iniquities." Hidden face, melted people — and yet the prayer continues. Prayer that continues after that confession is the purest kind: it has nothing left to stand on but God Himself.

**We are the clay (64:8–12).** And God Himself is exactly where it lands: "But now, O LORD, you are our Father; we are the clay, and you are our potter; we are all the work of your hand. Be not so terribly angry, O LORD, and remember not iniquity forever. Behold, please look, we are all your people." Clay does not argue with the potter (Isaiah 45:9); clay pleads with him — and the plea is *we are the work of your hand*, Your craftsmanship, Your people. The chapter ends with the devastation laid out as the final argument: "Your holy cities have become a wilderness; Zion has become a wilderness, Jerusalem a desolation. Our holy and beautiful house, where our fathers praised you, has been burned by fire, and all our pleasant places have become ruins. Will you restrain yourself at these things, O LORD? Will you keep silent, and afflict us so terribly?" The prayer ends without an answer recorded — a cliffhanger God intends, because the answer is not a sentence but a coming: the rest of the book, and then Bethlehem. Some prayers God answers by arriving.

### Key verses

> "Oh that you would rend the heavens and come down, that the mountains might quake at your presence — as when fire kindles brushwood and the fire causes water to boil — to make your name known to your adversaries, and that the nations might tremble at your presence!" — Isaiah 64:1–2 (ESV)

> "From of old no one has heard or perceived by the ear, no eye has seen a God besides you, who acts for those who wait for him." — Isaiah 64:4 (ESV)

> "We have all become like one who is unclean, and all our righteous deeds are like a polluted garment. We all fade like a leaf, and our iniquities, like the wind, take us away." — Isaiah 64:6 (ESV)

> "But now, O LORD, you are our Father; we are the clay, and you are our potter; we are all the work of your hand." — Isaiah 64:8 (ESV)

### The bad news and the good news

The bad news of Isaiah 64 is that all our righteous deeds — not just our sins — are a polluted garment before a holy God; we fade like leaves and our iniquities carry us like wind, and no one can rouse himself to fix it. The good news is that the Potter has not thrown the clay away: He is our Father, we are the work of His hand, and the prayer "rend the heavens and come down" has been answered — at a Jordan river, on a cross, and out of a tomb. Eye has not seen the half of what the waiting God has prepared.

**A note from the Reformers.** Luther called Isaiah 64:6 the end of all self-righteousness and therefore the beginning of all comfort: the man who has confessed that his best deeds are rags has finally stopped bringing God a résumé and started bringing Him a need — and need is the one thing God never turns away.

**Reflection question:** Have you ever confessed your righteous deeds, not just your sins? What would it mean to come to God as clay — no argument, no résumé — and simply say, "We are all the work of your hand"?

---

## Isaiah 65 — I Was Ready to Be Found: New Heavens and a New Earth

### Jesus in this chapter

Jesus is the God who stands with outstretched hands in this chapter: "I spread out my hands all the day to a rebellious people, who walk in a way that is not good, following their own devices." Paul quotes those hands in Romans 10:21 over Israel's unbelief — and the church has always seen in them a preview of hands spread out on a cross, held open all day to people walking the other way. Jesus is also the Creator of the chapter's climax: "For behold, I create new heavens and a new earth, and the former things shall not be remembered or come into mind." The One through whom all things were made (John 1:3) is the One through whom all things are made new (Revelation 21:5). And He is the seed of Jacob and the chosen of Judah through whom the promise runs — the Offspring in whom God's servants inherit the mountains and dwell in the land He is remaking.

### Commentary

**Found by those who did not seek (65:1–7).** The chapter opens with God's astonishing availability: "I was ready to be sought by those who did not ask for me; I was ready to be found by those who did not seek me. I said, 'Here I am, here I am,' to a nation that was not called by my name." Paul applies this to the Gentiles in Romans 10:20 — God found by people who were not even looking, while His own chosen nation walked past His outstretched hands. The indictment of that nation is detailed and strange: sacrificing in gardens, burning incense on bricks, sitting in tombs, eating swine's flesh, and saying to others, "Keep to yourself, do not come near me, for I am too holy for you." Holiness as a superiority complex — Isaiah saves some of his sharpest contempt for it. "These are a smoke in my nostrils, a fire that burns all the day." God's patience has a limit, and the limit is stated: "Behold, it is written before me: 'I will not keep silent, but I will repay.'" The cluster image softens it for the remnant: as a cluster of grapes with some good juice is spared — "Do not destroy it, for there is a blessing in it" — so God spares His servants for the blessing's sake in the bunch. Judgment in Isaiah is never indiscriminate; God can find the blessing in the cluster.

**Before they call, I will answer (65:8–16).** The remnant's inheritance is described — mountains inherited, Sharon a pasture, the valley of Achor (Trouble's valley, from Joshua 7) a resting place "for my people who have sought me." Hosea heard the same promise: the valley of Achor as a door of hope (Hosea 2:15). But those who forsake the LORD and set a table for Fortune and Destiny — gods named Gad and Meni, luck and fate — are numbered to the sword. The contrast lands in one of the chapter's most quoted lines: "My servants shall eat, but you shall be hungry; behold, my servants shall drink, but you shall be thirsty; behold, my servants shall rejoice, but you shall be put to shame." And the God of truth — literally, "the God of Amen" (*Elohim Amen*) — is sworn by, because He makes the former troubles forgotten and hidden from His eyes. Jesus is the Amen (Revelation 3:14); every promise of God finds its Yes in Him (2 Corinthians 1:20). The chapter's God is named Amen because His word does not wobble.

**New heavens and a new earth (65:17–25).** Then the horizon breaks open: "For behold, I create new heavens and a new earth, and the former things shall not be remembered or come into mind. But be glad and rejoice forever in that which I create; for behold, I create Jerusalem to be a joy, and her people to be a gladness. I will rejoice in Jerusalem and be glad in my people; no more shall be heard in it the sound of weeping and the cry of distress." God rejoicing over His people, weeping abolished — Revelation 21:1–4 is this paragraph fulfilled and expanded. The details are gloriously earthy: lifespans so long that a hundred-year-old death is a youth's tragedy; houses built and inhabited by the builders; vineyards eaten by the planters — futility's curse (another man's house, another man's harvest) reversed. "Before they call I will answer; while they are yet speaking I will hear" — prayer outrun by its Answerer. And the peaceable kingdom of chapter 11 returns as the last word: "The wolf and the lamb shall graze together; the lion shall eat straw like the ox... They shall not hurt or destroy in all my holy mountain, says the LORD." Isaiah's two great visions of the end — chapters 11 and 65 — rhyme on purpose: the book's hope is a healed world, not an escaped one.

### Key verses

> "I was ready to be sought by those who did not ask for me; I was ready to be found by those who did not seek me. I said, 'Here I am, here I am,' to a nation that was not called by my name. I spread out my hands all the day to a rebellious people, who walk in a way that is not good, following their own devices." — Isaiah 65:1–2 (ESV)

> "Behold, my servants shall eat, but you shall be hungry; behold, my servants shall drink, but you shall be thirsty; behold, my servants shall rejoice, but you shall be put to shame; behold, my servants shall sing for gladness of heart." — Isaiah 65:13–14 (ESV)

> "For behold, I create new heavens and a new earth, and the former things shall not be remembered or come into mind." — Isaiah 65:17 (ESV)

> "Before they call I will answer; while they are yet speaking I will hear." — Isaiah 65:24 (ESV)

### The bad news and the good news

The bad news of Isaiah 65 is that a man can walk past God's outstretched hands all day — setting tables for luck and fate, calling himself too holy for others — and be numbered to the sword while the hands stay open. The good news is that God is found by people who were not even seeking Him, answers before we call, spares the cluster for the blessing in it, and is creating new heavens and a new earth where weeping is unheard and the wolf grazes with the lamb. The hands are still spread out. The Amen still says yes.

**A note from the Reformers.** Spurgeon preached "Here I am, here I am" as God's eagerness put into words — the Lord repeating Himself like a father calling a lost child in the woods — and he marveled that the verse is addressed to people who were not seeking: grace does not wait to be found by the diligent; it finds the indifferent and the hostile alike.

**Reflection question:** Were you seeking God when He found you, or did He find you mid-stride in the wrong direction? And whose table are you still setting — Fortune's and Destiny's, or the LORD's?

---

## Isaiah 66 — Heaven Is My Throne: The Humble, the Trembling, and the Glory Among the Nations

### Jesus in this chapter

The book ends where Jesus stands at the center. He is the LORD whose throne is heaven and whose footstool is the earth — and who yet looks to "the one who is humble and contrite in spirit and trembles at my word." He is the One who comes with fire and chariots like the whirlwind, "to render his anger in fury" (66:15) — the returning Judge of 2 Thessalonians 1, where Paul borrows this chapter's imagery for Jesus' revelation from heaven. He is the sender of survivors to the nations — "I will send survivors to the nations... and they shall declare my glory among the nations" — the mission Jesus commissions in Matthew 28 and empowers in Acts 1:8, gathering brothers from all nations as offerings to Jerusalem. And He is the permanence behind the book's last promise: "For as the new heavens and the new earth that I make shall remain before me... so shall your offspring and your name remain." The Jesus who was pierced in chapter 53 is the Jesus whose kingdom, and whose people, remain forever in chapter 66. The book that began with a courtroom ends with a cosmos remade and a family preserved. He is the thread, the center, and the end.

### Commentary

**The God who cannot be housed (66:1–4).** "Thus says the LORD: 'Heaven is my throne, and the earth is my footstool; what is the house that you would build for me, and what is the place of my rest? All these things my hand has made.'" Stephen quotes this before the Sanhedrin (Acts 7:49–50) to make the book's final theological point: God cannot be contained, managed, or impressed by architecture. And the look He gives is not to the temple but to a person: "But this is the one to whom I will look: he who is humble and contrite in spirit and trembles at my word." Three qualifications for God's attention, and none of them is talent, wealth, or record: humility, contrition, trembling at the Word. Then the shocking exposure of empty religion at the book's end — the man who slaughters an ox as if he killed a man, who offers a lamb as if he broke a dog's neck: sacrifices performed by people who "have chosen their own ways, and their soul delights in their abominations" are counted by God as the sins they resemble. "I also will choose harsh treatment for them... because when I called, no one answered, when I spoke, they did not listen." The book that began with God's children not knowing Him ends with the same tragedy offered one final chance to be avoided.

**The word that vindicates and the mother who comforts (66:5–14).** To the tremblers, though, the word is pure comfort: "Hear the word of the LORD, you who tremble at his word: 'Your brothers who hate you and cast you out for my name's sake have said, "Let the LORD be glorified, that we may see your joy"; but it is they who shall be put to shame.'" The cast-out tremblers will be vindicated, and the mockers' pious slogan will be turned back on them. Then Zion gives birth before her labor — "Before she was in labor she gave birth; before her pain came upon her she delivered a son" — a nation born in a day, the miraculous speed of God's new-creation work. And the comfort image is maternal, completing chapter 49's nursing mother: "As one whom his mother comforts, so I will comfort you; you shall be comforted in Jerusalem." Hearts rejoice, bones flourish like grass, and the hand of the LORD is known to His servants — and His indignation to His enemies. Both. Always both, to the last page.

**Fire, glory, and the gathering of the nations (66:15–21).** "For behold, the LORD will come in fire, and his chariots like the whirlwind, to render his anger in fury, and his rebuke with flames of fire. For by fire will the LORD enter into judgment, and by his sword, with all flesh; and those slain by the LORD shall be many." The self-righteous processions to gardens and swine's flesh feasts end here. Then the mission: "For I know their works and their thoughts, and the time is coming to gather all nations and tongues. And they shall come and shall see my glory, and I will set a sign among them. And from them I will send survivors to the nations, to Tarshish, Pul, and Lud... to the coastlands far away, that have not heard my fame or seen my glory. And they shall declare my glory among the nations. And they shall bring all your brothers from all the nations as an offering to the LORD... to my holy mountain Jerusalem." Survivors become missionaries; the gathered become gatherers; brothers come home as offerings — carried, like the vessels of the temple, with care. And some of the gathered Gentiles God will even take "for priests and for Levites" — the foreigner of chapter 56, promoted to the priesthood by chapter 66. The book's welcome widens to its widest at the end.

**The last word: permanence and the worm (66:22–24).** "For as the new heavens and the new earth that I make shall remain before me, says the LORD, so shall your offspring and your name remain. From new moon to new moon, and from Sabbath to Sabbath, all flesh shall come to worship before me, declares the LORD." All flesh worshiping, forever — the courtroom of chapter 1 has become the congregation of eternity. And then the book's final verse, which Jesus Himself quotes three times in Mark 9 as His description of hell: "And they shall go out and look on the dead bodies of the men who have rebelled against me. For their worm shall not die, their fire shall not be quenched, and they shall be an abhorrence to all flesh." Isaiah ends with the door of choice standing open between worship and the worm — and it is fitting that he does. This prophet never forced a reader. He indicts, he comforts, he warns, he invites, and at the end he sets the two futures side by side and lets the book close. The choice it leaves us is the choice the whole book has been building: tremble at His word and be comforted forever, or choose your own way and be an abhorrence. Choose the trembling. The God of this book is worth it.

### Key verses

> "Thus says the LORD: 'Heaven is my throne, and the earth is my footstool; what is the house that you would build for me, and what is the place of my rest? All these things my hand has made, and so all these things came to be, declares the LORD. But this is the one to whom I will look: he who is humble and contrite in spirit and trembles at my word.'" — Isaiah 66:1–2 (ESV)

> "Rejoice with Jerusalem, and be glad for her, all you who love her; rejoice with her in joy, all you who mourn over her... As one whom his mother comforts, so I will comfort you; you shall be comforted in Jerusalem." — Isaiah 66:10, 13 (ESV)

> "For as the new heavens and the new earth that I make shall remain before me, says the LORD, so shall your offspring and your name remain." — Isaiah 66:22 (ESV)

> "And they shall go out and look on the dead bodies of the men who have rebelled against me. For their worm shall not die, their fire shall not be quenched, and they shall be an abhorrence to all flesh." — Isaiah 66:24 (ESV)

### The bad news and the good news

The bad news of Isaiah 66 is that God cannot be housed, managed, or impressed — religion chosen our own way is counted as the sin it imitates — and that rebellion's end is a worm that does not die and a fire that is not quenched, in Jesus' own quotation of this verse. The good news is that the God whose throne is heaven looks, with all His attention, on the humble and contrite who tremble at His word; He comforts like a mother, gathers the nations, makes priests of foreigners, and swears that our offspring and our name shall remain as long as the new heavens and the new earth. The book ends with worship forever for the trembling, and the invitation is still open on the last page.

**A note from the Reformers.** Luther said Isaiah ends the way the whole Bible ends — with God's glory among the nations and two destinies before every reader — and he urged that the last verse be read with the same seriousness as the first comfort: the church that preaches Isaiah 40's "Comfort, comfort" must also preach Isaiah 66's worm, because both came from the same mouth, and both are love's honesty about what is at stake.

**Reflection question:** Of the three qualifications for God's look — humble, contrite in spirit, trembling at His word — which is strongest in you, and which is weakest? What would trembling at His word look like in your daily life this week?

---

## Closing — Isaiah in One Sentence

Sixty-six chapters, and they can be carried in a single sentence:

**The Holy One of Israel, who saw our sin in full and judged it without flinching, sent His Servant — promised as a child, anointed as a King, and pierced as a Lamb — to bear the iniquity of us all, so that everyone who turns to Him, from the ends of the earth, is comforted, cleansed, and carried home to a new creation that will never end.**

In the "Jesus in Every Book of the Bible" walkthrough, this is Isaiah's testimony: **Jesus in Isaiah is the suffering Servant and the coming King — the Lamb who was pierced for our transgressions and the Lord whose glory fills the whole earth.** No other Old Testament book holds the two together so tightly. Isaiah sees the throne high and lifted up, and he sees the face marred beyond human semblance, and he never once treats them as two different people. The King reigns by suffering. The Servant saves by reigning. If you have walked through this commentary chapter by chapter, you have heard the five watchwords of the Reformers sound on every page, because they are Isaiah's watchwords first: Scripture alone, as the prophet binds up the testimony and commands, "To the teaching and to the testimony!"; grace alone, as scarlet sins are made white as snow and the thirsty buy without money; faith alone, as the frightened are told that if they are not firm in faith they will not be firm at all; Christ alone, as the Servant bears what no one else could bear and makes many to be accounted righteous; and glory to God alone, as the seraphim's cry — "Holy, holy, holy" — echoes from chapter 6 to chapter 66, where all flesh comes to worship.

The last word belongs to the promise Isaiah could not stop repeating: your God will come. He came — in a manger, to a cross, out of a tomb — and He is coming again with fire and with comfort, to gather the nations and wipe away the tears. Until that day, the invitation of this book stands open on every page, and it is the invitation of The Hope Connection itself: come, everyone who thirsts. The water is free. The Servant is willing. The King is coming. Meet Him where you are — He has already met you in His Word.

### A Closing Prayer

Lord Jesus Christ, suffering Servant and coming King,

We thank You for Isaiah — for the prophet who saw Your glory and spoke of You, and for every chapter that has shown You to us: the Child born, the shoot from the stump, the Shepherd carrying lambs, the Servant silent before His shearers and pierced for our transgressions. We confess that all we like sheep have gone astray, every one to his own way. Thank You that the LORD laid on You the iniquity of us all — that our peace was purchased by Your chastisement and our healing by Your wounds.

Teach us to live in this book's promises. Make us firm in faith, that we may be firm at all. Keep our minds stayed on You, and keep us in perfect peace. Blot out our transgressions like a cloud, and give us back the joy of drawing water from the wells of salvation. Make us oaks of righteousness, repairers of the breach, and runners with beautiful feet to people who still sit in darkness — until the day when You come again, the ransomed return with singing, and sorrow and sighing flee away forever.

Yours is the kingdom, and the righteousness, and the comfort, and the glory — now and always.

Christ Proclaimed. Jesus is Lord.

Amen.
